Quantum Matrix-Spherical Functions as Orthogonal Polynomials
Stein Meereboer & Philip Schlösser

Abstract
. A general theory of matrix-spherical functions for dual Hopf algebras and right coideal subalgebras is developed. We establish their existence and define their orthogonality relations. When specialized to Kolb and Letzter’s quantum symmetric pair coideal subalgebras, we associate, to each classical commutative triple, a unique corresponding quantum commutative triple. This leads to families of vector-valued orthogonal polynomials, which diagonalize a commutative algebra of difference-reflection operators and are invariant under sending q → q − 1 q\to q^{-1} . Various examples of these vector-valued orthogonal polynomials are given and identified with Intermediate Macdonald polynomials.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 33D80; Secondary 33D52, 17B37

Contents

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

The starting point of this article is the realization of
zonal spherical functions on symmetric spaces
G/K
as Jacobi polynomials.
In his 1987 preprint of
[
28
]
, Ian Macdonald
tentatively asked whether an analogous description exists
in the recently emerging field of quantum groups, and proposed
a set of polynomials as candidates.
In the following decade, various authors such as Tom Koornwinder,
Masatoshi Noumi, Tetsuya Sugitani, and Mathijs Dijhuizen indeed
managed to find examples of quantum analoga of symmetric pairs
whose zonal spherical functions were indeed the Macdonald polynomials
described in
[
28
]
or Koornwinder–Macdonald polynomials
(
[
20
]
), a generalisation for root systems of type
\mathsf{BC}
.
In later publications, e.g.
[
29
]
, both types of polynomials
have been unified and are now in full generality known as Macdonald
polynomials.

During this process there existed a plethora of ideas of what kind of algebraic object the subgroup
K
would correspond to (as it became clear very
quickly that
K
cannot correspond to a quantum group),
ranging from Koornwinder’s twisted primitive element (see
[
21
]
)
to the various two-sided coideals and two-sided coideal subalgebras
that are obtained from reflection equations in the work of
Dijkhuizen, Noumi, and Sugitani (
[
33
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
32
]
).

In the late 1990s, Gail Letzter showed (
[
22
]
) that all of these approaches
are equivalent and should be formulated in the language of
coideal subalgebras.
She then went on to develop a general theory of quantum symmetric
pairs (including but not limited to
[
23
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
25
]
,
[
26
]
), culminating in the general
identification of the zonal spherical functions of most quantum
symmetric pairs (i.e. with reduced restricted root system and standard
parameters) with Macdonald’s polynomials.
More details and a survey of the literature on the remaining cases
can be found in Section
5
.

In addition to having been studied in such detail,
the coideal subalgebra approach (
“twisted quantised enveloping algebras”
) has another advantage over,
say Noumi’s and Sugitani’s
“additive”
approach (see
[
34
, §2.4]
):
it generalises naturally to matrix-spherical functions
for other representations of the coideal subalgebra that corresponds to
K
.
The central question we hope to
answer with this paper is the question whether these quantum
matrix-spherical functions can be described as vector-valued
generalisations of Macdonald polynomials.
Previous forays into this direction have been made in

[
2
]
and
[
1
]
, as well as
[
10
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
30
]
and
[
35
]
for
1-dimensional spaces of intertwiners.

In
[
45
]
, a vector-valued generalisation
of the Jacobi polynomials (so-called Intermediate Jacobi Polynomials) was defined and exhibited as
matrix-spherical functions of compact symmetric pairs (of groups)
for a few examples.
This approach was then adapted for Macdonald polynomials by Philip Schlösser (
[
40
]
)
to define Intermediate Macdonald Polynomials.

1.2.
Main results

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a reductive Lie algebra and let
\theta

be an involutive automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Let
\mathfrak{h}:=\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
be the subalgebra fixed
by
\theta
.
The pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
or of their universal
enveloping algebras is a
symmetric pair
.

With
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
the theory of quantum symmetric pairs, as developed by Letzter and Kolb
[
22
]

[
18
]
, associates a pair

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of associative unital algebras (
\mathbf{U}
is a Drinfel’d Jimbo quantum group
and
\mathbf{B}
is a right coideal subalgebra) over
k=\overline{\mathbb{C}(q)}

whose
q=1
specialisations are isomorphic to

(U(\mathfrak{g}),U(\mathfrak{k}))
.
Such a pair
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is called a
quantum symmetric pair
, and

\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
depends on parameters
(\bm{c},\bm{s})
.

1.2.1.
Main results Part
I

The main result of Part
I
is the development of a general theory of matrix spherical functions.

Write
\mathcal{A}
for the algebra of finite-dimensional
matrix coefficients of
\mathbf{U}
.
Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
and
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
be quantum symmetric pairs
corresponding to potentially different choices of parameters.
Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{B}
-module, and

W
a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
-module.
A function
f\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\Hom(W,V)
is called a

matrix spherical function
if

\forall b\in\mathbf{B},x\in\mathbf{U},b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime}:\quad f(bxb^{\prime})=bf(x)b^{\prime}.

For a simple
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
, we define the
subspace
E^{V,W}(M)
of elementary matrix spherical functions,
i.e. elements of
f\in E^{V,W}
contained in
\mathcal{A}(M)\otimes\Hom(W,V)
,
where
\mathcal{A}(M)
consists of the matrix elements of
M
.

E^{V,W}
is the direct sum of
E^{V,W}(M)
with
M
ranging over
finite-dimensional simple modules.
In particular, if
V=W=k
are the restrictions of the trivial
representation
\epsilon
of
\mathbf{U}
(the counit), we call
f\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}(M)
a

zonal spherical function
. In general, the set
E^{V,W}
of such matrix spherical functions is
a vector space and a left module over the algebra
E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}
.
In the setting of quantum symmetric pairs, this setup for zonal spherical functions is analogous to
[
25
]
.

Moreover, we construct a
k
-bilinear pairing (see Definition

2.16
)

\Xi_{V,W}:E^{V,W}\otimes E^{W,V}\to E^{\epsilon,\epsilon},

which can be combined with the Haar state
h
on
\mathcal{A}
to
give a non-degenerate bilinear pairing
h\circ\Xi_{V,W}:E^{V,W}\otimes E^{W,V}\to k
with respect to which that the

E^{V,W}(M)
(for varying
M
) are orthogonal (see Proposition
2.21
).
This is a generalization the construction of
[
30
]
to higher dimensional representations and more general pairs of Hopf algebras and coideal subalgebras.

With respect to the
E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}
-module structure of

E^{V,W}
,
\Xi_{V,W}
interacts as follows:

\Xi_{V,W}(fF,gG)=f\Xi_{V,W}(F,G)g(S(\cdot)K_{-2\rho}),

where
K_{-2\rho}
corresponds to the element
-2\rho
(sum of
negative roots) embedded into the Cartan subalgebra of

\mathfrak{g}
.
Standard quantum group theory defines commutative algebra
\mathbf{U}^{0}\subseteq\mathbf{U}

corresponding to the Cartan subalgebra.
We show that
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
is Zariski dense in
\mathbf{U}
(see Theorem
4.21
), thus showing an analogue of the Cartan decomposition
for quantum symmetric pairs. As a concequence
every matrix spherical function
f
is defined by its restriction
\Res(f)
to

\mathbf{U}^{0}
(see Proposition
4.23
), which is an element of
k[X]\otimes\Hom(W,V)
, where

X
is a lattice of weights of
\mathfrak{g}
.

In particular, there exists a matrix weight distribution

\triangledown\in k[[X]]\otimes\End(\Hom(V,W))
such
that

h(\Xi_{V,W}(F,G))=\tr_{W}\ct((-\rho)\triangleright\Res(F)\triangledown\overline{(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(G)})

(see Proposition
6.6
), where
\ct:k[[X]]\to k
, the
constant term map,
maps
\sum_{\mu\in X}a_{\mu}e^{\mu}
to
a_{0}
, and
where
(-\rho)\triangleright e^{\mu}=q^{-\langle\rho,\mu\rangle}e^{\mu}
effects a shift by
-\rho
.
In essence, the symmetric bilinear pairing
h\circ\Xi_{V,W}

thus factors through
(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\cdot)
.
This factorisation can be further refined by noting that

E^{V,W}
and
E^{W,V}
are often free modules over
E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}
(see Lemma
6.5
).

Such a pleasant module structure arises in the context of quantum commutative triples
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},V(\gamma))
, which are defined by the condition that

\forall\lambda\in X^{+}:\quad\dim(\Hom_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}(V(\gamma),L(\lambda))),\dim(\Hom_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}(L(\lambda),V(\gamma)))\leq 1.

Using specialization and the techniques from Watanabe’s theory of integrable modules
[
50
]
, we prove that the classification of these triples is independent of the quantization parameter
q
(see Theorem
3.43
). Consequently, we obtain a wide range of quantum commutative triples, as many are already known in the classical case, in particular, those classified in
[
37
]
.

For quantum commutative triples, we can leverage techniques from the
\imath
quantum group program, initiated by Bao and Wang, to derive symmetries of the corresponding vector-valued orthogonal polynomials. The universal
K
-matrix
\mathcal{K}:L(\lambda)\to L(\lambda)

[
6
]
, under certain technical conditions, intertwines the
\mathbf{B}
-action on
L(\lambda)
. In the context of quantum commutative triples, it therefore acts by scalar multiplication on the
\mathbf{B}
-type. This invariant action, at the level of the associated vector-valued orthogonal polynomials, results in a
q\to q^{-1}
symmetry (see Theorem
6.19
).

The quantum Cartan decomposition (see Theorem
4.21
) implies that every (elementary) matrix spherical
function a vector-valued polynomial that diagonalises a
commutative algebra of matrix difference operators
(the radial parts of the centre of
\mathbf{U}
, see Theorem
6.17
). The procedure to calculate the radial parts is similar in spirit to the classical case
[
9
]
.

1.2.2.
Main results Part
II

In Part
II
we consider instructive examples. The main result is the computation of the
corresponding matrix weights, and the identification the matrix
spherical functions with Intermediate Macdonald polynomials.
These were first introduced in
[
40
]
by one of the authors,
and are a parameter-dependent family of Laurent polynomials in

k[2L]
that are invariant under a parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
of the
Weyl group
W_{\Sigma}
(symmetric and non-symmetric Macdonald
polynomials are special cases for
J=I
and
J=\emptyset
).
Like the Macdonald polynomials, they diagonalise a commutative
algebra of
q
-difference operators,
and as is demonstrated in
[
40
, §6]
, there are straightforward ways
of interpreting them vector-valued polynomials.

As such, the results of Part
II
can be seen as generalizations of Letzter’s identification of zonal spherical functions with Macdonald polynomials
[
26
]
by considering a more general notion of spherical functions, and then obtaining a corresponding generalization of the Macdonald polynomials.
The contents of Part
II
is very close to
[
45
]
and
[
43
]
in spirit, where in the classical case matrix-spherical functions are identified with Intermediate Jacobi polynomials.
In particular, we consider

•

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of type
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
, corresponding to

Spin(2n+1)/Spin(2n)
with a representation
\gamma

corresponding to
s\varpi_{n-1}
and
s\varpi_{n}
, where

\varpi_{n-1},\varpi_{n}
correspond to the spin representations of
Spin(2n)

•

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of type
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
, corresponding
to
Sp(2n)/(Sp(2)\times Sp(2n-2))
with a representation

\gamma
corresponding to
s\varpi_{1}
, where
\varpi_{1}

is the standard representation of the
Sp(2)
factor.

•

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of type
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
, corresponding
to
Spin(2n)/Spin(2n-1)
with a representation
\gamma

corresponding to the spin representation of
Sp(2n-1)
.

•

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of type
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, corresponding to
SU(3)/SO(3)
, with
a representation
\gamma
corresponding to the standard representation of
SO(3)
.

•

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2}
-group,
corresponding to
(SU(3)\times SU(3))/SU(3)
, with a representation

\gamma
corresponding to the standard representation of
SL(3)
.

•

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of type
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, corresponding
to
SU(6)/Sp(6)
, with a representation
\gamma
corresponding
to the standard representation of
Sp(6)
.

For the first two cases, we obtain symmetric Askey–Wilson polynomials
(i.e. Intermediate Macdonald polynomials of rank 1, symmetric under the entire
Weyl group).
In the third case, we obtain non-symmetric Askey–Wilson polynomials
(i.e. Intermediate Macdonald polynomials of rank 1, symmetric under
the trivial group).
And in the last three cases, we obtain Intermediate Macdonald polynomials
for the root system
\mathsf{A}_{2}
that are symmetric under
a non-trivial parabolic subgroup of the Weyl group. It is worth to mention that we are able to make the identification with Intermediate Macdonald polynomials by matching the orthogonality relations using the inner product methods, as developed in Part
I
.

1.3.
Structure

This paper is divided into two parts. Part
I
consists of Section
2
-
6
, here we develop the general theory of quantum matrix-spherical functions. Part
II
consits of Section
7
and Appendix
A
-
B
, and is devoted to the explicit study of matrix spherical functions in a number of specific cases.

We begin in Section
2
in the most general setting by reviewing the definitions of a Hopf algebra and a right coideal subalgebra.
In this setting, we define the notion of a (matrix) spherical function
and show some basic properties thereof, including orthogonality relations.

In Section
3
we then specialise by introducing the
concrete Hopf algebras and coideal subalgebras that will be considered
in the rest of the paper: Drin’feld–Jimbo quantum groups and
quantum-symmetric pair coideal subalgebras.
In their representation theory, they correspond to compact symmetric pairs, which we also make concrete.
In that setting we develop some further techniques to describe the orthogonality relations between the matrix-spherical functions.

In Section
4
we utilise representation theory to prove an analogon of the
KAK decomposition for symmetric pairs of groups.
This allows us to describe spherical functions by their restrictions to
the Cartan torus.
The representation theory part involves proving a statement for all
rank-1 diagram that is true for all finite-type rank-1 diagrams but fails already for some affine diagrams.
Therefore, we restrict to Cartan data of finite type from now on.

Section
5
deals with zonal functions and the extensive
literature about them.
We collect and unify the most important results (for our purposes) from
said literature.
In particular, we are concerned with the cases for which the zonal spherical functions are known to
be Macdonald polynomials

In Section
6
we assosiate of vector-valued polynomials to matrix-spherical functions. We combine the results of Sections
2
–
5
to study the structural properties of these polynomials. In particular, with use of the quantum Cartan decomposition of Section
4
the existence a of matrix valued
q
-difference equation for the associated vector-valued polynomials is shown, and the
q\mapsto q^{-1}
invariance.

Section
7
is the first section of Part
II
and is concerned with the examples.
The inner product from Section
2
and some ad-hoc computations
are used to describe the matrix-spherical functions as Intermediate
Macdonald polynomials.
It is a conjecture of the authors that this can be done whenever the
reduced Weyl group acts transitively on the
M
-types (respectively,
the bottom elements of the well) and
the stabiliser subgroup is parabolic.
Using the examples we show that this is true for all analoga of small

K
-types (that are integrable, i.e. can be described using our formalism),
for all rank-1 cases, and for all cases with restricted root system of type

\mathsf{A}_{2}
with three
M
-types.

Acknowledgment

This work is funded by grant number
OCENW.M20.108
of the
Dutch Research Council.
The authors would like to thank Jasper Stokman for his help with
assembling the literature on zonal spherical functions.
Moreover, they would like to thank Erik Koelink and Maarten van Pruijssen
for valuable feedback and useful discussions.

Part I
Generalities on quantum matrix-spherical functions

2.
Matrix-Spherical Functions for Hopf Algebras and Coideal Subalgebras

In this section we are going to define the notions of quantum groups,
coideal subalgebras, and matrix-spherical functions in great generality, without mentioning
root systems or Satake diagrams.
This generality is assumed in order to highlight the elementary nature of the constructions and proofs, and for potential generalizations.

2.1.
General Definitions

Let
k
be an algebraicly closed field. We write ??for the sets of natural numbers (without 0), integers, rationals, and complex numbers.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Hopf algebra
over
k
is a tuple
(??,\Delta,\epsilon,S)
of

(i)

an associative unital
k
-algebra
\mathbf{U}
,

(ii)

an algebra homomorphism
\Delta:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}
(
“comultiplication”
), usually written in
Sweedler notation
as

\Delta(x)=\sum x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}=x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)},

(iii)

an algebra homomorphism
\epsilon:\mathbf{U}\to k

(
“counit”
), and

(iv)

an algebra antihomomorphism
S:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}
(
“antipode”
)

such that

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\id)\circ\Delta

\displaystyle=(\id\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}

\displaystyle(\id\otimes\epsilon)\circ\Delta

\displaystyle=(\epsilon\otimes\id)\circ\Delta=\id:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}

\displaystyle m\circ(\id\otimes S)\circ\Delta

\displaystyle=m\circ(S\otimes\id)\circ\Delta=1\cdot\epsilon:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}

(where
m:\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}
is the multiplication map).

Remark 2.2
.

The axioms can be written in Sweedler notation as

\displaystyle x_{(1)(1)}\otimes x_{(1)(2)}\otimes x_{(2)}

\displaystyle=x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)(1)}\otimes x_{(2)(2)}=x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}\otimes x_{(3)}

\displaystyle\epsilon(x_{(1)})x_{(2)}

\displaystyle=x_{(1)}\epsilon(x_{(2)})=x

\displaystyle x_{(1)}S(x_{(2)})

\displaystyle=S(x_{(1)})x_{(2)}=\epsilon(x)

for all
x\in\mathbf{U}
.

Note that it is exactly the extra structure of a Hopf algebra that
allows us to equip the category of
\mathbf{U}
-modules with the structure of
a rigid monoidal category.

Definition 2.3
.

Define the
adjoint representation

\ad:\mathbf{U}\to\End(\mathbf{U})
as
follows:

\ad(x)(v):=x_{(1)}vS(x_{(2)}).

Let
M
be a module over a Hopf algebra
\mathbf{U}
, let
v\in M,f\in M^{*}
,
then define

c_{f,v}=??:\mathbf{U}\to k,\qquad x\mapsto f(xv).

This is the
matrix element
for the module
M
obtained from

f,v
.

For a pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathbf{U}\times\mathcal{A}\to k
, we equip the tensor products with the natural pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}\times\mathcal{A}\otimes\to k
defined by

\langle f\otimes g,a\otimes b\rangle=\langle f,a\rangle\langle g,b\rangle,\qquad f,g\in\mathbf{U},\quad a,b\in\mathcal{A}.

Definition 2.4
.

Two Hopf algebras
\mathbf{U}
and ??are said to be

dual
to each other if there is a nondegenerate pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{A}\times\mathbf{U}\to k
(that we will tacitly also use to denote the pairing of
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
with
\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}
induced by it) such that

(i)

\langle fg,x\rangle=\langle f\otimes g,\Delta(u)\rangle
for
f,g\in\mathcal{A},x\in\mathbf{U}
.

(ii)

\langle f,xy\rangle=\langle\Delta(f),x\otimes y\rangle
for
f\in\mathcal{A},x,y\in\mathbf{U}
.

(iii)

\langle 1,x\rangle=\epsilon(x)
for
x\in\mathbf{U}
.

(iv)

\langle f,1\rangle=\epsilon(f)
for
f\in\mathcal{A}
.

(v)

\langle f,Sx\rangle=\langle Sf,x\rangle
for

f\in\mathcal{A},x\in\mathbf{U}
.

The Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}
is said to contain the matrix
element
c_{f,v}
of a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
if there is a (necessarily
unique) element
h\in\mathcal{A}
such that

\forall x\in\mathbf{U}:\langle h,x\rangle=c_{f,v}(x)=f(xv).

We shall also denote that element by
c_{f,v}
and more generally
think of
\mathcal{A}
as a suitable subset of
\mathbf{U}^{*}
(and
hence interpret the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
as
the evaluation map).

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\mathbf{U},\mathcal{A}
be dual Hopf algebras. We can define left
and right actions of
\mathbf{U}
on
\mathcal{A}
as follows:

\displaystyle x\triangleright f

\displaystyle:=(\id\otimes x)\Delta(f)

\displaystyle f\triangleleft x

\displaystyle:=(x\otimes\id)\Delta(f)

where we identify
x\in\mathbf{U}
with the map
f\mapsto\langle f,x\rangle
, for
f\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle xy\triangleright f

\displaystyle=(\id\otimes xy)\Delta(f)=(\id\otimes x\otimes y)(\id\otimes\Delta)(\Delta(f))

\displaystyle=(\id\otimes x\otimes y)(\Delta\otimes\id)(\Delta(f))=(\id\otimes x)\Delta((\id\otimes y)\Delta(f))

\displaystyle=x\triangleright(y\triangleright f)

\displaystyle 1\triangleright f

\displaystyle=(\id\otimes 1)\Delta(f)=f

and similarly for
\triangleleft
.
∎

Example 2.6
.

A common choice for a dual
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbf{U}
is the set

\left\{f\in\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\exists I\leq\mathbf{U}:\dim(\mathbf{U}/I)<\infty,f(I)=0\right\}

(where
I
is a two-sided ideal), which can be shown
(see
[
13
, Corollary 1.4.5]
) to be generated by
matrix elements of finite-dimensional representations.

This is the maximal choice of
\mathcal{A}
: any other
choice
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
can be interpreted as a subset of
\mathbf{U}^{*}
satisfying
\Delta(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. By the proof of

[
13
, Lemma 1.4.2]
and by
[
13
, Lemma 1.4.1(i)]
we then find that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}

as defined above. In particular, any choice of

\mathcal{A}
is generated by matrix elements of
finite-dimensional modules.

Remark 2.7
.

In case
\mathbf{U}
has infinite-dimensional simple representations,
the matrix elements of finite-dimensional representations might
not suffice to separate the elements of
\mathbf{U}
. In other words:
there might be no Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}
dual to
\mathbf{U}
. In
that case, we can take
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbf{U}^{*}
to be the vector
space of matrix elements of semisimple representations (or of
another appropriate (full) subcategory of
\mathbf{U}-\operatorname{Rep}
).

This choice
\mathcal{A}
will not generally be a bialgebra (much less a
Hopf algebra),
but it will still be an algebra with a left and right
action of
\mathbf{U}
(via
uc_{f,v}=c_{f,uv}
and
c_{f,v}u=c_{fu,v}

for
v\in M,f\in M^{*},u\in\mathbf{U}
), so the notions from
Sections
2.1
2.2
still make sense.
However, in that case we lack an important tool in the study of
the matrix-spherical functions: the inner product.
The Haar state whose existence we assume from Lemma
2.19
onwards only exists in case

\mathcal{A}
is a cosemisimple Hopf algebra (
[
14
, Theorem 11.2.13]
).

Moreover, as
[
19
]
suggests, the proper treatment of
such cases, e.g.
\mathbf{U}
being the quantum analogue of an
affine Kac–Moody algebra, these cases likely also require
appropriate completions that we don’t introduce here.

2.2.
Matrix-Spherical Functions

From now on, we shall fix a dual pair
\mathbf{U},\mathcal{A}
of Hopf algebras.

Definition 2.8
.

A
right coideal subalgebra

is a
k
-subalgebra
??\leq\mathbf{U}
that is a right coideal, i.e.

\Delta(\mathbf{B})\subseteq\mathbf{B}\otimes\mathbf{U}.

Write
\Gamma=\Gamma(\mathbf{B})
for the set of equivalence classes of
finite-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{B}
-modules that are contained
in the restriction of a finite dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module. For every
??\in\Gamma

write
??
for a representative.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
be right coideal subalgebras.
Let
V
be a finite dimensional
\mathbf{B}
-module,
W
a finite dimensional
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
-module.
An element
f\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\Hom(W,V)
is called a

(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
-matrix-spherical function
(MSF) for
V,W
if

\forall b\in\mathbf{B},b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime},x\in\mathbf{U}:\quad f(bxb^{\prime})=bf(x)b^{\prime}

(in other words, we require
b^{\prime}\triangleright f=f\pi_{W}(b^{\prime})

for
b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}
and
f\triangleleft b=\pi_{V}(b)f
for
b\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
).
Write
E^{V,W}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}=??
(or
??
if
V\cong V(\gamma),W\cong V(\gamma^{\prime})
for
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
) for the vector space of MSF for
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},V,\mathbf{B}^{\prime},W)
.

For
V=W=k
and the module structures are given by the counit

\epsilon
, we write
E^{\epsilon}:=E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}
regardless of whether
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
or not.

In all other cases, we write
E^{V}:=E^{V,V}
and

E^{\gamma}:=E^{\gamma,\gamma}
which implies that
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
.

Example 2.10
.

Let
M
be a simple
\mathbf{U}
-module whose matrix elements are
contained in
\mathcal{A}
and
V,W
simple
finite-dimensional modules over
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
, respectively.
Let
j\in\Hom_{\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(W,M),p\in\Hom_{\mathbf{B}}(M,V)
.
Then
f:\mathbf{U}\to\Hom(W,V)
, given by

f(x)(w):=p(xj(w)),

is a MSF.
Any such function is called an
elementary
MSF.
Write
E^{V,W}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(M)=??
for
the vector space of elementary MSF for

M
, and call
the elements of
E^{\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(M)

(
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
)-zonal spherical functions
(ZSF).

Lemma 2.11
.

(i)

Let
M
be a simple
\mathbf{U}
-module and
V,W
finite-dimensional modules over
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
, respectively.
Any function
f\in E^{V,W}
that can be written in terms of matrix
elements of
M
is contained in

E^{V,W}(M)
.

(ii)

Assume that every finite-dimensional

\mathbf{U}
-module splits as a direct sum of simple modules.
Then

E^{V,W}=\bigoplus_{M\in\widehat{\mathbf{U}}}E^{V,W}(M),

where
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
denotes the set of equivalence classes of simple finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-modules.

Proof.

(i)

Write

f=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{f_{i},v_{i}}\otimes m_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}

(viewing
\Hom(W,V)\cong V\otimes W^{*}
)
for
v_{i}\in M,f_{i}\in M^{*},m_{i}\in V,\lambda_{i}\in W^{*}
.
Then the condition of being an MSF just means that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{f_{i}b,v_{i}}\otimes m_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{f_{i},v_{i}}\otimes bm_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{f_{i},b^{\prime}v_{i}}\otimes m_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{f_{i},v_{i}}\otimes m_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}b^{\prime}

for
b\in\mathbf{B},b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
.
Note that the map
M^{*}\otimes M\to\mathcal{A}
given by

f\otimes v\mapsto c_{f,v}
is injective. Applying its inverse to
f
and then permuting tensor legs, we obtain

F=\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}\otimes f_{i}\otimes v_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{B}}(M,V)\otimes\Hom_{\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(W,M).

Writing
F=\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_{i}\otimes j_{i}
and defining

f_{i}
to be the elementary MSF defined by
(p_{i},j_{i})
, we have

f=\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}.

(ii)

Let
f\in E^{V,W}
.
We decompose
\mathcal{A}
as a direct sum into
matrix elements of simple finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-modules. Write

f=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{f_{i},v_{i}}\otimes\phi_{i}

where
v_{i}\in M_{i},\lambda_{i}\in M_{i}^{*}

and
\phi_{i}\in\Hom(W,V)
for
simple
\mathbf{U}
-modules
M_{1},\dots,M_{r}
. Write

f_{i}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
M_{j}\cong M_{i}\end{subarray}}^{r}c_{f_{i},v_{i}}\otimes\phi_{i}.

Note that the decomposition of
\mathcal{A}
into

\mathbf{U}
-types is invariant under the left and right
action of
\mathbf{U}
. This shows that
f_{i}\in E^{V,W}

and hence with (i) that
f
can be written as a linear
combination of elementary MSF.

∎

Proposition 2.12
.

When equipped with the product inherited from
\mathcal{A}
, the
vector space
E^{\epsilon}
is a
k
-algebra and
E^{V,W}
is a left
E^{\epsilon}
-module.

Proof.

We only need to check that
E^{\epsilon}E^{V,W}\subseteq E^{V,W}
. Let

f\in E^{\epsilon},\Phi\in E^{V,W}
and
x\in\mathbf{U},b\in\mathbf{B},b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
and
f\in E^{\epsilon},\Phi\in E^{V,W}
, then

\displaystyle(f\Phi)(bx)

\displaystyle=f(b_{(1)}x_{(1)})\Phi(b_{(2)}x_{(2)})=\epsilon(b_{(1)})f(x_{(1)})\Phi(b_{(2)}x_{(2)})

\displaystyle=f(x_{(1)})\Phi(bx_{(2)})=b(f\Phi)(x)

\displaystyle(f\Phi)(xb^{\prime})

\displaystyle=f(x_{(1)}b^{\prime}_{(1)})\Phi(x_{(2)}b^{\prime}_{(2)})=\epsilon(b^{\prime}_{(1)})f(x_{(1)})\Phi(x_{(2)}u^{\prime}_{(2)})

\displaystyle=f(x_{(1)})\Phi(x_{(2)}u^{\prime})=(f\Phi)(x)u^{\prime}.\qed

Lastly, we prove a statement for
V=W=k
the trivial representation:

Lemma 2.13

(
[
31
, Proposition 4.25]
)
.

Let
\varphi\in E^{\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}
and assume that
S
is invertible.
Then
\varphi\circ S\in E^{\epsilon}_{S^{-2}(\mathbf{B}^{\prime}),\mathbf{B}}
.

Proof.

Let
b\in\mathbf{B},b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime},x\in\mathbf{U}
, then

\displaystyle\varphi(S(S^{-2}(b^{\prime})xb))

\displaystyle=\varphi(S(b)S(x)S^{-1}(b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\varphi\quantity(S\Big(\epsilon(b_{(1)})b_{(2)}\Big)
S(x)
S^{-1}\Big(\epsilon(b'_{(1)})b'_{(2)}\Big))

\displaystyle=\epsilon(b_{(1)})\>\varphi\quantity(S(b_{(2)}) S(x) S^{-1}(b'_{(2)}))\>\epsilon(b^{\prime}_{(1)}).

Since the elements
b_{(1)}
and
b^{\prime}_{(1)}
lie in
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
,
respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle=\varphi\Big(b_{(1)}S(b_{(2)})S(x)S^{-1}(b^{\prime}_{(2)})b^{\prime}_{(1)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\varphi\Big(b_{(1)}S(b_{(2)})S(x)S^{-1}(S(b^{\prime}_{(1)})b^{\prime}_{(2)})\Big)

\displaystyle=\epsilon(b)\epsilon(b^{\prime})\varphi(S(x))

\displaystyle=\epsilon(S^{-2}(b^{\prime}))\varphi(S(x))\epsilon(b).\qed

Corollary 2.14
.

Let
\varphi\in E^{\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}
and let
S
be invertible.
Let
b\in\mathbf{B},b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime},x\in\mathbf{U}
, then

\varphi(S(b)xS^{-1}(b^{\prime}))=\epsilon(S(b))\varphi(x)\epsilon(S^{-1}(b^{\prime})).

Proof.

By Lemma
2.13
,
\varphi\circ S
is
a ZSF for
S^{-2}(\mathbf{B}^{\prime}),\mathbf{B}
.
Consequently,

\varphi(S(b)xS^{-1}(b^{\prime}))=\varphi(S(S^{-2}(b^{\prime})S^{-1}(x)b))=\epsilon(b)\epsilon(b^{\prime})\varphi(x).\qed

2.3.
Orthogonality

We will now define inner product methods to study matrix-spherical functions. In later sections, these inner products will be crucial to identify the matrix-spherical functions with Intermediate Macdonald polynomials.
The elementary constructions of this section generalize constructions in the classical setting of
[
44
, §6]
, or those of
[
30
, §4]
in the quantum case of characters. The latter is based on a similar construction appearing in
[
2
, §6]
.

For this section we will assume that the square of the antipode can
be expressed as follows:
S^{2}=\ad(K)
for a
K\in\mathbf{U}
satisfying
\Delta(K)=K\otimes K
(or more
generally for a natural transformation
K
of the forgetful functor of a suitable monoidal subcategory of the
\mathbf{U}
-modules
that contains the adjoint representation and the left representation on
\mathcal{A}
, such that

K_{M\otimes N}=K_{M}\otimes K_{N}
).

Remark 2.15
.

In
[
4
, §3.2]
, the element
K
is referred to as a
sovereign
element, and
\mathbf{U}
is then termed a
sovereign
Hopf algebra (Appel’s further assumption of quasi-triangularity is not necessary for this definition).
We will later see that
Drinfel’d–Jimbo quantum groups,
the main choice of
\mathbf{U}
used in this work, are sovereign.

Definition 2.16
.

For finite-dimensional modules
V,W
over
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
we define

??:E^{V,W}\otimes E^{W,V}\to E^{\epsilon},\qquad\Xi_{V}(\Phi,\Psi)(x):=\tr_{V}\quantity(\Phi(x_{(1)})\Psi\big(S(x_{(2)})K\big)).

Proposition 2.17
.

(i)

\Xi_{V,W}
is well-defined.

(ii)

If
\Phi\in E^{V,W},\Psi\in E^{W,V}
and
f,g\in E^{\epsilon}
, then

\Xi_{V,W}(f\Phi,g\Psi)=f\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)S(K\triangleright g).

(iii)

Let
\Phi\in E^{V,W},\Psi\in E^{W,V}
,
then

\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)=S(K\triangleright\Xi_{W,V}(\Psi,\Phi)).

Proof.

(i)

\Xi_{V,W}
is evidently well-defined as map

(\mathcal{A}\otimes\Hom(V,W))\times(\mathcal{A}\otimes\Hom(W,V))\to\mathcal{A}
. It is
k
-bilinear and
hence descends to the tensor product. It remains to show
that it indeed restricts to
E^{V,W}\otimes E^{W,V}
as claimed.
Note that
\Psi(S(\cdot)K)=\Psi(S(K^{-1}\cdot))=S(\Psi)\triangleleft K^{-1}
.
Let
b\in\mathbf{B},b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime},x\in\mathbf{U}
, then

\displaystyle\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)(xb^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi\big(x_{(1)} b'_{(1)}\big)\Psi\big(S(b'_{(2)})S(x_{(2)}) K\big))

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi(x_{(1)})\Psi\big(b'_{(1)}S(b'_{(2)}) S(x_{(2)}) K\big))

\displaystyle=\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)(x)\epsilon(b^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)(bx)

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi\big(b_{(1)}x_{(1)}\big)
\Psi\big(S(x_{(2)})S(b_{(2)}) K\big))

\displaystyle=\tr(b_{(1)} \Phi(x_{(1)})\Psi\big(S(x_{(2)})S(b_{(2)}) K\big))

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi(x_{(1)}) \Psi\big(S(x_{(2)}) S(b_{(2)}) K b_{(1)}\big))

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi(x_{(1)}) \Psi\big(S(x_{(2)}) S(b_{(2)}) S^2(b_{(1)}) K\big))

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi(x_{(1)}) \Psi\big(S(x_{(2)}) S(S(b_{(1)})b_{(2)}) K\big))

\displaystyle=\epsilon(b)\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)(x)

using the fact that
\Delta(b)\in\mathbf{B}\otimes\mathbf{U}
(and similar for primes), general
properties of
\Delta
and
S
, and the fact that
S^{2}=\ad(K)
by
assumption.

(ii)

Let
x\in\mathbf{U}
, then

\displaystyle\Xi_{V,W}(f\Phi,g\Psi)(x)

\displaystyle=\tr\quantity((f\Phi)(x_{(1)})(g\Psi)\big(S(x_{(2)})K\big))

\displaystyle=\tr(f(x_{(1)}) \Phi(x_{(2)})
g\big(S(x_{(4)})K\big) \Psi\Big(S(x_{(3)})K\big))

\displaystyle=f(x_{(1)})\tr\quantity(\Psi(x_{(2)})
\Psi\big(S(x_{(3)})K\big))g\big(S(x_{(4)})K\big)

\displaystyle=f(x_{(1)})\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)(x_{(2)})S(K\triangleright g)(x_{(3)}),

which equals
f\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)S(K\triangleright g)
, evaluated in
x
.

(iii)

Let
x\in\mathbf{U}
, then

\displaystyle S(K\triangleright\Xi_{W,V}(\Psi,\Phi))(x)

\displaystyle=\Xi_{W,V}(\Psi,\Phi)(S(x)K)

\displaystyle=\tr_{W}(\Psi(S(x)_{(1)}K)\Phi(S(S(x)_{(2)}K)K))

\displaystyle=\tr_{W}(\Psi(S(x_{(2)})K)\Phi(K^{-1}S^{2}(x_{(1)})K))

\displaystyle=\tr_{W}(\Psi(S(x_{(2)})K)\Phi(x_{(1)})).

Since
V,W
are finite-dimensional,
we can leverage the cyclicity of the trace to obtain

=\tr_{V}(\Phi(x_{(1)})\Psi(S(x_{(2)})K))=\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)(x).\qed

Definition 2.18
.

A functional

??:\mathcal{A}\to k
is called
left-invariant
if

(\id\otimes h)\circ\Delta=h.

A left-invariant functional
h
satisfying
h(1)=1
is called a

Haar state
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
have a unique Haar state
h
and let

f,g\in\mathcal{A}
be matrix coefficients of the simple modules

M,N
, respectively. Then
h(fS(g))\neq 0
implies that

M\cong N
. Moreover, restricted to the matrix coefficients
of a simple module
M
, the map
(f,g)\mapsto h(fS(g))
defines
a non-degenerate pairing. In particular, we have

h(c_{f,v}S(c_{g,w}))=\frac{f(w)g(Kv)}{\tr(K_M)}

for a simple
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
and
v,w\in M
and
f,g\in M^{*}
,
and for
K
still chosen such that
S^{2}=\ad(K)
.

Proof.

The first claim follows from
[
14
, Theorem 11.2.13, Proposition 11.2.15]
.

In particular, pick a basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in M
and

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in M^{*}
the dual basis. Then
assume that

v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}v_{i},\qquad w=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}v_{i},\qquad f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\lambda_{i},\qquad g=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}\lambda_{i}.

Note that
K
is the intertwiner in question. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle h(c_{f,v}S(c_{g,w}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{j}c_{k}d_{l}h(c_{\lambda_{k},v_{i}}S(c_{\lambda_{l},v_{j}}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{j}c_{k}d_{l}\delta_{k,j}\frac{\lambda_{l}(Kv_{i})}{\tr(K_M)}

\displaystyle=\frac{f(w)g(Kv)}{\tr(K_M)}.\qed

Corollary 2.20
.

Assume there exists a unique Haar state. Then the map
h\circ\Xi_{V,W}

is a symmetric bilinear pairing of
E^{V,W}
with
E^{W,V}
,
such that elementary MSFs for
non-isomorphic simple
\mathbf{U}
-modules are orthogonal.

Proof.

Let
\Phi,\Psi
be elementary MSF for non-isomorphic simple
\mathbf{U}
-modules,
say

\Phi=\sum_{i}c_{f_{i},v_{i}}\otimes\phi_{i},\qquad\Psi=\sum_{j}c_{g_{i},w_{i}}\otimes\psi_{i}.

Then

\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)=\sum_{i,j}c_{f_{i},v_{i}}S(c_{g_{j},Kw_{j}})\tr_{V}(\phi_{i}\psi_{j})

and hence

h(\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi))=\sum_{i,j}h(c_{f_{i},v_{i}}S(c_{g_{j},Kw_{j}}))\tr_{V}(\phi_{i}\psi_{j})=0

by Lemma
2.19
.

To show symmetry, it suffices to consider the case
M=N
,
in which we have

\displaystyle h(\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}h(c_{f_{i},v_{i}}S(c_{g_{j},Kw_{j}}))\tr_{V}(\phi_{i}\psi_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\frac{f_{i}(Kw_{j})g_{j}(Kv_{i})}{\tr_{M}(K)}\tr_{V}(\phi_{i}\psi_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}h(c_{g_{j},w_{j}}S(c_{f_{i},Kv_{i}}))\tr_{W}(\psi_{j}\phi_{i})

\displaystyle=h(\Xi_{W,V}(\Psi,\Phi))

by Lemma
2.19
.
∎

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
M
be a simple
\mathbf{U}
-module that is
semisimple over
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
with
\tr(K_M)\neq 0
and
let

\Phi\in E^{V,W}
be a nonzero elementary MSF
for
M
.
Then there is
\Psi\in E^{W,V}
elementary for
M
such that
h\circ\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)=\frac{\tr(K_M|_V)\tr(K_M|_W)}{\tr(K_M)}
(assuming that
V,W
are realised as subsets of

M
).

Proof.

Assume that
V\subseteq M,W\subseteq M
according to the
embedding and projection that define
\Phi
.

Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V\subseteq M
and

w_{1},\dots,w_{m}\in W\subseteq M
be bases of
V,W
and let

f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in V^{*},g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in W^{*}
be the dual
bases extended to
M
in such a way that the other
\mathbf{B}
-types are mapped to 0. We then have

\Phi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{g_{j},v_{i}}\otimes w_{j}f_{i}.

Here
w_{j}f_{i}
denotes the linear map
V\to W
mapping

v\mapsto f_{i}(v)w_{j}
.
Define

\Psi:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{f_{i},w_{j}}\otimes v_{i}g_{j}\in E^{W,V}.

Then we have

\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)=\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}\sum_{j,l=1}^{m}c_{g_{j},v_{i}}S(c_{f_{k},Kw_{l}})\tr_{V}(w_{j}f_{i}v_{k}g_{l})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{g_{j},v_{i}}S(c_{f_{i},Kw_{j}}).

Applying
h
to this, and applying Lemma
2.19
, we obtain

h(\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{g_{j}(Kw_{j})f_{i}(Kv_{i})}{\tr(K_M)}=\frac{\tr(K_M|_V)\tr(K_M|_W)}{\tr(K_M)}.\qed

3.
Drinfel’d–Jimbo Quantum Groups

We will now define the concrete Hopf algebras and coideal subalgebras we
will be working with for the rest of this paper. The notation is mostly
taken from
[
27
]
and
[
18
]
.

3.1.
Root System

Let
I=\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}
and
A=(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
be a symmetrisable Cartan
matrix and ??be two lattices equipped with a perfect
pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:Y\times X\to\mathbb{Z}
, and with
linearly independent elements

??\in X,\qquad??\in Y

such that
\langle h_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle=a_{i,j}
for all

i,j\in I
. Note that in general we don’t require
X\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and

Y\otimes\mathbb{Q}
to be spanned by the
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
or

(h_{i})_{i\in I}
. If e.g.
A
is of affine type, this will not be
the case.

Let
??\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
DA
is
symmetric for
D=\operatorname{diag}(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n})
. Equivalently, we can imagine
X
to be equipped
with a symmetric bilinear form
\cdot
such that

\displaystyle\forall i\in I:\quad

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i}\in 2\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\forall i\neq j\in I:\quad

\displaystyle 2\frac{\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i}}\in-\mathbb{N}_{0}

and such that

\forall i,j\in I:\quad\langle h_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle=2\frac{\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i}}.

Then
\epsilon_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i}}{2}
.

Define the embedding
X\to Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q},\lambda\mapsto??
by
mapping
\lambda\in X
to the unique element
h\in Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
satisfying

\forall\mu\in X:\mu\cdot\lambda=\langle h,\mu\rangle.

In particular,
h_{\alpha_{i}}=\epsilon_{i}h_{i}\in Y
.

Let
H=(h_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
be the Coxeter matrix associated
to
A
, i.e. we have

h_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1&i=j\\
\frac{\pi}{\arccos(\frac{\sqrt{a_{i,j}a_{j,i}}}{2})}&a_{i,j}a_{j,i}<4\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

The braid group
B
and the Weyl group ??are then the
Artin braid group and the Coxeter group for the Coxeter matrix
H
. Both act on
X
and
Y
as follows:

s_{i}\mu:=\mu-\langle h_{i},\mu\rangle\alpha_{i},\qquad s_{i}h:=h-\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle h_{i}.

Let
Q,Q^{\vee}
be the lattices generated by
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}

and
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
, respectively,
and let
P^{\vee},P
be the corresponding dual lattices.
Note that in general,
P^{\vee},P
are quotients of
Y,X
, respectively.
Let

\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n}\in P,\qquad\omega_{1}^{\vee},\dots,\omega_{n}^{\vee}\in P^{\vee}

be the dual bases of
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
.

These are the fundamental weights and coweights. Note
that in general,
P,P^{\vee}
are quotients of
X,Y
, respectively.
The lattices
Q,Q^{\vee},P,P^{\vee}
are evidently also acted upon
by
W
.
Let
??\subseteq Q
be the union of all
W
-orbits of

\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
.
It is a reduced root system, ??are its root and
weight lattices, and ??are its coroot and coweight lattices.
Moreover,
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n},\omega_{1}^{\vee},\dots,\omega_{n}^{\vee}
are its fundamental (co)weights.

Write
\operatorname{ht}:Q\to\mathbb{Z}
for the group homomorphism
mapping
\alpha_{i}\mapsto 1
(
i\in I
).
In case
X=P,Y=Q^{\vee}
, the lattices
X,Y
are said to
make up the
simply connected root datum
. In case

X=Q,Y=P^{\vee}
, the lattices are referred to as the
adjoint root datum
.

We make
Q,Q^{\vee}
into ordered abelian groups by declaring that the
elements of
Q^{+}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{N}_{0}\alpha_{i}
and
Q^{\vee+}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{N}_{0}h_{i}

are non-negative.
This extends to partial orders on
X,Y
.
Write
\mathcal{R}^{+},\mathcal{R}^{\vee+}
for the subsets of

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}^{\vee}
of positive elements.

\omega\in P
(resp.
\omega^{\vee}\in P^{\vee}
) is said to be dominant if

\omega(Q^{\vee+})\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
(resp.
\omega^{\vee}(Q^{+})\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
).
Similarly
\mu\in X
is said to be dominant if

\langle Q^{\vee+},\mu\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Write
X^{+}
for the subset of dominant elements.

Choose
2??\in Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
such that

\langle 2\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle=2\epsilon_{i}.

In case
\mathcal{R}
is finite (equivalently,
W
is finite), we say that
I
is of
finite type
.
In that case, such a
2\rho
is given for example by

2\rho:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{R}^{+}}h_{\alpha}.

Bijective maps
\tau:I\to I
are called
diagram automorphisms
if
a_{i,j}=a_{\tau(i),\tau(j)}
for all
i,j\in I
.

3.2.
Quantum Groups

Let ??be an indeterminate and let

??:=\overline{\mathbb{C}(q)}
, this is the base field for all our definitions,
although most notions can also be defined over
\mathbb{Q}(q)
.
Later we will also adopt the notation of
q
-numbers. Let
K
be an indeterminate, then we set:

(1)

??:=\frac{q^{ab}-q^{-ab}}{q^{b}-q^{-b}},\qquad??:=\frac{Kq^{ab}-K^{-1}q^{-ab}}{q^{b}-q^{-b}}

(as in
[
48
, §2.2]
) as well as
[K]_{q^{b}}=[K;0]_{q^{b}}
wherever this doesn’t lead to confusion. Define

q_{i}:=q^{\epsilon_{i}}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Define the
Drinfel’d–Jimbo quantum group
??to be the associative
k
-algebra generated by

(E_{i})_{i\in I},\qquad(F_{i})_{i\in I},\qquad(K_{h})_{h\in Y}

subject to the relations

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\forall h,h^{\prime}\in Y:\quad K_{h}K_{h^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=K_{h+h^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\forall i\in I,h\in Y:\quad K_{h}E_{i}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle}E_{i}K_{h}

\displaystyle\forall i\in I,h\in Y\quad K_{h}F_{i}

\displaystyle=q^{-\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle}F_{i}K_{h}

\displaystyle\forall i,j\in I:\quad\commutator{E_i}{F_j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}}=\delta_{ij}[K_{i}]_{q_{i}}

\displaystyle\forall i,j\in I:\quad F_{ij}(E_{i},E_{j})

\displaystyle=F_{ij}(F_{i},F_{j})=0

where
K_{i}:=K_{\epsilon_{i}h_{i}}
and

F_{ij}(x,y)=\sum_{p+p^{\prime}=1-a_{ij}}\frac{(-1)^{p^{\prime}}}{[p]^{!}[p^{\prime}]^{!}}x^{p}yx^{p^{\prime}}

for
i,j\in I
.

We equip
\mathbf{U}
with the following Hopf algebra structure:

\displaystyle\Delta(E_{i})

\displaystyle:=E_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}\otimes E_{i}

\displaystyle\Delta(F_{i})

\displaystyle:=F_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{-1}+1\otimes F_{i}

\displaystyle\Delta(K_{h})

\displaystyle:=K_{h}\otimes K_{h}

\displaystyle\epsilon(E_{i})

\displaystyle:=\epsilon(F_{i}):=0

\displaystyle\epsilon(K_{h})

\displaystyle:=1

\displaystyle S(E_{i})

\displaystyle:=-K_{i}^{-1}E_{i}

\displaystyle S(F_{i})

\displaystyle:=-F_{i}K_{i}

\displaystyle S(K_{h})

\displaystyle:=K_{-h}

for
i\in I
and
h\in Y
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The thus-defined
(\mathbf{U},\Delta,\epsilon,S)
is a Hopf algebra.

Proof.

This is concluded at the end of
[
27
,
§3.3.4
]
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Our setting corresponds to an
X
- and
Y
-regular root
datum of type
(I,\cdot)
from
[
27
]
, where the
embeddings
I\to X
and
I\to Y
are given by

i\mapsto\alpha_{i}
and
i\mapsto h_{i}
, respectively.
In
[
18
, §2.1]
, an explicit construction is given
for lattices
Y
(
P^{\vee}
in Kolb’s notation) and
X
(resp.

P
) where
Y
is an extension of
Q^{\vee}
.
In that setting of Kac–Moody algebras, the
elements of
\mathcal{R}
are referred to as the

real
roots, in contrast to those weights of the
Kac–Moody algebra that are not contained in
\mathcal{R}
, which are the
imaginary
roots.
This differentiation does not make much sense for quantum
groups as the weights of
\mathbf{U}
form a lattice,
Q
,
and not a root system.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
2\rho
as chosen earlier lies in
Y
(as is the case for
\mathcal{R}
finite), we have
S^{2}=\ad(K_{-2\rho})
.
This shows that
\mathbf{U}
is sovereign as in Remark
2.15
.

Proof.

Both
S^{2}
and
\ad(K_{-2\rho})
are endomorphisms of

k
-algebras, so it suffices to prove that they agree on the
algebra generators. We have

\displaystyle S^{2}(K_{h})

\displaystyle=K_{h}=\ad(K_{-2\rho})(K_{h})

\displaystyle S^{2}(E_{i})

\displaystyle=-S(K_{i}^{-1}E_{i})=-S(E_{i})S(K_{i}^{-1})=K_{i}^{-1}E_{i}K_{i}

\displaystyle=q^{-2\epsilon_{i}}E_{i}=K_{-2\rho}E_{i}K_{2\rho}=\ad(K_{-2\rho})(E_{i})

\displaystyle S^{2}(F_{i})

\displaystyle=-S(F_{i}K_{i})=K_{i}^{-1}F_{i}K_{i}

\displaystyle=q^{2\epsilon_{i}}F_{i}=K_{-2\rho}F_{i}K_{2\rho}=\ad(K_{-2\rho})(F_{i}).\qed

Definition 3.5
.

For
\lambda\in X^{+}
, write ??for the simple

\mathbf{U}
-module with highest weight
\lambda
, as defined e.g. in
[
27
, Proposition 3.5.6]
(it is called
\Lambda_{\lambda}
there; simplicity is proven in Corollary 6.2.3 under the condition of
X
- and

Y
-regularity).

Similarly, we write

E^{V,W}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(\lambda)
for

E^{V,W}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(L(\lambda))
.

Theorem 3.6

(
[
27
, Corollary 6.2.3(c)]
)
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}
-module satisfying:

(i)

Weight module: For
\lambda\in X
we define

M_{\lambda}:=\left\{m\in M\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall h\in Y:K_{h}m=q^{\langle h,\lambda\rangle}m\right\}.

We require that
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}M_{\lambda}
;

(ii)

Integrability: For every
m\in M
andH
i\in I
there is

N\in N_{0}
such that for all
n\geq N
:

E_{i}^{n}m=F_{i}^{n}m=0;

(iii)

Local boundedness above: For every
m\in M
there is

N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that for all
\lambda\in Q
with
\operatorname{ht}(\lambda)\geq N
and all
x\in\mathbf{U}_{\lambda}
(here
\mathbf{U}_{\lambda}
is the
\lambda
-weight space considered as a
\ad(\mathbf{U})
module) we have

xm=0
.

Then
M
can be written as a sum of simple modules of the form

L(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in X^{+}
.

Corollary 3.7

(
[
27
, Propositions 6.3.4,6.3.6]
)
.

If
I
is of finite type, the simple modules
L(\lambda)
for

\lambda\in X^{+}
are finite-dimensional. Furthermore, any
finite-dimensional or integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
satisfies the
condition of Theorem
3.6
.

Remark 3.8
.

Note that even if
2\rho\not\in Y
, the following construction allows us to construct a natural transformation
K_{-2\rho}

(of the forgetful functor of the category of
\mathbf{U}
-weight modules)
such that

S^{2}(x)=\ad(K_{-2\rho})(x)

for all
x\in\mathbf{U}
, and such that

S^{2}(x)v=K_{-2\rho}xK_{-2\rho}^{-1}v

whenever in addition
v
is an element of a
\mathbf{U}
-weight module.

Let
h\in Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, let

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
nh\in Y
.
We pick a
n
-th root
q_{0}
of
q
, which exists since
k
is algebraically closed.

Given this choice, we define the natural transformation
K_{h}
as follows:
for any weight module
M
we have

\forall\lambda\in X,m\in M_{\lambda}:\quad K_{h}m:=q_{0}^{\langle nh,\lambda\rangle}m,

which is well-defined since
\langle nh,\lambda\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
.
This is a natural
transformation since for every morphism
f:M\to N
we have

f(M_{\lambda})\subseteq N_{\lambda}
, and hence
f
also intertwines the
action of
K_{h}
.

In particular, since
\mathbf{U}
is a weight module over
\mathbf{U}
(via
\ad
),
the operator
\ad(K_{-2\rho})
makes sense and with the same proof as for Lemma
3.4
we obtain that
S^{2}=\ad(K_{-2\rho})
for a suitably chosen

2\rho\in Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, which means that

\langle 2\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle=2\epsilon_{i}.

Recall that
k=\overline{\mathbb{C}(q)}
and that
\mathbb{C}\subseteq k
is a subfield. In this way, the quantum group
\mathbf{U}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra.

Definition 3.9
.

Define
??:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}
to be the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra homomorphism mapping

q\mapsto q^{-1},\qquad E_{i}\mapsto E_{i},\qquad F_{i}\mapsto F_{i},\qquad K_{h}\mapsto K_{-h}

for
i\in I,h\in Y
. This is well-defined by

[
27
, §3.1.12]
and is called the
bar involution
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
and
v_{\lambda}\in L(\lambda)_{\lambda}\setminus\{0\}
. Define
\overline{\,\cdot\,}:L(\lambda)\to L(\lambda)
to be the unique
\mathbb{C}
-linear map with

\overline{xv_{\lambda}}=\overline{x}v_{\lambda},\qquad x\in\mathbf{U}

which extends to a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map
\overline{\,\cdot\,}:L(\lambda)^{\ast}\to L(\lambda)^{\ast}
by setting

\overline{f}(v):=\overline{f(\overline{v})}.

This is well-defined by

[
27
, §19.3.4]

Definition 3.11
.

For each
w\in W
write ??for the automorphism
T_{w,1}^{\prime\prime}:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}
from
[
27
,
§37.1.3
, §39.4.7]
.

For all
h\in Y
and
w\in W
, we recall that
T_{w}(K_{h})=K_{wh}
.

Definition 3.12
.

For a
\mathbf{U}
-weight module
M
and
\xi\in\Hom(X,k^{\times})
we define the endomorphism
\xi:M\to M
as
\xi v_{\mu}=\xi(\mu)v_{\mu}
, for
v_{\mu}\in M_{\mu}.

We set
\mathbf{U}^{0}=k\langle K_{h}:h\in Y\rangle.
Recall that
\mathbf{U}
is a
\mathbf{U}
-weight module via
\ad
, for each
\xi\in\Hom(X,k)
the automorphism
\ad(\xi):\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}
(i.e.
\xi
acting in the adjoint representation of
\mathbf{U}
) acts as the identity on
\mathbf{U}^{0}
as it has weight
0
.

Definition 3.13
.

In case
I
is of finite type, let
\mathcal{A}
be the algebra
generated by finite-dimensional matrix elements, i.e.
the maximal dual algebra from Example
2.6
. This is the
quantum function algebra
and
it is dual with
\mathbf{U}
via the evaluation map.

If
\mathcal{R}
is irreducible and of infinite type, by
[
13
, Lemma 7.1.15(ii)]
every simple
module is either 1-dimensional or infinite-dimensional.
We conclude that the maximal dual algebra
\mathcal{A}
from
Example
2.6
consists of the
multiplicative characters.
Unless
\mathbf{U}
is commutative,
\mathcal{A}
will not
be large enough to be a dual Hopf algebra for

\mathbf{U}
. This is why we will later assume that
I
is of
finite type.

Lemma 3.14
.

If
I
is of finite type, the centre
Z(U)
is
spanned by elements ??for every
\mu\in X^{+}

satisfying
2h_{\mu}\in Y
.

c_{\mu}
acts on the finite-dimensional simple module

L(\lambda)
as the scalar

\sum_{\nu\in X}q^{-2\lambda\cdot\nu-\langle 2\rho,\nu\rangle}\dim(L(\mu)_{\nu})

Proof.

This follows from
[
13
, §7.1.17,§7.1.19]
.
The fact that we work with arbitrary choices of lattices

X,Y
instead of the simply-connected lattices, is
reflected in the fact that the
c_{\mu}
of Joseph is an
element whose image under the Harish-Chandra map
is made up of
K_{-2h_{\nu}}
where
L(\mu)_{\nu}\neq 0
,
so in order for these elements to be contained in our
quantum group, we also require that
2h_{\mu}\in Y

(which then also implies
2h_{\nu}\in Y
for all other
weights
\nu
of
L(\mu)
).
∎

3.3.
Quantum Symmetric Pair Coideal Subalgebras

In this section we introduce quantum symmetric pair coideal subalgebras following
[
22
]
and
[
18
]
.

Notation 3.15
.

For each subset
I_{\bullet}\subseteq I
of finite type, let ??denote the longest element in the parabolic subgroup
??=\langle s_{i}:i\in I_{\bullet}\rangle\subseteq W
.

Definition 3.16
.

An
admissible pair
??consists of a subset
I_{\bullet}\subseteq I
of finite type and an involutive diagram automorphism
\tau:I\to I
such that

(i)

\tau|_{I_{\bullet}}=-w_{\bullet}
;

(ii)

If
i\in I\setminus I_{\bullet}
and
\tau(i)=i
then
\langle\rho^{\vee}_{\bullet},\alpha_{i}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Here
\rho_{\bullet}^{\vee}
denotes the half sum of positive coroots of the root system generated by
I_{\bullet}
. Set
I_{\circ}:=I\setminus I_{\bullet}
and set

I_{\mathrm{ns}}:=\left\{i\in I_{\circ}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau(i)=i,\forall j\in I_{\bullet}:\quad\langle h_{j},\alpha_{i}\rangle=0\right\}.

An admissible pair is said to be
compatible
with the datum of
the lattices
X,Y
if there is an involution
\tau
on
X
(that we extend
to
Y
by the pairing) satisfying
\tau(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{\tau(i)}
.

Remark 3.17
.

Note that in case
X
and
Y
are generated
by roots and coweights or weights and coweights
(respectively), the compatibility condition is
automatically satisfied.

Example 3.18
.

Let
I=I^{\prime}\sqcup I^{\prime}
, where
I^{\prime}
are the
simple roots of a root system
\Sigma
,
and let
\tau
be the diagram automorphism that
exchanges the two copies of
I^{\prime}
.
Let furthermore
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
be lattices used to
define a quantum group with
I^{\prime}
, and let

X:=X^{\prime}\oplus X^{\prime}
(same for
Y
), and extend

\tau
to these lattices by exchanging the
two copies of
X^{\prime}
(resp.
Y^{\prime}
).
Then
(\emptyset,\tau)
is an admissible pair
for
I
that is compatible with
X,Y
.
This is called the
group case
since
this mirrors the construction
G=G^{\prime}\times G^{\prime}

and
K=\diag(G^{\prime})
of a symmetric pair
(G,K)

whose symmetric space
G/K
is the group
G^{\prime}

itself.

For each admissible pair
(I_{\bullet},\tau)
compatible with
X,Y
we associate an involution
??=-w_{\bullet}\circ\tau
acting on
X,Y
as
well as
Q,Q^{\vee},P,P^{\vee}
.
For each root
\alpha\in\mathcal{R}
, set
??=\frac{\alpha-\Theta(\alpha)}{2}
. The set

??=\left\{\widetilde{\alpha}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\alpha\in\mathcal{R},\,\Theta(\alpha)\neq\alpha\right\}\subseteq\frac{1}{2}X.

is called the
restricted root system
.
Write
\Sigma^{\vee}\subseteq Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
for its dual.
Define

??:=\left\{\lambda\in X\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\langle Y^{\Theta},\lambda\rangle=0,\langle\Sigma^{\vee},\lambda\rangle\subseteq 2\mathbb{Z}\right\}

(
??
denoting the
\Theta
-invariant elements of
Y
).
We set

??=k\langle E_{j},F_{j},K_{j}^{\pm}\,:\,j\in I_{\bullet}\rangle\leq\mathbf{U}

and set

\mathbf{U}_{\Theta}^{0}=k\langle K_{h}\,:\,h\in Y^{\Theta}\rangle\leq\mathbf{U}.

Remark 3.19
.

By
[
18
, Definition 2.3]
and an argument similar to
[
5
, §2.5]
, the
finite-type admissible pairs are classified by the group
cases (Example
3.18
) and the Satake diagrams.

Definition 3.20
.

We now define the
quantum symmetric pair coideal subalgebra

??=\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
associated to the admissible pair
(I_{\bullet},\tau)
compatible with
X,Y
. It is the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
,
\mathbf{U}_{\Theta}^{0}
, and the elements

B_{i}=F_{i}+c_{i}T_{w_{\bullet}}(E_{\tau(i)})K_{i}^{-1}+s_{i}K_{i}^{-1}\qquad(i\in I_{\circ}).

We refer to
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}})
as a
quantum symmetric pair
(QSP), and to the algebra
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
as a QSP-coideal subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
.
The QSP-coideal subalgebra
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
depends on a family of scalars
(\bm{c},\bm{s})\in(k^{\times})^{I_{\circ}}\times k^{I_{\circ}}
that satisfy

\displaystyle\bm{c}\in\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{d}\in(k^{\times})^{I_{\circ}}:d_{i}=d_{\tau(i)}\quad\text{if}\quad\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})=0\}

\displaystyle\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{t}\in k^{I_{\circ}}:t_{j}\neq 0\implies(j\in I_{\mathrm{ns}}\text{ and }\langle h_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle\in-2\mathbb{N}_{0}\,\forall i\in I_{\mathrm{ns}}\setminus\{j\})\}.

Remark 3.21
.

Note that our choice of parameters
\bm{c}
differs from
[
18
]

because we absorb the factors
s(I,\tau)(\alpha_{i})
into

c_{i}
.
That the condition
c_{i}=c_{\tau(i)}
for
\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})=0
remains unchanged follows from
the fact that
\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})=0
for

i\neq\tau(i)
and
i\in I_{\circ}
implies that
\langle\rho_{\bullet},\alpha_{i}\rangle=0
as well.

QSP-coideal subalgebras
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
are right coideal subalgebras, i.e.

\triangle(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}})\subseteq\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\otimes\mathbf{U}.

For
\bm{s}=0
, the corresponding QSP-coideal subalgebra is denoted by
\mathbf{B}
and is referred to as
standard
.
As is explained in
[
18
, §9.1]
, the group
\Hom(X,k)
acts on the set of parameters by
mapping

(2)

\xi:(\bm{c},\bm{s})\mapsto(\bm{d},\bm{t})\qquad\text{if}\qquad\Ad(\xi)(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}})=\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}.

This action induces an equivalence relation on the set of parameters.

Remark 3.22
.

By
[
18
,
Rem 9.3
]
, if
I
is of finite type and
\Sigma
is reduced, any two standard parameters are equivalent.
Furthermore, if
I
is irreducible and
\Sigma
is
non-reduced, there is
i\in I
such
that
(\bm{c},0)\sim(\bm{d},0)
iff

c_{i}=d_{i}
.

A concrete and complete criterion for equivalence
under
\ad(\Hom(X,k))
will be provided in (
31
).

Remark 3.23
.

From now on we assume that
I
is of finite type, unless stated otherwise. It is worth to mention that most of the notions and some of the results can be extended to Kac–Moody type.

3.4.
Specialization

This section studies the specialization
q=1
of quantum groups, QSP-coideal subalgebras and their modules. Our approach follows
[
23
]
.

Definition 3.24
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be the smallest subring of
k
containing
q
such that every element in
\mathbf{A}
that is not contained in the ideal generated by
(q-1)
has a square root and is invertible, cf.
[
23
,
Sec 1
]
.

Definition 3.25
.

Let
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
denote the
\mathbf{A}
-subalgebra generated by
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{-1}
and
\frac{K_{i}-1}{q-1}
, with
i\in I
. Furthermore, for each subspace
W\subseteq\mathbf{U}
we write
\widehat{W}=W\cap\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
.

Recall that the quotient
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}/(q-1)\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
is isomorphic to
U(\mathfrak{g})
, the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, the reductive
Lie algebra that can be defined from the Cartan subalgebra
Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
and
the Dynkin diagram
I
.
For a dominant weight
\lambda\in X^{+}
the
\mathbf{A}
-module
L_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda):=\widehat{\mathbf{U}}v_{\lambda}
is specialized by setting
L(\lambda)^{1}:=L_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)/(q-1)L_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
.
The
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module
L(\lambda)^{1}
is the highest weight module of weight
\lambda
.
We write
\cl
for the quotient maps

\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\to U(\mathfrak{g})
,

L^{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)\to L(\lambda)^{1}
and

\mathbf{A}\to\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 3.26
.

A
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-module
V
is called
specializable
if there exists a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{v_{1},\dots v_{n}\}\subseteq V
such that
\widehat{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}\,\cdot\mathrm{span}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{span}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 3.27
.

A parameter
(\bm{c},\bm{s})
is
specializable
if
c_{i},s_{i}\in\mathbf{A}
and

\cl\bigg(\frac{c_{i}}{c_{\tau(i)}}\bigg)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\tau(i)=i\\
(-1)^{\langle 2\rho_{\bullet}^{\vee},\alpha_{i}\rangle}&\text{if }\tau(i)\neq i\end{cases}

for each
i\in I_{\circ}

and
quasi-specializable
if it is contained in the orbit, regarding the action (
2
), of a specializable parameter.

In the case of a specializable parameter we have
U(\mathfrak{g})\supseteq\widehat{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}/(1-q)\widehat{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}=U(\mathfrak{k})
, cf.
[
22
,
Thm 4.9
]
or
[
18
,
Thm 10.8
]
.
We recall that specializable modules
V
allow us to form the
U(\mathfrak{k})
-module
\widetilde{V}:=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{A}v\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{C}
. In particular, for each finite-dimensional simple
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-module
V
the existence of a specializable basis is assured by
[
23
,
Lemma 7.3
]
.

Theorem 3.28
.

[
31
,
Thm 4.15
]

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
be a dominant weight, and let
(\bm{c},\bm{s})
be a quasi-specializable parameter. If the
\mathbf{U}
-module
L(\lambda)
decomposes as
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}L_{i}
into simple
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-modules, then the
U(\mathfrak{g})
module
L(\lambda)^{1}
decomposes as
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\widetilde{L}_{i}
into simple
U(\mathfrak{k})
modules.

Remark 3.29
.

From now on always assume that
(\bm{c},\bm{s})
is quasi-specializable.

Lemma 3.30
.

The bilinear pairing

h\circ\Xi_{V,W}:E^{V,W}\times E^{W,V}\to k

is non-degenerate.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show the claim for elementary spherical functions. Let
\Phi\in E^{V,W}
be nonzero elementary MSF and let
\Psi\in E^{W,V}
be as in Proposition

2.21
. Then it follows by Proposition

2.21
that

h\circ\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)=\frac{\tr(K_M|_V)\tr(K_M|_W)}{\tr(K_M)}.

Since the eigenvalues of
K
specialise
to 1, the above expression specialises to

\frac{\dim(V)\dim(W)}{\dim(M)},

which is nonzero. Consequently, we have

\Xi_{V,W}(\Phi,\Psi)\neq 0
.
∎

3.5.
Quantum commutative triples

We continue the study of matrix-spherical functions from Section
2.2
. In this section we study
\mathbf{B}
-modules which are multiplicity-free in every integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module. Following
[
44
]
, such modules give rise to a family of vector-valued orthogonal polynomials. We apply the techniques of Section
3.4
to show that the classification of such modules is independent of
q
.

Definition 3.31
.

A
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-module
V
is
integrable
if it occurs in an integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module.

This notion is equivalent to the integrablility of
[
50
, Def 3.3.4]
, as shown in
[
50
, Prop 4.3.1]
.
An analogous integrability definition exists for
\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{k})
-modules.
Recal from Definition
2.8
that
\Gamma
denotes the set of equivalence classes of
finite-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{B}
-modules that are contained
in the restriction of a
\mathbf{U}
-module.

Definition 3.32
.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
. The triple
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\gamma)
is a
quantum commutative triple
if

\forall\lambda\in X^{+}:\quad\dim(\Hom_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}(V(\gamma),L(\lambda))),\dim(\Hom_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}(L(\lambda),V(\gamma)))\leq 1.

If both inequalities are equalities and
L(\lambda)
is finite-dimensional,
\lambda
is said to be
\gamma
-
spherical
. Write
??for the set of dominant
\gamma
-spherical weights.

We write
??=E^{V(\gamma),V(\gamma^{\prime})}(L(\lambda))
(recall the notation ??from
Example
2.10
).

Lemma 3.33
.

Write
\gamma=\epsilon
for the trivial
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-module
(given by the counit
\epsilon
). If
I
is of finite type and

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
is either standard or real nonstandard, we have
X^{+}(\epsilon)=2L^{+}
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
25
, Theorem 3.2]
. Note that
the proofs also work for the case where
X\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
is
not spanned by the roots.
∎

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
and
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
be quantum commutative triples and let
0\neq\Phi\in E^{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}
, then
X^{+}(\gamma)\cap X^{+}(\gamma^{\prime})
is nonempty.
Moreover, if
\Phi\in E^{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(\lambda)
, then
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)\cap X^{+}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.11
(ii) we can
without loss of generality assume that
\Phi
can be written
in terms of matrix elements of a simple finite-dimensional module,
say of
L(\lambda)
.
By Lemma
2.11
(i), we can
then write
\Phi
in terms of elementary MSF. Therefore there
exist nonzero intertwiners in
\Hom_{\mathbf{B}}(L(\lambda),V(\gamma))
and
\Hom_{\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}(V(\gamma^{\prime}),L(\lambda))
.
This shows that
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)\cap X^{+}(\gamma^{\prime})
,
which is therefore nonempty.
∎

In case
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
and
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
,
each one-dimensional space

E^{\gamma}_{\mathbf{B}}(\lambda)
(for
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
) has a distinguished element.

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\gamma)
be a quantum commutative triple where
\mathbf{U}
is of finite type and let

\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
.
There exists a unique
elementary MSF
??\in E^{\gamma}(\lambda)

satisfying
\Phi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}(1)=1

(the identity endomorphism of
V(\gamma)
).
Furthermore,
the
(\Phi^{\lambda}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in X^{+}(\gamma)}

form a
k
-basis of
E^{\gamma}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.11
(i),
the space of elementary MSF for
L(\lambda)
is
one-dimensional, consequently the scaling requirement
fixes a unique MSF.

For linear independence observe that matrix elements for non-isomorphic
simple modules are linearly independent, hence so are elementary MSF
for non-isomorphic simple modules.

To see that these elementary MSF form a generating
system, we quote
[
27
, Proposition 6.3.4(b)]
to
see that every finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module
is also semisimple and then apply Lemma
2.11
(ii).
∎

Definition 3.36
.

Define the map
\cl:\Gamma\to\Gamma_{1}
, mapping
\gamma
to
\cl(\gamma)
, the
U(\mathfrak{k})
-type of the specialized module
\widetilde{V(\gamma)}:=V(\cl(\gamma))
.

This is well defined according to
[
31
,
Theorem 4.15
]
, based on
[
23
]
.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
(U(\mathfrak{g}),U(\mathfrak{k}),\cl(\gamma))
be a commutative triple, then
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\gamma)
is a commutative triple.

Proof.

In order to reach a contradiction, we assume the
existence of
\lambda\in X^{+}
such that

[L(\lambda):V(\gamma)]>1
. Applying

[
31
,
Theorem 4.15
]
and
[
23
,
Thm 7.9
]

yields
[L^{1}(\lambda):V(\cl(\gamma))]>1
,
a contradiction.
Therefore,
[L(\lambda):V(\gamma)]\leq 1
for all
\lambda\in X^{+}
.
∎

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
(\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{g}),\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{k}),\cl(\gamma))

be a commutative triple (in the classical sense),
and let
V(\gamma)
and
V(\gamma^{\prime})
be integrable
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-modules
with isomorphic specializations such that there
exists a
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
-spherical weight

\lambda\in X^{+}
.
Then,
V(\gamma)
and
V(\gamma^{\prime})
are isomorphic
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-modules.

Proof.

Let
m_{\gamma}
and
m_{\gamma^{\prime}}
denote the
multiplicities of
V(\gamma)
and
V(\gamma^{\prime})
in

L(\lambda)|_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}
respectively.
Using
[
31
,
Theorem 4.15
]
,
the decomposition

L(\lambda)^{1}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}m_{\gamma_{i}}V(\cl(\gamma_{i}))

corresponds to a decomposition
L(\lambda)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}m_{\gamma_{i}}V(\gamma_{i})
.
Thus, due to multiplicity-freeness
V(\gamma)
is isomorphic to
V(\gamma^{\prime})
.
∎

To show that there is a one-to-one correspondence
between quantum commutative triples and
classical commutative triples we can then use
Lemmas
3.38
and
3.37
.
The only missing step is then to show that if
(U(\mathfrak{g}),U(\mathfrak{k}),\cl(\gamma))

is commutative and
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
specialize to

\cl(\gamma)
that
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
are
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-types in an integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module.
We show this in Theorem
3.43
.

Notation 3.39
.

Write
\Gamma^{c}\subseteq\Gamma
for the equivalence
classes of modules for which
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\gamma)

is commutative.
Analogously define
\Gamma^{c}_{1}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}
.

In Theorem
3.43
we use techniques of

[
50
]
, as preparation we introduce a lattice and an abelian group.

Set
X^{\imath}
equal to

X/\{\lambda-\Theta(\lambda):\lambda\in X\}
, and

\overline{\,\cdot\,}:X\to X^{\imath}
equal to
the corresponding quotient map.
Set
Y^{\imath}:=\{h\in Y:\Theta(h)=h\}
and
\langle\,,\,\rangle:X^{\imath}\times Y^{\imath}
to be the pairing induced from
X\times Y
.
These abelian groups play the role of the coroot, and weight lattices for
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
.

Definition 3.40

(Weight module,
[
50
, §3.2]
)
.

A
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-module
M
is a
weight module
if it admits a decomposition

M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta}

such that

(i)

M_{\zeta}\subseteq\{m\in M|K_{h}m=q^{\langle h,\zeta\rangle}v\}
for all
h\in Y^{\imath}
;

(ii)

E_{j}M_{\zeta}\subseteq M_{\zeta+\overline{\alpha_{j}}}
,
F_{j}M_{\zeta}\subseteq M_{\zeta-\overline{\alpha_{j}}}
for all
j\in I_{\bullet}
;

(iii)

B_{k}M_{\zeta}\subseteq M_{\zeta-\overline{\alpha_{k}}}
for all
k\in I_{\circ}
.

Remark 3.41
.

Note that each
\mathbf{U}
-weight module
M
is a
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-weight module via

M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}V_{\zeta}\qquad V_{\zeta}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X,\,\overline{\lambda}=\zeta}V_{\lambda}.

According to
[
50
, §5.1]
, each irreducible
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-submodule
V(\gamma)\subseteq L(\lambda)
is itself canonically a
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-weight module.

Notation 3.42
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
denote the Levi subalgebra
generated by
E_{i},F_{i}
with
i\in I_{\bullet}
and

\mathbf{U}^{0}
.
We remark that
\mathbf{L}
is itself a
quantum group, and we let

\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{L}}(-\infty)
denote
its canonical basis, cf.
[
27
,
§25
]
.

For
n\geq 0,k\in I_{\circ}
and
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
let
\mathfrak{B}^{(n)}_{k,\zeta}
be the divided power as introduced in
[
50
, §4.1]
.

Theorem 3.43
.

The map
\cl:\Gamma^{c}\to\Gamma^{c}_{1}
is a bijection.

Proof.

Well-Definedness & Injectivity:

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\eta\in\Gamma^{c}

such that
V(\cl(\gamma))\cong V(\cl(\eta))
as

U(\mathfrak{k})
-modules.
This isomorphism induces a linear space isomorphism
preserving the weight spaces

V(\gamma)_{\xi}\to V(\eta)_{\xi}
, for each

\xi\in X^{\imath}
. Let
v\in V(\gamma)_{\xi}

and
w\in V(\eta)_{\xi}
be nonzero

\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-highest weight vectors with
identical specialization.
Let
(b_{j})_{j\in I_{\bullet}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I_{\bullet}}

and
(c_{i})_{i\in I_{\circ}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I_{\circ}}
satisfy

E_{j}^{(a_{j}+a)}w=F_{j}^{(b_{j}+1)}w=0\quad\text{and}\quad E_{j}^{(a_{j}+a)}v=F_{j}^{(b_{j}+1)}v=0\quad\text{for all}\quad j\in I_{\bullet},

and

\mathfrak{B}_{i,\xi}^{(c_{k}+1)}v=0,\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{i,\xi}^{(c_{k}+1)}w=0\quad\text{for all}\quad i\in I_{\circ}.

The existence of these integers is assured by
[
50
, Proposition 4.3.2.]
.
Given
\mu\in X^{+}
such that
\overline{\mu}=\xi
, we may, by replacing
\mu
by
\mu+\nu
where
\overline{\nu}=0
, assume that

\displaystyle\langle h_{j},\mu\rangle\geq b_{j},

\displaystyle\langle h_{i},\mu+\mathrm{wt}(b)\rangle\geq c_{i}

for all
j\in I_{\bullet}
,
i\in I_{\circ}
and

b\in B_{V(\gamma)}=B_{V(\eta)}=\{b\in\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{L}}(-\infty):bv\neq 0\}
.
Let
V^{\imath}(0,\mu)
be the module from
[
50
, §3.3]
. By
[
50
, Theorem 4.2.6]
, we have
inclusions

V(\gamma)\hookrightarrow V^{\imath}(0,\mu)\hookleftarrow V(\eta).

By
[
50
, Proposition 3.3.3]
we have
V^{\imath}(0,\mu)\cong L^{\imath}(0,\mu)\cong\mathbf{U}v_{0}\otimes v_{\mu}\cong L(\mu)
,
in particular
\cl:\Gamma^{c}\to\Gamma_{1}^{c}
is
injective and well-defined.

Surjectivity:

Let
V^{1}
be a simple integrable
U(\mathfrak{k})
-module.
Then there exists a dominant weight
\lambda\in X^{+}
,
such that
L(\lambda)^{1}=\oplus_{i=1}^{l}L_{i}
,
with
L_{1}=V^{1}
.
By
[
31
, Theorem 4.15]
, this decomposition
corresponds to
L(\lambda)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}L_{i}
.
Thus there exists a
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-module
V(\gamma)

specializing to
V^{1}
.∎

Definition 3.44
.

The
bottom of the
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
-well
is defined as

\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=\left\{\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)\cap X^{+}(\gamma^{\prime})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall\mu\in 2L^{+}:\quad\lambda-\mu\notin X^{+}(\gamma)\cap X^{+}(\gamma^{\prime})\right\}.

We write
??:=\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma,\gamma)
.
If
\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)
contains one element, we say that

\gamma
is an integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-type.

Corollary 3.45
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\gamma)
be a quantum commutative triple
then
X^{+}(\gamma)=\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)+2L^{+}
.
Furthermore, if
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}},\gamma^{\prime})
is a
quantum commutative triple with different parameters and
\cl(\gamma)=\cl(\gamma^{\prime})
, we have

X^{+}(\gamma)=X^{+}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Using Theorem
3.43
the branching rules are as in the classical commutative triple case, the classical result can be found in
[
37
,
Proposition 8.7
]
.
∎

3.6.
Bar Involutions and Symmetries

The goal of this section is to study spherical functions in light of bar-involutions.
The goal is to derive elementary symmetries. These symmetries will yield symmetries of the
matrix-valued weight
for the vector-valued polynomials that we construct later.

To ensure existence of the
quasi and universal
K
-matrix
, we assume that the parameters are
uniform
.

Definition 3.46
.

A parameter
(\bm{c},\bm{s})\in(k^{\times})^{I_{\circ}}\times k^{I_{\circ}}
is called
uniform
if

(3)

c_{\tau(i)}=(-1)^{\langle 2\rho^{\vee}_{\bullet},\alpha_{i}\rangle}q^{\alpha_{i}\cdot(\Theta(\alpha_{i})-2\rho_{\bullet})}\overline{c_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{i}=\overline{s_{i}}\quad\text{for each}\quad i\in I_{\circ}.

Notation 3.47
.

Let
??\in W
denote the longest element.

Let
??:I\to I
denote the diagram involution with
w_{0}(\alpha_{i})=-\alpha_{\tau_{0}(i)}
for each
i\in I_{\circ}
.
Diagram automorphisms
\eta:I\to I
act on
\mathbf{U}
by

E_{i}\mapsto E_{\eta(i)},\quad F_{i}\mapsto F_{\eta(i)},\quad K_{i}\mapsto K_{\eta(i)},\qquad\text{for each }i\in I.

We set
\sigma:=\tau\circ\tau_{0}
(where
\tau
is used
to define
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
).

During the remainder of this section we furthermore
assume
c_{\sigma(i)}=c_{i}
and
s_{\sigma(i)}=s_{i}
,
for each
i\in I_{\circ}
.
When these conditions are fulfilled,

\sigma
restricts to an involutive automorphism of

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
and thus acts on
\Gamma
.
Let
\mathscr{U}
denote an algebraic completion of
\mathbf{U}
,
cf.
[
6
,
§3
]
.

Definition 3.48

(Anti-linear)
.

Let
V
be a
k
-vector space. An anti-linear map
f:V\to V
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear endomorphism with
q^{s}\mapsto q^{-s}
for each
s\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Theorem 3.49

(
[
6
]
[
7
]
)
.

The following three statements hold

(i)

There exists a unique anti-linear
involution
??:\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\to\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}

such that
\psi^{\iota}|_{\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}}=??|_{\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}}

and

B_{i}\mapsto B_{i},\qquad K_{h}\mapsto K_{-h},\qquad\text{where}\qquad i\in I_{\circ},h\in Y_{\Theta}.

(ii)

There exists an up to scalar multiple unique element

??\in{\mathscr{U}}^{+}
such that

\psi^{\iota}(x)\Upsilon=\Upsilon\overline{x},\qquad\text{where}\qquad x\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}.

We refer to
\Upsilon
as the
quasi
K
-matrix.

(iii)

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
there exists a

\xi\in\mathrm{Hom}(X,k)
such that the operator

??=\Upsilon\xi T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}T_{w_{0}}^{-1}

satisfies

\mathcal{K}xv=\sigma(x)\mathcal{K}v,\qquad\text{where}\qquad x\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},v\in L(\lambda),\lambda\in X^{+}.

We refer to
\mathcal{K}
as the
universal
K
-matrix.

Lemma 3.50
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
and let

p:L(\lambda)\to V(\gamma)
and
i:V(\gamma)\to L(\lambda)
be

\mathbf{B}
-linear maps satisfying
p\circ i=id_{V(\gamma)}
.

Then
p\circ\mathcal{K}:L(\lambda)\to V(\sigma(\gamma))
and

\mathcal{K}^{-1}\circ i:V(\sigma(\gamma))\to L(\lambda)
are
also
\mathbf{B}
-linear maps satisfying
p\circ\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}^{-1}\circ i=\id_{V(\gamma)}
.

Proof.

Let
v\in L(\lambda)
and
x\in\mathbf{B}
, then

p(\mathcal{K}xv)=p(\sigma(x)\mathcal{K}v)=\sigma(x)p(\mathcal{K}v)

by Theorem
3.49
(iii).
The other claim follows analogously.
∎

Recall that the dual of a left
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
is a right
\mathbf{U}
-module
M^{\ast}
where the action is defined in Definition
2.3
.

Definition 3.51

(
\imath
bar invariant)
.

We say a vector
v\in L(\lambda)
is
\imath
bar invariant if
\Upsilon\overline{v}=v
and we say a vector
f\in L(\lambda)^{\ast}
is
\imath
bar invariant if
\overline{f}\Upsilon^{-1}=f
.

In Proposition
3.53
, we show the existence of a
\imath
bar invariant basis that is independent of embedding.
We first need a preliminary result. Recall the elements
\mathfrak{B}^{(\langle h_{j},\lambda\rangle)}_{k,\overline{\lambda}}
from Notation
3.42
.

Lemma 3.52
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
be a quantum commutative triple. Then there exists a, up to scalar multiple, unique vector
v\in V(\gamma)
with

(4)

0\neq v\in L(\lambda)_{\overline{\lambda}},\qquad E_{j}v=0=F_{j}^{(\langle h_{j},\lambda\rangle+1)}v,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{B}^{(\langle h_{j},\lambda\rangle+1)}_{k,\overline{\lambda}}v=0,

for all
j\in I_{\bullet}
and
k\in I_{\circ}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi_{\gamma}:L(\lambda)\to V(\gamma)
be the
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-equivariant projection. As
v_{\lambda}
is a cyclic vector for
L(\lambda)
as a
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-module, cf.
[
49
, Lem 6.2.1]
, existence of the vector
v
follows from
[
50
, Theorem 4.2.6]
. Uniqueness, up to scalar, then follows from
\dim\big(\Hom_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}(L(\lambda),V(\gamma)\big)=1
, since any
v\in V(\gamma)
, with (
4
) defines a
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-linear map

v_{\lambda}\mapsto v
, again by
[
50
, Theorem 4.2.6]
.
∎

For Proposition
3.53
, recall the notion of a specializable basis as introduced in Definition
3.26
.

Proposition 3.53
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
be a quantum commutative triple. There exist bases
(v_{j})_{j=1,\dots,n}\subseteq V(\gamma)
and

(f_{j})_{j=1,\dots,n}\subseteq V(\gamma)^{*}
dual to each other such that
for every
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
there are

\mathbf{B}
-linear maps
i:V(\gamma)\to L(\lambda)

and
p:L(\lambda)\to V(\gamma)
such that
p\circ i=\id_{V(\gamma)}

and such that all
i(v_{j})
and
f_{j}\circ p
(
j=1,\dots,n
) are

\imath
bar-invariant.

Proof.

Fix a
\gamma
-spherial weight
\lambda^{\prime}\in X^{+}(\gamma)
and write
v=\pi_{\gamma}(v_{\lambda^{\prime}})\in V(\gamma)
for the vector from Lemma
3.52
. Let
\{b_{1}v,\dots,b_{n}v\}
be a specializable basis of
V(\gamma)
with
b_{j}\in\mathbf{B}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
. For
1\leq j\leq n
let
v_{j}=(b_{j}+\psi^{\imath}(b_{j}))v
. Since
\cl\circ\psi^{\iota}=\cl
, it follows that
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
is a specializable basis of
V(\gamma).

Next, consider a
\gamma
-spherial weight
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
and the, up to scalar multiple unique,
\mathbf{B}
-linear map
i:V(\gamma)\to L(\lambda)
.
According to
[
3
,
Proposition 4.2.1 (a), Proposition 4.2.2
]
, we have
\cl(\Upsilon)=1
.
Moreover, we note that

\cl\circ\overline{\,\cdot\,}=\cl
.
Together this yields the equality

\widetilde{\Upsilon\overline{i(V(\gamma))}}=\widetilde{i(V(\gamma))},

from which it follows that

\Upsilon\overline{V(\gamma)}=V(\gamma)
by multiplicity-freeness.
Thus

\cl\circ\overline{\,\cdot\,}:i(V(\gamma))\to i(V(\gamma)).

By
\mathbf{B}
-linearity
p(\Upsilon\overline{i(v)})
