defines another vector satisfying the properties of Lemma
3.52
, thus by multiplying with a scalar, we can fix
i
so that
\Upsilon\overline{i(v)}=i(v)
.
Let
\tilde{v}_{j}=i(v_{j})=i(b_{j}+\psi^{\imath}(b_{j}))v
.
Because
\Upsilon\overline{i(v)}=i(v)
and
\psi^{\imath}(b_{j}+\psi^{\imath}(b_{j}))=b_{j}+\psi^{\imath}(b_{j})
, it follows that the vectors
\{\tilde{v_{1}},\dots,\tilde{v_{n}}\}
are
\imath
bar invariant.

Let
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in V(\gamma)^{*}
be the dual basis of
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
.
For
1\leq j\leq n
let
\tilde{f}_{j}=f_{j}\circ p
, then it holds that

\displaystyle(\Upsilon^{-1}\triangleright\overline{\tilde{f}_{k}})(\tilde{v}_{j})=\overline{\tilde{f}_{k}}(\Upsilon^{-1}\tilde{v}_{j})=\overline{\tilde{f}_{k}(\overline{\Upsilon^{-1}\tilde{v}_{j}})}=\overline{\tilde{f}_{k}(\overline{\overline{\tilde{v}_{j}}})}=\overline{f_{k}(v_{j})}=\overline{\delta_{k,j}}=\delta_{k,j},

which shows
\overline{\tilde{f}_{k}}\Upsilon^{-1}=\tilde{f}_{k}
. Write
L(\lambda)=V(\gamma)\bigoplus V(\gamma)^{\perp}
, with respect to the decomposition of
L(\lambda)
as a semisimple
\mathbf{B}
-module, which is guaranteed by Definition
3.32
. As
\Upsilon\overline{V(\gamma)}=V(\gamma)
, it follows that
\Upsilon\overline{V(\gamma)^{\perp}}=V(\gamma)^{\perp}
. Let
w\in V(\gamma)^{\perp}
. Then

\Upsilon^{-1}\triangleright\overline{\tilde{f}_{k}}(w)=f_{k}(p(\underbrace{\Upsilon\overline{w}}_{\in V(\gamma)^{\perp}})=f_{k}(0)=0.

Thus,
\Upsilon^{-1}\triangleright\overline{\tilde{f}_{k}}=\tilde{f}_{k}
.
∎

Recall from Definition
3.13
the algebra
\mathcal{A}
generated by finite-dimensional matrix elements

Definition 3.54
.

Denote by
??:\mathcal{A}\to k[X]
the composition of
\Res_{\mathbf{U}^{0}}:\mathcal{A}\to\Hom(\mathbf{U}^{0},k)
with
\Hom(\mathbf{U}^{0},k)\cong k[X]
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}
-module.
If
f\in M^{*}
, and
v\in M
is written as
v=\sum_{\mu\in X}v_{\mu}
for
v_{\mu}\in M_{\mu}
,
then

\Res(c_{f,v}^M)=\sum_{\mu\in X}f(v_{\mu})e^{\mu}.

Notation 3.55
.

Define the following action of
Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
on
k[X]
(or even
on
k[[X]]
):

h\triangleright e^{\lambda}:=q^{\langle h,\lambda\rangle}e^{\lambda}.

This is an action by
k
-algebra automorphism.

Moreover, denote
{}^{0}:k[X]\to k[X]
the anti-linear
involution mapping
e^{\lambda}\mapsto e^{\lambda}
for all

\lambda\in X
.

Lemma 3.56
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
,
v\in L(\lambda)
and
f\in L(\lambda)^{*}
.
Then

c_{\overline{f},\overline{v}}^{\lambda}(K_{h})=\overline{f(K_{-h}v)}.

Proof.

We have

c_{\overline{f},\overline{v}}^{\lambda}(K_{h})=\overline{f}(K_{h}\overline{v}).

By definition of
\overline{f}
, this equals

=\overline{f(\overline{K_{h}\overline{v}})}=\overline{f(K_{-h}v)}.\qed

Lemma 3.57
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
, and let
v\in L(\lambda),f\in L(\lambda)^{*}

be
\imath
bar-invariant.
Then

c^{\lambda}_{\overline{f},\overline{v}}(K_{h})=c^{\lambda}_{f\circ T_{w_{\bullet}}\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{-1}T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}\circ v}(K_{w_{0}h}).

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{K}=\Upsilon\xi T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}T_{w_{0}}^{-1}
.
This implies that

\Upsilon=\mathcal{K}T_{w_{0}}T_{w_{\bullet}}\xi^{-1}=\mathcal{K}T_{w_{0}}(w_{\bullet}\xi)^{-1}T_{w_{\bullet}}.

Moreover, since
\psi^{\iota}
and
\overline{\cdot}
coincide on

\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
,
\Upsilon
commutes with
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
and hence also
with its closure.
This closure contains
T_{w_{\bullet}}
, so that

\Upsilon=T_{w_{\bullet}}\Upsilon T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}=T_{w_{\bullet}}\mathcal{K}T_{w_{0}}(w_{\bullet}\xi)^{-1}.

This allows us to conclude

\displaystyle c^{\lambda}_{\overline{f},\overline{v}}(K_{h})

\displaystyle=c^{\lambda}_{f\Upsilon,\Upsilon^{-1}v}(K_{h})=f(\Upsilon K_{h}\Upsilon^{-1}v)

\displaystyle=f(T_{w_{\bullet}}\mathcal{K}T_{w_{0}}(w_{\bullet}\xi)^{-1}K_{h}(w_{\bullet}\xi)T_{w_{0}}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{-1}T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}v)

\displaystyle=f(T_{w_{\bullet}}\mathcal{K}T_{w_{0}}K_{h}T_{w_{0}}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{-1}T_{w_{\bullet}}v)

\displaystyle=f(T_{w_{\bullet}}\mathcal{K}K_{w_{0}h}\mathcal{K}^{-1}T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}v)

\displaystyle=c^{\lambda}_{f\circ T_{w_{\bullet}}\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{-1}T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}v}(K_{w_{0}h}).\qed

Lemma 3.58
.

Let
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
be coideal subalgebras derived
from the same admissible pair,
where
\bm{c},\bm{d}
are related as
follows:

\begin{cases}c_{i}=d_{i}&i=\tau(i)\text{ or }\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})=0\\
c_{i}c_{\tau(i)}=d_{i}d_{\tau(i)}&i\neq\tau(i)\text{ and }\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0\end{cases}.

Let
f\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}}
and write
P:=(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f)
.
We have
P\in k[2L]^{??}
.

In particular this implies
P=\tau_{0}(\overline{P})

and
w_{\bullet}P=P
.

Proof.

The first claim follows from

[
25
, Corollary 5.4]
and

[
31
, Theorem 5.15]
).

More precisely, in Letzter’s language, the restriction of a
ZSF for
(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}_{\theta,\bm{s},\bm{d}})

is
W_{\Sigma}
-invariant if
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\chi_{\bm{c}}(\mathbf{B}_{\theta,\bm{s}^{\prime},\bm{c}^{2}\bm{d}})

for
\bm{s}^{\prime}
an allowed non-standard parameter and

c_{i}\neq 1
implying that
\tau(i)\neq i
and
\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0
.
In particular, we have

\chi_{\bm{c}}(B_{i,\theta,s_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}^{2}d_{i}})=c_{i}q^{\frac{1}{2}\langle\rho,\Theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{i}\rangle}\ad(K_{\rho})(B_{\theta,i,s^{\prime\prime}_{i},\frac{c_{i}d_{i}}{c_{\tau(i)}}})

for
s^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\frac{s^{\prime}_{i}}{c_{i}q^{\frac{1}{2}\langle\rho,\Theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{i}\rangle}}
.
Note that
s_{i}^{\prime}\neq 0
is only allowed for cases when
c_{i}=1
and

\alpha_{i}=-\Theta(\alpha_{i})
, so that
s^{\prime\prime}_{i}=s^{\prime}_{i}
.
This shows in particular that we can rewrite the condition
on
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
as
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\ad(K_{\rho})(\mathbf{B}_{\theta,\bm{s}^{\prime},\frac{\bm{c}\bm{d}}{\tau(\bm{d})}})
,
or alternatively that
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\ad(K_{\rho})(\mathbf{B}_{\theta,\bm{s}^{\prime},\bm{d}^{\prime}})
where

\begin{cases}d_{i}=d_{i}^{\prime}&i=\tau(i)\text{ or }\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})=0\\
d_{i}d_{\tau(i)}=d_{i}^{\prime}d_{\tau(i)}^{\prime}&i\neq\tau(i)\text{ and }\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0.\end{cases}

To translate this statement to our notations we assume that there
is an involutive algebra automorphism and coalgebra antiautomorphism

\phi
of
\mathbf{U}
mapping
\phi(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}})=\mathbf{B}_{\theta,\bm{Y}\bm{s},\bm{X}\bm{c}}

for some structural constants
\bm{X},\bm{Y}
that only depend on

I_{\bullet},\tau
.
Assume furthermore that
\phi(K_{h})=K_{-h}
.

Let now
f\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}}
,
then
P=\Res(f\triangleleft K_{-\rho})
where

f\triangleleft K_{-\rho}\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\ad(K_{\rho})(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}),\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}}
.
Then
(f\triangleleft K_{-\rho})\circ\phi^{-1}
is a ZSF for
\ad(K_{\rho})(\mathbf{B}_{\theta,\bm{Y}\bm{s},\bm{X}\bm{c}})
and
\mathbf{B}_{\theta,\bm{Y}\bm{t},\bm{X}\bm{d}}

in Letzter’s parlance.
The conditions that
\bm{c},\bm{d}
satisfy can be rewritten
as follows

\begin{cases}c_{i}X_{i}=d_{i}X_{i}&i=\tau(i)\text{ or }\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})=0\\
c_{i}c_{\tau(i)}X_{i}X_{\tau(i)}=d_{i}d_{\tau(i)}X_{i}X_{\tau(i)}&i\neq\tau(i)\text{ and }\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0,\end{cases}

which by the previous argument shows that

\Res((f\triangleleft K_{-\rho})\circ\phi^{-1})=\overline{P}

is invariant under
W_{\Sigma}
, hence so is

P
.

To prove the two conclusions, we recall that
W_{\Sigma}

and its actions are obtained from the group
??
, the
subgroup of elements of
W
that commute with
\Theta
,
and its normal subgroup
W_{\bullet}
(
[
5
, §2.9]
).
In particular,
P
is invariant under

W_{\Theta}
.

Denote by
R_{\bullet}
the root system generated by the simple roots
\alpha_{j}
with
j\in I_{\bullet}
.
Since
\Theta
leaves
R_{\bullet}
invariant,
W_{\bullet}
and
in particular
w_{\bullet}
commutes with
\Theta
.
Moreover, we have
\Theta w_{0}\Theta=w_{\bullet}\tau w_{0}w_{\bullet}\tau
.
Since
\tau
is a diagram morphism that permutes the elements of
I_{\bullet}
,
it commutes with
w_{\bullet}
. Since it also permutes the elements of
I
, it
commutes with
w_{0}
, so we have

\Theta w_{0}\Theta=w_{\bullet}\tau w_{0}\tau w_{\bullet}=w_{\bullet}w_{0}w_{\bullet}.

Since
w_{0}
can be written as the composition of
-1
with the
diagram automorphism
\tau_{0}
(that is the nontrivial
diagram automorphism if
\mathbf{U}
is of type
A_{n},D_{n}
(
n
odd),
or
E_{6}
), we have

\Theta w_{0}\Theta=w_{0}\tau_{0}(w_{\bullet})w_{\bullet}.

Using
[
5
]
, it can be checked that in every case
\tau_{0}
permutes
I_{\bullet}
,
and as such
\tau_{0}(w_{\bullet})=w_{\bullet}
.
Consequently, also
w_{0}
commutes with
\Theta
and hence also
leaves
P
invariant.
∎

Recall the involutions
{}^{\circ}:k[2L]\to k[2L]
and
\sigma=\tau\circ\tau_{0}:\mathbf{U}\to\mathbf{U}
from Notation
3.55
and the beginning of Section
3.6
, respectively.

Definition 3.59
.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in X^{+}(\gamma)
, then using Definition
2.16
we define the
matrix weight

??:=(-\rho)\triangleright\Res\big(\Xi_\gamma(\Phi^\lambda_\gamma,\Phi^{\lambda'}_\gamma)\big).

Lemma 3.60
.

Let
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in X^{+}(\gamma)
, then

(H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma})^{\circ}=H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\sigma(\gamma)}
.

Proof.

Choose
\imath
bar-invariant dual bases
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V(\gamma)

and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in V(\gamma)^{*}
and linear maps

p:L(\lambda)\to V(\gamma),p^{\prime}:L(\lambda^{\prime})\to V(\gamma)
and

i:V(\gamma)\to L(\lambda),i^{\prime}:V(\gamma)\to L(\lambda^{\prime})
as in
Proposition
3.53
(for
\lambda
and

\lambda^{\prime}
).
Then
p,i
(resp.
p^{\prime},i^{\prime}
) can be used to define the elementary
MSF
\Phi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}
(resp.
\Phi^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}
).
In particular, we have

\Phi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}c^{\lambda}_{f_{j}\circ p,i(v_{k})}v_{j}\otimes f_{k}.

This shows that for
h\in Y
we have

(5)

H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}(h)=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}c^{\lambda}_{f_{j}\circ p,i(v_{k})}(K_{h-\rho})c^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{f_{k}\circ p^{\prime},i^{\prime}(v_{j})}(K_{-h-\rho}).

We apply Lemma
3.56
to obtain

(6)

\eqref{eq:3591}=\quantity(\sum_{j,k=1}^n c^\lambda_{\overline{f_j\circ p},
\overline{i(v_k)}}(K_{-h+\rho})
c^{\lambda'}_{\overline{f_k\circ p'},\overline{i'(v_j)}}(K_{h+\rho}))^{-}.

Since by choice, the elements
f_{j}\circ p,i(v_{j})
(
j=1,\dots,n
,
also the primed versions) are
\imath
bar-invariant, we can apply
Lemma
3.57
and obtain

(7)

\eqref{eq:3592}=\quantity(\sum_{j,k=1}^n c^\lambda_{f_j\circ p\circ T_{w_\bullet}\mathcal{K},
\mathcal{K}^{-1}T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}i(v_k)}(K_{-w_0h-\rho})
c^{\lambda'}_{f_k\circ p'\circ T_{w_\bullet}\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{-1}T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}i'(v_j)}(K_{w_0h-\rho}))^{-}.

Note that
p,i,p^{\prime},i^{\prime}
are
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-linear, so they all commute with

T_{w_{\bullet}}
.
Furthermore,
(T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}v_{j})_{j=1,\dots,n}
and

(f_{j}T_{w_{\bullet}})_{j=1,\dots,n}
are dual bases of
V(\gamma)
and its
dual, and we can replace them with our original
(v_{j})_{j=1,\dots,n}

and
(f_{j})_{j=1,\dots,n}
, so that the above reads

(8)

\eqref{eq:3593}=\quantity(\sum_{j,k=1}^n c^\lambda_{f_j\circ p\circ\mathcal{K},
\mathcal{K}^{-1}i(v_k)}(K_{-w_0h-\rho})
c^{\lambda'}_{f_k\circ p'\circ\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{-1}i'(v_j)}(K_{w_0h-\rho}))^{-}.

We can now apply Lemma
3.50
to

(p,i)
and
(p^{\prime},i^{\prime})
, which shows that

(9)

\eqref{eq:3594}=\overline{H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\sigma(\gamma)}(-w_{0}h)}.

By Lemma
3.58
we have

(10)

\eqref{eq:3595}=\overline{H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\sigma(\gamma)}(-h)}=\quantity(H^{\lambda,\lambda'}_{\sigma(\gamma)})^{\circ}(h).\qed

This allows us to prove the following properties of the matrix weight from Definition
3.59
:

Proposition 3.61
.

Let
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in X^{+}(\gamma)
.

(i)

We have

\overline{H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}}=H^{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}_{\gamma}.

(ii)

We have

\overline{H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}}=\tau_{0}H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}.

(iii)

We have

H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}=\tau H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}.

(iv)

We have

\quantity(H^{\lambda,\lambda'}_\gamma)^{\circ}=H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\sigma(\gamma)}

(v)

In case
\gamma\cong\sigma(\gamma)
, we conclude that

H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}
is invariant under
\cdot^{\circ}
.

Proof.

(i)

We have

\displaystyle H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}(h)

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi^\lambda_\gamma(K_{h-\rho})
\Phi^{\lambda'}_\gamma(K_{-h-\rho}))

\displaystyle=\tr(\Phi^{\lambda'}_\gamma(K_{-h-\rho})
\Phi^{\lambda}_\gamma(K_{h-\rho}))

\displaystyle=H^{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}_{\gamma}(-h).

(ii)

We have

H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}(-h)=H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}(w_{0}\tau_{0}h)=H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}(\tau_{0}h)

by Lemma
3.58
.

(iii)

We have
\tau=-w_{\bullet}\Theta
.
By Lemma
3.58
,
H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}
is
supported in
2L
, which is
\Theta
-anti-invariant, hence

\tau H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}=\overline{w_{\bullet}\Theta H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}}=\overline{w_{\bullet}\overline{H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}}}=H^{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}_{\gamma}

by Lemma
3.58
.

(iv)

This is the statement of Lemma
3.60
.

(v)

Clear.

∎

Remark 3.62
.

The invariance of Proposition
3.61
(iv) can be observed in the existing literature and in the examples of this paper, cf. Section
7.1
,
7.2
,
[
30
, Theorem 5.7. & (5.7)]
and
[
2
, §6.8]
.

Conjecture 3.63
.

For each multiplicity free triple
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
the twisted module
V(\gamma)^{\sigma}
is isomorphic to
V(\gamma).

Recall that a Satake diagram is said to be of
split type
if
I_{\bullet}=\emptyset
and
\tau=\mathrm{id}
.

Remark 3.64
.

It should be noted that the case

\sigma\neq\mathrm{id}
is rather sparse.
This inequality occurs in the cases

\mathsf{AI}
,
\mathsf{AII}
,
\mathsf{EI}
,

\mathsf{EIV}
and may occur for

\mathsf{DI}_{n,p}
with
p
odd, cf.
[
5
,
p 32-33
]
. Here
\mathsf{AI}
,
\mathsf{EI}
are split and
\mathsf{DI}
might be split, and hence in these cases we can apply Proposition
3.66
.
However, in the remaining cases, it still seems that
V(\gamma)\cong V(\gamma)^{\sigma}
.
One can check this for the multiplicity free triples
for
\mathsf{AII}
and
\mathsf{EIV}
listed in
[
37
,
Table 2
]
.

Remark 3.65
.

It is generally not true that
V(\gamma)^{\sigma}\cong V(\gamma)
. For an explicit counterexample, take the rank one symmetric pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})=(\mathfrak{sl}_{4},\mathfrak{sp}_{4})
of type
\mathsf{AII}
. Given that
\mathfrak{k}
is semisimple it follows from
[
23
, 7.10]
that the isomorphism
V(\gamma)^{\sigma}\cong V(\gamma)
holds if and only if
V(\gamma)^{\sigma}\cong V(\gamma)
at
q=1
. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
with basis
\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\}
Note that
\{h_{1},h_{3}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{k}
and that
\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{h_{1},h_{3}\}=\mathfrak{t}
is a Cartan subalgebra for
\mathfrak{k}
. We note
\sigma(h_{1})=h_{3}
. For a dominant integral weight
\mu
of
\mathfrak{t}
, we have
L(\mu)\cong L(\mu)^{\sigma}
, precisely when
\mu
is of the form
\mu=a(\omega_{1}^{\vee}+\omega_{3}^{\vee})
for some
a\geq 0
. A similar result is obtained in higher rank and for type
\mathsf{EIV}
.

In Proposition
3.66
we show that Conjecture
3.63
holds for split types, even when
\sigma\neq\mathrm{id}_{I}
.

Proposition 3.66
.

Let
(I_{\bullet},\tau)
be of split type and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, then
V(\gamma)^{\sigma}\cong V(\gamma)
.

Proof.

We prove the proposition using a specialization argument. Note that the classical limit of the universal
K
-matrix and the longest element of the Wang–Zhang braid group operator both specialize to
T_{w_{0}}
, cf.
[
46
, Definition 10.4]

[
3
,
Proposition 4.2.1 (a), Proposition 4.2.2
]
. Thus, it suffices to prove that the Wang–Zhang braid group operator
\mathbf{T}_{w_{0}}
preserves the modules
V(\gamma)\subseteq L(\lambda).
Let
w_{0}=s_{1}\dots s_{n}
be a reduced expression of
w_{0}\in W_{\Sigma}=W
, then by
[
46
, Thm 10.6]

\mathbf{T}_{w_{0}}=\mathbf{T}_{s_{1}}\dots\mathbf{T}_{s_{n}}
. By the explicit expressions of
\mathbf{T}_{s_{i}}
given in
[
47
, §3.1.]
, each operator
\mathbf{T}_{s_{i}}
preserves the module
V(\gamma).

Thus,
\mathbf{T}_{w_{0}}:V(\gamma)\to V(\gamma)
and as a result
V(\gamma)\cong V(\gamma)^{\sigma}
.
∎

Remark 3.67
.

With use of
\Ad(\eta)
, the symmetries of
this section can be extended to all parameters
that are in the
\mathrm{Hom}(X,k)
orbit of
uniform parameters. In particular, this shows
that these symmetries are present for each
standard parameter in the reduced case.

4.
Cartan Decomposition

The goal of this section is to show the existence of
a radial component decomposition for suitable
elements of
\mathbf{U}
using a Cartan decomposition of

\mathbf{U}
.

For reasons elaborated on later in Remark
4.2

this means that we need to assume that
I
is of finite type
from now on, or at least that all rank-1 subdiagrams of
I
satisfy the
Lemma
4.1
(iii).

More concretely, in this section we show that
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
lies
Zariski-dense in
\mathbf{U}
.
This implies that MSF for
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
are uniquely
determined by their restrictions to
??
, the subalgebra
generated by the
(K_{h})_{h\in Y}
.
Since the MSF are sums
of matrix elements, they are already determined by their restrictions to
\left\{K_{h}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}h\in Y\right\}
.

In case
Y=Y^{\Theta}\oplus Y^{-\Theta}
, these results can be
strengthened by replacing
\mathbf{U}^{0}
and
Y
by
\mathbf{U}^{0}_{-\Theta}
and

Y^{-\Theta}
,
where
\mathbf{U}^{0}_{-\Theta}\leq\mathbf{U}^{0}
is the group algebra of
Y^{-\Theta}
(the

\Theta
-antiinvariants).

Recall from
[
18
,
Prop 6.3
]
the quantum Iwasawa decomposition of
\mathbf{U}

(11)

\mathbf{U}^{\prime}\cong\mathbf{V}_{\bullet}^{+}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{\Theta}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},

where under certain conditions
\mathbf{U}^{\prime}=\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{U}_{\Theta}^{\prime}
is the group
algebra of a complement of
Y^{\Theta}
.
Here
\mathbf{V}_{\bullet}^{+}
is the subalgebra of

\mathbf{U}^{+}
generated by
\mathrm{ad}(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})

with
i\in I_{\circ}
and
\mathbf{U}^{0}_{I_{\circ},\tau}=k[K_{i}^{\pm}:i\in I_{\circ,\tau}]

where
I_{\circ,\tau}\subseteq I_{\circ}
contains precisely one representative for each
\tau
-orbit of
I_{\circ}
.

If we let go of a need for unique representation, the same proofs show
that

(12)

\mathbf{U}\cong\mathbf{V}_{\bullet}^{+}\otimes\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}.

In order to show the desired decomposition, it thus remains to show
that
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
intersects densely with

\mathbf{V}_{\bullet}^{+}\mathbf{U}^{0}
.

To that end, we have a closer look at
\bm{V}_{\bullet}^{+}
and
in particular study the
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-modules (via the adjoint action)
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})
and
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
.

4.1.
The
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-modules
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})
,
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
and their product

Recall from Definition
2.3
the adjoint action of
\mathbf{U}
on itself.
The goal of this section is to determine the
q
-commutation relations between elements of

\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})

and
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
.
Our approach is to study the
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-modules
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})
,
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
, and their product as an
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-module.
As a preliminary step, we show that
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})
and
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
are simple modules corresponding to (quasi-)minuscule weights. Recall that a weight
\nu
is minuscule if the weights of
L(\lambda)
are contained in a single Weyl group orbit, and quasi-minuscule possibly with the addition of the zero-weight space.

Lemma 4.1
.

[
18
, Lemma 3.5]

Let
i\in I_{\circ}
.

(i)

The subspace
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})
of
\mathbf{U}^{+}
is a finite dimensional, irreducible
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-
submodule of
\mathbf{U}
with highest weight vector
T_{w_{\bullet}}(E_{i})
and lowest weight vector
E_{i}
.

(ii)

The subspace
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
of
S(\mathbf{U}^{-})
is a finite dimensional, irreducible

\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-submodule of
\mathbf{U}
with highest weight vector
F_{i}K_{i}
and lowest
weight vector
T^{-1}_{w_{\bullet}}(F_{i}K_{i})
.

(iii)

The weights corresponding to the simple
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-modules of
(i)
and
(ii)
are quasi-minuscule if the rank one diagram corresponding to
i
is of type
\mathsf{BII}
, else the weight is minuscule.

Proof.

Statement
(i)
and
(ii)
are the content of
[
18
, Lemma 3.5]
.

Consider the irreducible
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-module
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})
. The
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-weight of this module is determined entirely by the rank-one diagram associated with the index
i\in I_{\circ}
. The rank one cases where
I_{\bullet}
is empty are trivial. The remaining rank-one cases require further examination.

In case
\mathsf{AII_{3}}
:

For
j=1,3
we have

\langle h_{j},w_{\bullet}\alpha_{2}\rangle=-\langle h_{\tau(j)},\alpha_{2}\rangle=1.

The Dynkin type of
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is
\mathsf{A_{1}\times A_{1}}.

As such
w_{\bullet}\alpha_{1}
is identified with the
\mathsf{A_{1}}\times\mathsf{A_{1}}
-weight
\omega_{1}+\omega_{3}
, which is minuscule.

In case
\mathsf{AIV}_{n}
:

For
j\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
and
i\in\{1,n\}
we have

\langle h_{j},w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}\rangle=-\langle h_{n-j},\alpha_{i}\rangle=\delta_{|i-n+j|,1}

The Dynkin type of
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is
\mathsf{A}_{n-2}
. As such
w_{\bullet}\alpha_{1}
is identified with a fundamental weight for
\mathsf{A}_{n-2}
, all of which are minuscule.

In case
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
:

Let
i=1
and
j\in\{2,\dots,n\}
. Then

\langle h_{j},w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}\rangle=-\langle h_{\tau(j)},\alpha_{i}\rangle=\delta_{2,j}.

The Dynkin type of
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is
\mathsf{B}_{n-1}
. As such
w_{\bullet}\alpha_{1}
is identified with the first fundamental weight
\omega_{2}
for
\mathsf{B}_{n-1}
, which is quasi-minuscule.

In case
\mathsf{CII}_{n}
:

Let
i=2
and
j\in\{1,3,\dots,n\}
. Then

\langle h_{j},w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}\rangle=-\langle h_{\tau(j)},\alpha_{i}\rangle=\delta_{1,j}+\delta_{3,j}.

The Dynkin type of
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is
\mathsf{A}_{1}\times\mathsf{C}_{n-2}
. As such
w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}
is identified with the miniscule
\mathsf{A}_{1}\times\mathsf{C}_{n-2}
-weight
\omega_{1}+\omega_{3}
.

In case
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
:

Let
i=1
and
j\in\{2,\dots,n\}
. Then

\langle h_{j},w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}\rangle=-\langle h_{\tau(j)},\alpha_{i}\rangle=\delta_{2,j}.

The Dynkin type of
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is
\mathsf{D_{n-1}}
. As such
w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}
is identified with the the first fundamental weight
\omega_{2}
of
\mathsf{D}_{n-1}
, which is minuscule.

In case
\mathsf{FII}
:

Let
i=4
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then

\langle h_{j},w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}\rangle=-\langle h_{j},\alpha_{i}\rangle=\delta_{3,j}.

The Dynkin type of
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is
\mathsf{B_{3}}
. As such
w_{\bullet}\alpha_{i}
is identified the last fundamental weight for
\mathsf{B_{3}}
, which is minuscule.

A similar check can be carried out for the modules
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Note that Lemma
4.1
(iii) is not true in general if

\mathbf{U}
is of affine type.
Consider for example the diagram of type
\widetilde{\mathsf{BC}_{1}}

with one vertex coloured in:
0
1

corresponding to an affine Lie algebra of type
\mathsf{A}_{2}^{(2)}
.
We take
I=\left\{0,1\right\}
and

\langle h_{0},\alpha_{1}\rangle=-1,\qquad\langle h_{1},\alpha_{0}\rangle=-4.

Taking
I_{\bullet}=\left\{1\right\}
, we have

\rho_{\bullet}^{\vee}=\frac{h_{1}}{2},

so that
\langle\rho_{\bullet}^{\vee},\alpha_{0}\rangle=2\in\mathbb{Z}
.
This shows that
(I_{\bullet},\id)

is an admissible Satake diagram.
Furthermore, since
\langle h_{1},-\alpha_{0}\rangle=4
, the projection
of
-\alpha_{0}
to the weight lattice of
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is
4\omega_{1}
,
which is neither a minuscule nor a pseudo-minuscule weight.

We also note that it is this result that makes the remainder
of this section wrong in general for admissible pairs of infinite type.
If every rank-1 subdiagram has either a minuscule weight or is of
type
\mathsf{BII}
, the proofs in the remainder of this section still
work.

Notation 4.3
.

Let
T^{\mathrm{ad}}
be the Lusztig braid group operator associated with
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
acting on the
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-modules
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
,
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})
and their (tensor-)product.

Corollary 4.4
.

We have the decompositions

\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})=\mathrm{span}_{k}\{T^{\mathrm{ad}}(W_{\bullet})(E_{i})\}\oplus\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})_{0}

and

\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})=\mathrm{span}_{k}\{T^{\mathrm{ad}}(W_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})\}\oplus\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})_{0}.

Furthermore,
\dim_{k}\big(\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})_{0}\big),\dim_{k}\big(\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{i})_{0}\big)\leq 1
, with equality holding if and only if the rank-one diagram corresponding to
i\in I_{\circ}
is of type
\mathsf{BII}
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.1
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
i,k\in I_{\circ}
. The
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-module
V=\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{k})
is a quotient of the
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-module

\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})\otimes\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{k})
.

Proof.

The action of
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
on the tensor product is by the coproduct. As
\ad(x)(ab)=\ad(x_{(1)})(a)\ad(x_{(2)})(b)
, this implies that the multiplication map

m:\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})\otimes\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{k})\to\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{k}),\qquad\qquad x\otimes y\mapsto xy

is a surjective
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})
-homomorphism. This establishes the claim.
∎

Let
M
and
N
be finite-dimensional integrable
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-modules. The Lusztig braid group action is related to the tensor product via the rank-one quasi
R
matrix
L_{j}
for
j\in I_{\bullet}
cf.
[
27
, §5.3.1]
, then by
[
27
, Proposition 5.3.4]

(13)

T_{j}\otimes T_{j}(z)=T_{j}(L_{i}(z)),\qquad\qquad\forall z\in M\otimes N.

On the right hand side the action of the braid group operator
T_{j}
is regarded as the action on the tensor product
M\otimes N
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
J
be a finite sequence of simple roots in
I_{\bullet}
and
i,k\in I_{\circ}
. We set

\kappa(J,i,k)=\langle h_{i},\sum_{j\in J}\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{k}\rangle.

Putting the previous results together, we have the tools to study the
q
-commutators

\commutator{x}{\ad(E_J)(E_k)}_{q_{i}^{\kappa(J,i,k)}}\qquad x\in\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i}).

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
J
be a finite sequence of simple roots,
i,k\in I_{\circ}
and
x\in\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})
, then

(14)

[x,\ad(E_{J})(E_{k})]_{q_{i}^{\kappa(J,i,k)}}\in\delta_{i,k}\bigg(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}\big(K_{i}^{2}-1\big)\bigg).

Remark 4.8
.

Note that Proposition
4.7
is clearly true in case
I_{\bullet}=\emptyset
, the technical difficulty is dealing with the case
I_{\bullet}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Assume
x,\ad(E_{J})(E_{k})
are both nonzero. Consider the case where
y:=\ad(E_{J})(E_{k})
is generic and
x=T^{\ad}_{w}(F_{i}K_{i})
with
w\in W_{\bullet},
cf. Corollary
4.4
. Let
w=s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{n}}
be a reduced expression. The identity
xy=m(x\otimes y)
implies that by repeated application of (
13
)

(15)

\displaystyle xy

\displaystyle=m(T^{\ad}_{w}(F_{i}K_{i})\otimes y)

\displaystyle=m(T^{\ad}_{w}(F_{i}K_{i})\otimes T^{\ad}_{w}\circ(T^{\ad}_{w})^{-1}(y))

\displaystyle=m\big(T_{i_{1}}^{\ad}\ad(L_{i_{1}})\dots T_{i_{n}}^{\ad}\ad(L_{i_{n}})(F_{i}K_{i}\otimes(T^{\ad}_{w})^{-1}(y))\big).

The adjoint action of
T_{i_{1}}^{\ad}\ad(L_{i_{1}})\dots T_{i_{n}}^{\ad}\ad(L_{i_{n}})
on
\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})\otimes\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{k})
is given by an element
z\in\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
, cf.
[
27
, §5.2.1]
.
Loc.cit. also implies that the element
z
is determined by the homogeneous
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-weight of
x\otimes y
. From Lemma
4.1
, it follows that

(16)

\displaystyle m\big(T_{i_{1}}^{\ad}\ad(L_{i_{1}})\dots T_{i_{n}}^{\ad}\ad(L_{i_{n}})(F_{i}K_{i}\otimes(T^{\ad}_{w})^{-1}(y))\big)

\displaystyle=m(\ad(z)(F_{i}K_{i}\otimes(T^{\ad}_{w})^{-1}(y)))

\displaystyle=\ad(z)(F_{i}K_{i}(T^{\ad}_{w})^{-1}(y))

\displaystyle=q_{i}^{\kappa(J,i,k)}\ad(z)((T^{\ad}_{w})^{-1}(y)F_{i}K_{i})

\displaystyle\quad+\delta_{i,k}\bigg(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}\big(K_{i}^{2}-1\big)\bigg).

As
z\in\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is determined by the
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-weight of
x\otimes y
, which is the same for
y\otimes x
, it follows from
[
27
, §5.2.1]
that
\ad(z)((T^{\ad}_{w})^{-1}(E_{k}))F_{i}K_{i})=yx
, by reversing the steps in (
15
) and (
16
). An analogous argument applies when
y=T_{w}^{\ad}(E_{k})
and
x
is any weight vector.

By Corollary
4.4
, it remains to consider the case where
x\in\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(F_{i}K_{i})_{0}
and
y\in\ad(\mathbf{U}_{\bullet})(E_{k})_{0}
. This implies both
i
and
k
are of type
\mathsf{BII}
. Assuming irreducibility of
I
, we must have
i=k
. The commutation relations in
\mathbf{U}
with
x=\ad(z^{-})(F_{i}K_{i})
and
y=\ad(z^{+})(E_{i})
for
z^{-}\in\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}^{-}
and
z^{+}\in\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}^{+}
directly yield (
14
).
∎

4.2.
A Cartan decomposition of
V_{\bullet}^{+}\mathbf{U}^{0}

Definition 4.9
.

[Regular pair]
A
regular pair

(K,J)
consists of

(i)

K=(\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{n}})
, a finite sequence of simple roots in
I_{\circ}
;

(ii)

J=(J_{1},\dots,J_{n})
, a sequence of finite sequences of simple roots in
I_{\bullet}
;

with the property that
\ad(E_{J_{i_{j}}})(E_{i_{j}})\neq 0
for all
1\leq j\leq n.

Remark 4.10
.

In Definition
4.9
we allow empty sequences
()
for
K
and
J_{j}
, taking the convention
E_{()}=1
.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
X
be a finite set and let
\mathrm{Fin}(X)
denote the set of finite sequences
of elements in
X
. Let
\varsigma:\mathrm{Fin}(X)\to\mathrm{Fin}(X)
be the permutation map
\varsigma(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(x_{2},...,x_{n},x_{1}).

Notation 4.12
.

For each regular pair
(K,J)
and
h\in Y
, set

(17)

C(J,K,h)=\frac{d_{\tau(i_{1})}}{c_{\tau(i_{1}})}q-\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\sum_{j=2}^{n}\langle\epsilon_{i_{1}}h_{i_{1}},-\Theta\big(\alpha_{i_{j}}+\mathrm{wt}(J_{i_{j}})\big)\rangle-\langle h,\alpha_{i_{1}}-\Theta(\alpha_{i_{1}})\rangle\bigg).

Moreover, set

(18)

R(J,K,h)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}C(\varsigma^{j-1}(J),\varsigma^{j-1}(K),h).

Notation 4.13
.

Let
K=(\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{n}})
, a finite sequence of simple roots in
I_{\circ}
, then we write
\mathrm{wt}(K)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i_{j}}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(K,J)
be a regular pair. Then, there are finitely many regular pairs
(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{wt}(K^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{wt}(K)
.

Proof.

Note that there are finitely many
K^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{wt}(K^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{wt}(K)
. Fix such a finite sequence
K^{\prime}=(\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{n}})
. For each
1\leq j\leq n
it follows by Lemma
4.1
that there are finitely many
J
such that
\ad(E_{J})(E_{i_{j}})\neq 0.
Therefore there are finitely many regular pairs
(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{wt}(K^{\prime})<\mathrm{wt}(K)
.
∎

Definition 4.15
.

[Regular values]
Let
(K,J)
be a regular pair, then the set of
(K,J)
-regular values

??(K,J)\subseteq Y
is defined as
Y
if
K=\emptyset
and otherwise it is inductively defined as the set of
h\in Y
such that

(i)

h-\Theta(h_{i}),h,h+2h_{i}\in Y_{\mathrm{reg}}(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})
for all
i\in I
, regular pairs
(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{wt}(K^{\prime})<\mathrm{wt}(K)
;

(ii)

for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
we have
R(\varsigma^{i}(K),\varsigma^{i}(J),h)\neq 0
.

Remark 4.16
.

Let
(K,J)
be a regular pair. Note that the set of
h\in Y
that satisfy condition (ii) from Definition
4.15
for
(K,J)
equals
Y
minus a finite number of hyperplanes. The inductive definition of regular values together with Lemma
4.14
implies that
Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(K,J)
is contained in
Y
minus a finite number of hyperplanes. Therefore,
Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(K,J)
is Zariski dense in
Y
.

Next, we extend Definition
4.15
to
V_{\bullet}^{+}
and
\mathbf{U}
. For this let

\{N(K_{\varsigma},J_{\varsigma})=\ad(E_{J_{\varsigma_{1}}})(E_{i_{\varsigma_{1}}})\dots\ad(E_{\varsigma_{\mathrm{max}}})(E_{{\varsigma_{\mathrm{max}}}}):\varsigma\in\delta\}\subseteq V_{\bullet}^{+}

be a basis of
V_{\bullet}^{+}
, for some index set
\delta
.

Definition 4.17

(Regular values of
\mathbf{U}
)
.

Let
y=\sum_{(K,J)\in\delta}a_{\varsigma}N(K,J)\in V_{\bullet}^{+}
, then define

Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(y)=\bigcap_{(K,J)\in\delta}Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(K,J).

Extending this to elements of
\mathbf{U}
, making use of the quantum Iwasawa decomposition, let

x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}N(\varsigma_{i})K_{\mu_{i}}B_{L_{i}},

then

Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(x)=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\big(Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}\big(N(K_{\varsigma_{i}},J_{\varsigma_{i}})\big)-\mu_{i}\big).

Remark 4.18
.

The definition of
Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}:V_{\bullet}^{+}\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
depends on a choice of basis of
V_{\bullet}^{+}
, Section
4.1
gives us a canonical choice.

Notation 4.19
.

Denote by
\sim
the equivalence relation modulo

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
. That is to say
x\sim y
if and only if
x-y\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
.

Next, we introduce the restricted grading of
\mathbf{U}
as in
[
26
, p. 97]
. We note that
V_{\bullet}^{+}
can be written as a direct sum of weight spaces. For
\beta\in X
, recall the notation
\tilde{\beta}=\frac{\beta-\Theta(\beta)}{2}
.

Definition 4.20
.

For a subset
S\subseteq\mathbf{U}
and weight
\beta\in X
, we write

S_{\beta,r}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta^{\prime}\in X\\
\widetilde{\beta}=\widetilde{\beta^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}S_{\beta^{\prime}}.

Recall that
\mathbf{U}^{+}\cong V_{\bullet}^{+}\otimes\mathbf{U}^{+}_{\bullet}
as linear spaces via the multiplication map, cf.
[
18
, (3.11)]
. As
\widetilde{\alpha_{j}}=0
for all
j\in I_{\bullet}
, we note that

(19)

\mathbf{U}^{+}_{\beta,r}\cong\mathbf{U}^{+}_{\bullet}\otimes V_{\bullet,\beta,r}^{+}.

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
(K,J)
be a regular pair and let
h\in Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(K,J)
be a regular value, then

(20)

\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}\sim 0.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the restricted weight
\widetilde{\beta}
of
\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})
.

For the induction base consider the case that
\widetilde{\beta}=0
. Then
K=()
and hence

\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}=K_{h}\sim 0.

Now assume that (
20
) holds for all
\ad(E_{N_{1}})(E_{m_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{N_{p}})(E_{m_{p}})
with restricted weight
\tilde{\beta^{\prime}}
, where
\tilde{\beta^{\prime}}<\tilde{\beta}
.

Claim 1:

Assume that

\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})
has restricted weight
\tilde{\beta}
. Then

\displaystyle\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(J_{k})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}\sim C(K,J,h)\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})K_{h},

here
C(K,J,h)
is defined in (
17
).

Proof Claim 1:

We can write

(21)

E_{i_{1}}=\frac{T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(B_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})-T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})-s_{\tau(i_{1})}}{c_{\tau(i_{1})}}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{\tau(i_{1})}}\ad(E_{J_{1}})(T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(B_{\tau(i_{1})}))K_{w_{\bullet}\tau(i_{1})}\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}

\displaystyle-\ad(E_{J_{1}})\quantity(\frac{s_{\tau(i_1)}}{c_{\tau(i_1)} })\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}

\displaystyle-\ad(E_{J_{1}})\quantity(\frac{1}{c_{\tau(i_1)} }
T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}(F_{\tau(i_1)} K_{\tau(i_1)}))\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}.

Note
\ad:\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}\to\End(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}})
and that by definition
h+\epsilon_{i_{1}}w_{\bullet}(h_{\tau(i_{1})})=h-\Theta(h_{i_{1}})
is regular for

\big((\alpha_{i_{2}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{k}}),(J_{2},\dots,J_{k})\big).

Note that the restricted weight of
\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})
is less then the restricted weight of
\ad(E_{J_{1}})E_{i_{1}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})
, moreover by definition we have

h\in Y_{\mathrm{reg}}((\alpha_{i_{2}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{k}}),(J_{2},\dots,J_{k})).

As
s_{\tau_{i_{1}}}\in\mathbf{B}
and
T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(B_{\tau(i_{1})})\in\mathbf{B}
, we can apply the induction hypothesis to show that

(22)

\displaystyle\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}\sim

\displaystyle-\ad(E_{J_{1}})\quantity(\frac{1}{c_{\tau(i_1)} }
T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}(F_{\tau(i_1)} K_{\tau(i_1)}))\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}.

By
[
18
, Lemma 3.5]
there exists a
J_{1}^{-}\in\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}^{-}
with

(23)

\ad(E_{J_{1}})(T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})}))=\ad(E_{J_{1}^{-}})(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})\in S(\mathbf{U}^{-}).

By Proposition
4.7
, we have

\displaystyle\ad(E_{J_{1}^{-}})(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})}),\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})]K_{h}

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{U}^{+}_{\bullet}V_{\tilde{\beta^{\prime}}-2\tilde{\alpha}_{\tau(i_{1})},r}^{+}K_{h+\epsilon_{i_{1}}h_{\tau({i_{1}})}\pm\epsilon_{i_{1}}h_{\tau({i_{1}})}}.

Here, one applies the induction hypothesis, noting that regularity holds by definition, to deduce that

(24)

\mathbf{U}^{+}_{\bullet}V_{\tilde{\beta^{\prime}}-2\tilde{\alpha}_{\tau(i_{1})},r}^{+}K_{h+\epsilon_{i_{1}}h_{\tau({i_{1}}})\pm\epsilon_{i_{1}}h_{\tau({i_{1}})}}{\sim}0.

Moreover, we note that

(25)

\displaystyle\ad(E_{J_{1}^{-}})(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})K_{h}=\ad(E_{J_{1}})\big(T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})\big)K_{h}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h,\mathrm{wt}(J_{1})-w_{\bullet}\alpha_{\tau(i_{1})}\rangle}K_{h}\ad(E_{J_{1}})\big(T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})\big)

\displaystyle\sim-d_{\tau(i_{1})}q^{\langle h,\mathrm{wt}(J_{1})-w_{\bullet}\alpha_{\tau(i_{1})}\rangle}K_{h}\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})

\displaystyle=-d_{\tau(i_{1})}q^{\langle h,-w_{\bullet}\alpha_{\tau({i_{1}})}-\alpha_{i_{1}}\rangle}\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})K_{h}

here, in (
25
) the second to last equality follows by an argument analogous to (
22
). Remark that
\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})
has weight

\sum_{j=2}^{k}\quantity(\alpha_{i_j}+ \mathrm{wt}(J_j))
and set

L(J,K)=q^{\langle\epsilon_{i_{1}}w_{\bullet}\tau(h_{i_{1}}),\sum_{j=2}^{k}\alpha_{i_{j}}+\mathrm{wt}(J_{j})\rangle},

such that
\frac{c_{\tau(i_{1})}}{d_{\tau(i_{1}})}q^{\langle h,-w_{\bullet}\alpha_{\tau({i_{1}})}-\alpha_{i_{1}}\rangle}L(J,K)=C(J,K,h)
. As a final step we combine equations (
22
)-(
25
) to deduce

(26)

\displaystyle\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:babsim0}}}{{\sim}}\frac{-1}{c_{\tau(i)}}\ad(E_{J_{1}})(T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:jmin}}}{{=}}\frac{-1}{c_{\tau(i)}}\ad(E_{J_{1}^{-}})(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:sim02}}}{{\sim}}\frac{-1}{c_{\tau(i)}}L(J,K)\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})\ad(E_{J_{1}^{-}})(F_{\tau(i_{1})}K_{\tau(i_{1})})K_{h}+\lot

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bablasteq}}}{{\sim}}C(J,K,h)\ad(E_{J_{2}})(E_{i_{2}})\cdots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})K_{h}.

Claim 2:

Under the assumptions of Claim 1,
\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}\sim 0
.

Proof Claim 2:

k
-fold application of Claim 1 yields
(note that
h
is
(K,J)
-regular iff it is

(\varsigma(K),\varsigma(J))
-regular) that

\displaystyle\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}\sim

\displaystyle\quantity(\prod_{j=1}^k C(\varsigma^{j-1}(J),\varsigma^{j-1}(K),h))\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}.

Since
h\in Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(K,J)
, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{k}C(\varsigma^{j-1}(J),\varsigma^{j-1}(K)),h)=R(J,K,h)\neq 1.

This implies
\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}\sim 0
.∎

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
(K,J)
be a regular pair, then the radial part calculation in Theorem
4.21
has the property that for each
h\in Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(K,J)
the
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\mathbf{U}^{0}\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
decomposition of

\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}=b_{(1)}K_{h_{(2)}}b_{(3)}^{\prime}

can be chosen such that
h\leq h_{(2)}\leq h+\Theta(\mathrm{wt}(K))
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the restricted weight of the
K
. The case
K=()
needs no proof. Next, suppose the conclusion holds for all regular pairs
(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{wt}(K^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{wt}(K)
. The dependence on the regular pairs of lower restricted weight in the inductive procedure in the proof of Theorem
4.21
is governed by the equations (
22
) and (
26
). From (
22
) and (
26
) the induction hypothesis, we deduce that decomposition of

\ad(E_{J_{1}})(E_{i_{1}})\dots\ad(E_{J_{k}})(E_{i_{k}})K_{h}=b_{(1)}K_{h_{(2)}}b_{(3)}^{\prime}

satisfies
h\leq h_{(2)}\leq h+\Theta(\mathrm{wt}(K))
.
∎

As in the classical case the restriction of spherical functions to a torus is injective. Recall the space of spherical functions
E^{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}
from Definition
2.9
.

Proposition 4.23
.

The restriction
\mathrm{res}_{\mathbf{U}^{0}}:E^{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}\to\mathrm{Hom}\big(\mathbf{U}^{0},\mathrm{Hom}(V(\gamma),V(\gamma^{\prime}))\big)
is injective.

Proof.

Let
\Phi\in E^{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}
and suppose that
\mathrm{res}_{\mathbf{U}^{0}}(\Phi)=0
. Write
\Phi=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}c_{f_{j},v_{i}}\otimes E_{i,j}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathrm{Hom}\big(V(\gamma),V(\gamma^{\prime})\big)
.
If
c_{f_{j},v_{i}}\neq 0
, then there exists
x_{i,j}\in\mathbf{U}
such that
x_{i,j}\triangleright\phi_{i,j}(1)=1
. By Theorem
4.21
and
\mathrm{res}_{\mathbf{U}^{0}}(\Phi)=0
, it follows that
x_{i,j}\triangleright c_{f_{i},v_{j}}(K_{h})=0
for all
h\in Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(x_{i,j})
. Since
Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(x_{i,j})\subseteq Y
is Zariski dense, it follows that
c_{f_{i},v_{j}}=0
. A contradiction. Thus, we conclude
\Phi=0
.
∎

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
\Phi\in E^{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}
and let
x\in\mathbf{U}
. Then, using Theorem
4.21
there exists a function

\Pi_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}(x):Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(x)\to\mathrm{End}(V_{\gamma})\otimes\mathbf{U}^{0}\otimes\mathrm{End}(V_{\gamma^{\prime}}),\qquad K_{h}\mapsto L^{h,x}_{(1)}\otimes K_{x,{h_{(2)}}}\otimes R^{x,h}_{(3)}

such that

(27)

(x\triangleright\Phi)(K_{h})=L^{x,h}_{(1)}\Phi(K_{x,{h_{(2)}}})R^{x,h}_{(3)}.

In particular if

\Phi\in E^{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}(\lambda)

and
\Omega\in\mathbf{U}^{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}=\{\Omega\in\mathbf{U}:\Omega b=b\Omega\qquad\text{for all }b\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\}
then

(28)

a_{\Omega,\lambda}\Phi(K_{h})=(\Omega\triangleright\Phi)(K_{h})=L^{\Omega,h}_{(1)}\Phi(K_{\Omega,{h_{(2)}}})R^{\Omega,h}_{(3)},\qquad\text{where}\qquad a_{\Omega,\lambda}\in k.

Definition 4.25

(Order)
.

For each element
x\in\mathbf{U}

order

\mathrm{ord}(x)
is defined as the minimal elements of

\{{h-h_{(2)}}:h\in Y_{\mathrm{Reg}}(x),\,\,K_{h}x=b_{(1)}K_{h_{(2)}}b_{(2)},\quad b_{(1)}\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},b_{(2)}\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}\}.

If
\mathrm{ord}(x)
consists of one element
\{\mu\}
, we say that
x
has order
\mu
.

Recall the distinguished elements
c_{\mu}\in Z(\mathbf{U})
from
Lemma
3.14
.
By
[
26
, Theorem 7.7]
it follows that for
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}=\epsilon
,
the radial part of
c_{\mu}
has order
-2h_{\mu}
.
We will show this in general.

First, we need a preliminary lemma. As preparation set

\mathcal{F}^{n}(\mathbf{U}^{-})=\{F_{J}:J\in\mathrm{Fin}(\mathbf{I}),\,|J|\leq n\}

and for a finite sequence of simple roots
J=(\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{n}})
write
B_{J}=B_{i_{1}}\dots B_{i_{n}}
, taking the convention that
B_{j}=F_{j}
if
j\in I_{\bullet}
.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
J
be a finite sequence of simple roots and
F_{J}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{I_{i}}K_{\mu_{i}}B_{J_{i}}
be the corresponding quantum Iwasawa decomposition, then
-\mathrm{wt}(J)\leq K_{\mu_{i}}\leq 0
and
0\leq\mathrm{wt}(J_{i})\leq\mathrm{wt}(J)
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the length of
J
. The case
J=\emptyset
is clear. Next assume that the statement holds for all
J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{n-1}(\mathbf{U}^{-})
and let
F_{J}\in\mathcal{F}^{n}(\mathbf{U}^{-})\setminus\mathcal{F}^{n-1}(\mathbf{U}^{-})
. Then
B_{J}-F_{J}\in\mathbf{U}^{+}K_{j_{1}}^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{n-1}(\mathbf{U}^{-})
. Thus, using
the quantum Serre relations for
\mathbf{U}^{-}
it follows that
F_{J}-B_{J}
is contained in the left
\mathbf{U}^{+}
-submodule of generated by
K_{j_{1}}^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{n-1}(\mathbf{U}^{-})
. By the induction hypothesis, the lemma follows.
∎

By construction, the central elements
c_{\mu}
lie in the
\ad(\mathbf{U})
-module
\ad(\mathbf{U})(K_{-2h_{\mu}})
and satisfy

(29)

c_{\mu}\in K_{-2h_{\mu}}+\ad(\mathbf{U}_{+})(K_{-2h_{\mu}}),

where
\mathbf{U}_{+}=\ker(\epsilon)
, cf.
[
26
, p. 109]
.

Proposition 4.27
.

For
\mu\in X^{+}
with
2h_{\mu}\in Y
, the central element
c_{\mu}
has order
-2h_{\mu}
.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in X^{+}
and
h\in Y_{\mathrm{Reg}(c_{\mu})}
. By (
29
) and the triangular decomposition of
\mathbf{U}
, we may write

c_{\mu}=K_{-2\mu}+\sum_{\mathrm{wt}(A)=\mathrm{wt}(C)}c_{A,C}\ad(E_{A}F_{C})(K_{-2h_{\mu}}).

Using the relations
[
12
, 4.9 (5), Lemma 4.12]
for the coproduct and antipode, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{wt}(A)=\mathrm{wt}(C)}c_{A,C}\ad(E_{A}F_{C})(K_{-2h_{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathrm{wt}(A+A^{\prime})=\mathrm{wt}(C)}c^{\prime}_{A,C}E_{A}K_{-2h_{\mu}}K_{\mathrm{wt}(C)}F_{C}E_{A^{\prime}}.

The commutation relation
E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{i,j}[K_{i}]_{q_{i}}
implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{wt}(A+A^{\prime})=\mathrm{wt}(C)}c^{\prime}_{A,C}E_{A}K_{-2h_{\mu}}K_{\mathrm{wt}(C)}F_{C}E_{A^{\prime}}=\sum_{\mathrm{wt}(A)=\mathrm{wt}(C)}c^{\prime\prime}_{A,C}E_{A}K_{-2h_{\mu}}K_{\mathrm{wt}(C)+h_{A}}F_{C}.

with
0\leq h_{A}\leq 2\mathrm{wt}(A)
and scalars
c^{\prime\prime}_{A,C}
. Now, apply Lemma
4.26
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{wt}(A)=\mathrm{wt}(C)}c^{\prime\prime}_{A,C}E_{A}K_{-2h_{\mu}}K_{\mathrm{wt}(C)+h_{A}}F_{C}=\sum_{\mathrm{wt}(A)\geq\mathrm{wt}(C)}c^{\prime\prime\prime}_{A,C}E_{A}E_{C^{\prime\prime}}K_{-2h_{\mu}}K_{\mathrm{wt}(C)+h_{A}+h_{C}}B_{C^{\prime}},

with
-\mathrm{wt}(C)\leq h_{C}\leq 0
. According to Proposition
4.22
we have

K_{h-2h_{\mu}}E_{A}E_{C^{\prime\prime}}=b_{(1)}h_{(2)}b_{(3)}

with

(30)

h-2h_{\mu}+\mathrm{wt}(C)+h_{A}+h_{C}\leq h_{(2)}\leq h-2h_{\mu}+\mathrm{wt}(C)+h_{A}+h_{C}+\Theta(\mathrm{wt}(K))-2h_{\mu}

and
b_{(1)}\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},b_{(3)}\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
. Combining (
30
) with the inequalities for
h_{A}
and
h_{C}
, we conclude that
h-2h_{\mu}\leq h_{(1)}
. Thus, using (
29
) the order of
c_{\mu}
equals
-2h_{\mu}
.
∎

5.
Zonal Spherical Functions

We now assume that
\mathcal{R}
is finite.
In this section we summarise some results from the literature
about ZSFs of quantum symmetric pairs.
Later, we deduce the matrix valued orthogonality weight for the matrix-spherical function from the zonal spherical case. Thus, it is of utmost importance to understand the zonal spherical functions.

From
[
18
, §9.1]
we can see that most coideal Hopf algebras

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
associated to a given admissible pair
(I_{\bullet},\tau)

are related via appropriate conjugation automorphisms:
we can interpret a group homomorphism
\xi:X\to k^{\times}
as
a natural transformation of the forgetful functor of the category of
weight modules over
k
by having
\xi
act as
\xi(\mu)
on the
weight space
M_{\mu}
of a weight module
M
.
In particular,
\ad(\xi)
acts on
\mathbf{U}_{\lambda}
as multiplication with

\xi(\lambda)
.
Kolb calls the group of such automorphisms
\tilde{H}
.

In particular,
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
are
\tilde{H}
-related
if and only if

(31)

\begin{cases}\frac{c_{i}}{c_{\tau(i)}}=\frac{d_{i}}{d_{\tau(i)}}&i\in I_{\circ},\tau(i)\neq i,\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0\\
c_{i}t_{i}^{2}=d_{i}s_{i}^{2}&i\in I_{ns}.\end{cases}

In accordance to
[
25
]
we write
\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}
for the
set of right coideal subalgebras of
\mathbf{U}
associated to
(I_{\bullet},\tau)
.
Evidently,
\tilde{H}
acts on
\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\in\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}
and let
X\in\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}/\tilde{H}
be
an orbit.
There exists at least one
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}\in X
such that

-\rho
-shifted restrictions of
E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}}
are
W_{\Sigma}
-invariant.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d}^{\prime},\bm{t}^{\prime}}\in X
. For
i\in I_{\circ}
with

\tau(i)\neq i
and
\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0
pick

d_{i}
to be a square root of
\frac{c_{i}c_{\tau(i)}d_{i}^{\prime}}{d_{\tau(i)}^{\prime}}
in such a way that
d_{i}d_{\tau(i)}=c_{i}c_{\tau(i)}
.
For any other
i\in I_{\circ}
pick
d_{i}=c_{i}
.
Lastly, for
i\in I_{ns}
pick
t_{i}=\sqrt{\frac{d_{i}}{d^{\prime}_{i}}}t^{\prime}_{i}
.
Then
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}\in X
by (
31
), and by Lemma
3.58
the claim holds.
∎

Lemma 5.2

(
[
25
, Lemma 6.2]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}\in\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}
, let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}:X\to k^{\times}
be group homomorphisms,
and let
F\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}}
.

(i)

\xi_{2}\cdot F\cdot\xi_{1}^{-1}\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\xi_{1}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\xi_{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}}
;

(ii)

For
\xi:X\to k^{\times}
, we define
\tilde{\xi}:2L\to k^{\times}
by restriction,
and having it act on
k[2L]
by
\tilde{\xi}\triangleright e^{\lambda}=\xi(\lambda)e^{\lambda}
.
Then
\Res(\xi_2\cdot F\cdot\xi_1^{-1})=\tilde{\xi_{2}}\tilde{\xi_{1}}^{-1}\triangleright\Res(F)
,
and
(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\xi_2\cdot F\cdot\xi_1^{-1})=\tilde{\xi_{2}}\tilde{\xi_{1}}^{-1}\triangleright(-\rho)\triangleright\Res( F)
.

Proof.

Let
b\in\ad(\xi_{1})(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}),b^{\prime}\in\ad(\xi_{2})(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}})
,
then
\ad(\xi_{1}^{-1})(b)=\xi_{1}^{-1}b\xi_{1}\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
and idem for

b^{\prime}
.
Let
x\in\mathbf{U}
, then

\displaystyle(\xi_{2}\cdot F\cdot\xi_{1}^{-1})(bxb^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F(\xi_{1}^{-1}bxb^{\prime}\xi_{2})=F(\xi_{1}^{-1}b\xi_{1}\xi_{1}^{-1}x\xi_{2}\xi_{2}^{-1}b^{\prime}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\ad(\xi_{1}^{-1})(b))F(\xi_{1}^{-1}x\xi_{2})\epsilon(\ad(\xi_{2}^{-1})(b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\epsilon(b)\epsilon(b^{\prime})(\xi_{2}\cdot F\cdot\xi_{1}^{-1})(x),

which shows the first claim.

For the second claim assume that
F=c^{M}_{f,v}
with

v=\sum_{\lambda\in 2L}v_{\lambda}
for
v_{\lambda}\in M_{\lambda}
.
Then we have

\Res(F)=\sum_{\lambda\in 2L}f(v_{\lambda})e^{\lambda}.

Moreover, we have
\xi_{2}\cdot F\cdot\xi_{1}^{-1}=c^{M}_{f\xi_{1}^{-1},\xi_{2}v}
whose restriction is the same as that of

c^{M}_{f,\xi_{1}^{-1}\xi_{2}v}
, which is

\displaystyle\Res(\xi_2\cdot F\cdot\xi_1^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in 2L}f((\xi_{1}^{-1}\xi_{2}v)_{\lambda})e^{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in 2L}\xi_{1}^{-1}(\lambda)\xi_{2}(\lambda)f(v_{\lambda})e^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\xi_{2}}\tilde{\xi_{1}}^{-1}\triangleright\Res(F).\qed

When attempting to classify the ZSFs for
arbitrary (allowed) choices of parameters for the
coideal subalgebras
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
,
Lemmas
5.1
and

5.2
show that we can restrict ourselves to one
representative per
\tilde{H}\times\tilde{H}
orbit of

\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}\times\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}
.
It makes sense to have these representatives satisfy the
conditions of Lemma
3.58
, so that the
resulting
-\rho
-shifted ZSFs are

W_{\Sigma}
-invariant.

Proposition 5.3
.

Assume that
\Sigma
is irreducible.
The
\tilde{H}
-orbits of

\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}
look as follows:

(i)

If
\mathcal{R}=\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
and
\tau
exchanges
the two copies of
\Sigma
, there is one orbit, the one of

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c}}
with
c_{i}=1
(
i\in I_{\circ}
); otherwise,

\mathcal{R}
is irreducible as well.

(ii)

If the Satake diagram for
(I_{\bullet},\tau)
is
of the types

\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}
(
2p<n+1
),
\mathsf{AIV}
,
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1}
, or
\mathsf{EIII}

the orbits are characterised by one parameter up to sign:
pick
i\in I
such that
I_{\bullet}\cup\left\{i,\tau(i)\right\}
is
of type
\mathsf{AIV}_{m}
and
define
\bm{c}(a)
by

c_{i}(a)=i^{m-2}a
,
c_{\tau(i)}=(-i)^{m-2}a^{-1}
and
c_{j}=1
for
j\in I_{\circ}\setminus\left\{i,\tau(i)\right\}
.
Then every orbit contains two elements of the type

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c}(\pm a),0}
for

a\in k^{\times}
.
In
[
24
]
, this is called
“Variation 1”
.

(iii)

If the Satake diagram is of type
\mathsf{AI}_{1}
,

\mathsf{AIII}_{2p-1,p}
,
\mathsf{BI}_{n,2}
,
\mathsf{CI}_{n}
,
\mathsf{DI}_{n,2}
,
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p}
,
or
\mathsf{EVII}
,
the orbits are also characterised by one parameter up to sign:
there is exactly one
i\in I_{\circ}
such that
there are elements
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
with
s_{i}\neq 0
.
Then every orbit contains
one or two elements

\mathbf{B}_{1,\pm\bm{s}}
with

s_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
.
In
[
24
]
, this is called
“Variation 2”
.

(iv)

In all other cases, there is one orbit.

All representatives listed here yield
W_{\Sigma}
-invariant

-\rho
-shifted restrictions of ZSFs.

Proof.

By
[
5
]
,
\Sigma
is irreducible if either
\mathcal{R}=\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
and
\tau
maps one copy of
\Sigma
to the
other (as described in the first case), or if
\mathcal{R}
is
irreducible as well, in which case the classification from

[
5
, pp. 32–33]
holds.

In the first case, we have
\tau(i)\neq i
for all
i
as well as

\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})=0
.
By (
31
), all elements of
\mathbf{B}_{\Theta}

are
\tilde{H}
-conjugate, which proves (i).

It now suffices to check the admissible Satake diagrams for
occurrences of
i\in I_{\circ}
such that
\tau(i)\neq i
and

\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0
and for allowed non-zero
non-standard parameters.

Note that by
[
18
, Remark 9.3]
, Variation 1 can only
occur in case
\Sigma
is of type
\mathsf{BC}
.
Examining these cases (in e.g.
[
5
]
) yields that
the cases listed under (ii) are in fact the ones for which
Variation 1 can be made.
In every case,
\mathcal{S}=\left\{0\right\}
, and there is exactly one
\tau
-orbit
\left\{i,\tau(i)\right\}
with two elements
satisfying

\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0
.
In every case, adding this
\tau
-orbit to
I_{\bullet}
yields
a diagram of type
\mathsf{AIV}_{m}
.

Equation
31
shows that
up to conjugacy, all
c_{j}
(
j\neq i,\tau(i)
) can be set to 1, and that
c_{i},c_{\tau(i)}
can be scaled by the
same number.
Consequently, without loss of generality we can assume
that
c_{j}=1
(
j\neq i,\tau(i)
) and that

c_{i}c_{\tau(i)}=1
.
In other words,
\bm{c}=\bm{c}(a)
for some
a\in k^{\times}
.
Furthermore,
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
’s orbit comprises one more element:

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c}(-a),0}
.

For Variation 2, inspection yields the diagram types listed under (iii),
with one non-zero non-standard parameter permitted in each case:
s_{i}
.
Given a coideal subalgebra
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
,
\bm{c}
can be scaled to be 1 in every component,
and two coideal subalgebras
\mathbf{B}_{1,\bm{s}}
and

\mathbf{B}_{1,\bm{t}}
are then
conjugate iff
s_{i}^{2}=t_{i}^{2}
, which explains the ambiguity in
sign.

In all other cases we have
\mathcal{S}=\left\{0\right\}
and no
i\in I_{\circ}

satisfying
\tau(i)\neq i
and
\alpha_{i}\cdot\Theta(\alpha_{i})\neq 0
.
Consequently, all coideal subalgebras are conjugate and there is
only one orbit, which proves (iv).

We now pick two representatives listed here,
e.g. two times
\mathbf{B}_{1,0}
for (i) or (iv),

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c}(a),0},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c}(b),0}
for (ii),
or
\mathbf{B}_{1,a\bm{e}_{i}},\mathbf{B}_{1,b\bm{e_{i}}}
for (iii) (where
\bm{e}_{i}
has 1 as its
i
-th entry, and 0 everywhere else, and is chosen such that
\bm{e}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
)
All of these pairs satisfy the conditions of
Lemma
3.58
.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
(\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}})
be one of the representatives
from Proposition
5.3
.

(i)

If
\Sigma
is reduced and
\bm{s}=\bm{t}=0
, define
??as follows:

S=S^{\prime}=S((2\Sigma)^{\vee})^{\vee}),\qquad R=R^{\prime}=2\Sigma,\qquad L=L^{\prime}=2P(\Sigma)

(where we give the span of
2\Sigma
the inner product it
inherits from
X
),
let
\psi\in\Sigma
be the highest short root and define

\epsilon:=2\absolutevalue{\psi}^{2}
.

(ii)

If
(I_{\bullet},\tau)
is of any of the following
types:
\mathsf{AI}_{1},\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p},\mathsf{AIV}_{n},\mathsf{CII}_{n,p},\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1}
, write

\Sigma^{0}
for the immultipliable subsystem of
\Sigma
and
define

\displaystyle S=S^{\prime}

\displaystyle=S(2\Sigma^{0})\cup 2S(2\Sigma^{0})^{\vee},

\displaystyle R=R^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2\Sigma^{0},

\displaystyle L=L^{\prime}

\displaystyle=P(2\Sigma^{0})=P(2\Sigma)

and let
\epsilon=2
.

Furthermore, pick the parameters as follows: if
\Sigma
is
reduced and
\bm{s}=\bm{t}=0
, we pick
l:S\to k
as follows:

l(2\alpha+c)=\frac{\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})}{2\absolutevalue{\psi}^{2}}.

Otherwise, pick

\displaystyle\mathsf{AI}_{1}:\qquad(l_{1},\dots,l_{4})

\displaystyle=\quantity(\frac{\sigma-\tau+1}{2},\frac{\sigma+\tau+1}{2},\frac{-\sigma+\tau}{2},\frac{-\sigma-\tau}{2})

\displaystyle\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}:\quad(l_{1},\dots,l_{5})

\displaystyle=\quantity(\frac{\sigma-\tau+1}{2},\frac{\sigma+\tau+1}{2},\frac{-\sigma+\tau}{2}+n+1-2p,\frac{-\sigma-\tau}{2},1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{AIV}_{n}:\quad(l_{1},\dots,l_{4})

\displaystyle=\quantity(\frac{\sigma-\tau+1}{2},\frac{\sigma+\tau+1}{2},\frac{-\sigma+\tau}{2}+n-1,\frac{-\sigma-\tau}{2})

\displaystyle\mathsf{CII}_{n,p}:\quad(l_{1},\dots,l_{5})

\displaystyle=\quantity(\frac{3}{2}+n-2p,\frac{3}{2},n-2p,0,2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1}:\quad(l_{1},\dots,l_{5})

\displaystyle=\quantity(\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},1,0,2),

where
\sigma,\tau
are dependent on the parameters
\bm{c},\bm{s},\bm{d},\bm{t}
:

•

For
\mathsf{AI}_{1}
, we assume that
t_{1}=[\sigma]_{q}
and

s_{1}=[\tau]_{q}

and
c_{1}=d_{1}=1
.

•

For
\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}
for
2p<n+1
and
\mathsf{AIV}_{n}
, we assume that

\bm{d}=\bm{c}(q^{\sigma}),\bm{c}=\bm{c}(q^{\tau})
and

\bm{t}=\bm{s}=0
.

•

For
\mathsf{AIII}_{2p-1,p}
we assume that

\bm{c}=\bm{d}=1
, that
t_{p}=[\sigma]_{q}
, and

s_{p}=[\tau]_{q}
.

•

For
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1}
, we assume that
\bm{s}=\bm{t}=0
and
that
\bm{c}=\bm{d}=\bm{c}(1)
.

Let now
\phi\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}}(\lambda)
,
then there is
C\in k
such that

(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\phi)=CP_{\lambda}

where
P_{\lambda}
is the symmetric Macdonald polynomial

[
29
, §5.3.1]
for the Macdonald data

(S,S^{\prime},R,R^{\prime},L,L^{\prime})
, the labelling
l
, and the base parameter
q^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

For
\Sigma
reduced and
\bm{s}=\bm{t}=0
, this follows from

[
26
, Theorem 8.2]
.
For type
\mathsf{AI}_{1}
, this follows from
[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
.
For type
\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}
, it follows from

[
32
, Theorem 3.4]
, with added explanation of the parameter
provided in
[
30
, Lemma A.2]
for
2p<n+1
and in

[
35
]
for
2p=n+1
.
For type
\mathsf{CII}_{n,p}
, it follows from
[
42
, Theorem 3.1]
. Note that for
p=n
,
\Sigma
is reduced and the weight
(and the orthogonal polynomials) are the same as would be
obtained from Letzter.
For type
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1}
, it follows from
[
33
, Theorem 3(type 7)]
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

The attentive reader might note, that Proposition

5.4
fails to cover the following non-standard cases:

\mathsf{BI}_{n,2},\mathsf{CI}_{n},\mathsf{DI}_{n,2},\mathsf{DIII}_{2p},\mathsf{EVII}

where we expect two-parameter families of orthogonal polynomials,
and the following standard cases:

\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1},\mathsf{EIII},\mathsf{FII},

of which we expect two-parameter families only for
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1},\mathsf{EIII}
, and a family
for
\mathsf{FII}
that has no parameters.

Note also that in the cases
\mathsf{AI}_{1},\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p},\mathsf{AIV}_{n}

where the labelling
l
explicitly depends on the parameters
\bm{c},\bm{s},\bm{d},\bm{t}
, we can remove
the restriction on the values that these parameters might take (i.e. that they be powers of
q
or
q
-numbers).
The modern theory of Macdonald polynomials (as given in
[
29
]
) is phrased in
terms of parameters
\tau_{i},\tilde{\tau}_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,p
; notation borrowed from
[
40
, Definition 2.28]
).
Since the coefficients of the ZSF can be shown to be rational functions in the parameters
\bm{c},\bm{d},\bm{s},\bm{t}
,
and the coefficients of the Macdonald polynomials can be shown to be rational functions in the
\tau_{i},\tilde{\tau}_{i}
,
a Zariski argument shows that the conclusion of Proposition
5.4
holds for all parameter values.

In that case, the relations between
\bm{c},\bm{d},\bm{s},\bm{t}
and
\tau_{i},\tilde{\tau}_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,p
) are given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{AI}_{1}

\displaystyle:\quad\tau_{0}\tau_{1}=q,\tilde{\tau}_{0}\tilde{\tau}_{1}=1,\quad s_{1}=-[\tilde{\tau}_{1};0]_{q},t_{1}=[\tau_{1};-1]_{q}

\displaystyle\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}(2p<n+1)

\displaystyle:\quad\tau_{0}\tau_{p}=q^{n+2-2p},\tilde{\tau}_{0}\tilde{\tau}_{p}=q^{n+1-2p},\tau_{1}=q,

\displaystyle\qquad a=\tilde{\tau}_{0}^{-1},b=\tau_{p}q^{-1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{AIII}_{2p-1,p}

\displaystyle:\quad\tau_{0}\tau_{1}=q,\tilde{\tau}_{0}\tilde{\tau}_{1}=1,\tau_{1}=q

\displaystyle\qquad s_{p}=-[\tilde{\tau}_{p};0]_{q},t_{p}=[\tau_{p};-1]_{q}

\displaystyle\mathsf{AIV}_{n}

\displaystyle:\quad\tau_{0}\tau_{1}=q^{n},\tilde{\tau}_{0}\tilde{\tau_{1}}=q^{n-1},\quad a=i^{n}\tilde{\tau}_{0}^{-1},b=i^{n}\tau_{1}q^{-1}.

where we use (
1
) for the notation
[\tau_{1};-1]_{q}
.

In the following we provide more details on how exactly to match
the results from
[
26
,
21
,
32
,
42
,
33
]
to our conventions.

Remark 5.6

(Letzter,
\Sigma
reduced and
\bm{s}=\bm{t}=0
)
.

In
[
26
, Theorem 8.2]
and the definitions before,
Letzter refers to the Macdonald polynomials as defined in

[
28
]
.
In contrast to
[
29
]
, these Macdonald polynomials depend
not on an affine root system
S
, but on two root systems
(R,S)
,
which Letzter chooses to be
(2\Sigma,(2\Sigma)^{\vee})
.
In particular, the Macdonald polynomials that Letzter works with
are orthogonal with respect to the weight distribution

\triangledown=\Delta^{+}\overline{\Delta^{+}}
with

(32)

\Delta^{+}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\frac{(e^{2\alpha};q^{2\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}})_{\infty}}{(q^{\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})}e^{2\alpha};q^{2\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}})_{\infty}}.

To translate this into the language of
[
29
]
, note that
the infinite
q
-Pochhammer symbols have potentially varying
powers of
q
as base (if
\Sigma
is not simply laced).
This can only occur for
(S,S^{\prime},R,R^{\prime},L,L^{\prime})
of type
[
29
, §1.4.2]
.
Matching (
32
) with
[
29
, §5.1.28(ii)]
, we obtain that
R^{\vee}=2\Sigma
, i.e. that
S=S^{\prime}=S((2\Sigma)^{\vee})^{\vee}
and
L=L^{\prime}=P^{\vee}((2\Sigma)^{\vee})=P(2\Sigma)
.

More concretely, we have

S((2\Sigma)^{\vee})^{\vee}=\left\{2\alpha+2\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}r\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\alpha\in\Sigma,r\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Let
\psi\in\Sigma
be the highest short root, then

(2\psi)^{\vee}
is the highest root of
(2\Sigma)^{\vee}
.
Consequently, the zeroth simple affine root is given by

-2\psi+2\absolutevalue{\psi}^{2}
, so that the fundamental constant function
is
c=2\absolutevalue{\psi}^{2}
, which is what we defined as
\epsilon
.

The symmetric weight function from
[
29
, §5.1.28(ii)]

(fixing the typo in that formula) is then

\triangledown=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\quantity(e^{2\alpha};q_{(2\alpha)^\vee})_{\infty}}{\quantity(q^{l(2\alpha)} e^{2\alpha}; q_{(2\alpha)^\vee})_{\infty}}.

By
[
29
, §5.1.13]
, we have

q_{(2\alpha)^{\vee}}=q^{\frac{\absolutevalue{(2\psi)^\vee}^{2}}{\absolutevalue{(2\alpha)^\vee}^{2}}}=q^{\frac{\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}}{\absolutevalue{\psi}^{2}}}.

We therefore see that to match this with (
32
),
we need to replace
q
by
q^{\epsilon}
.
Furthermore, this shows that
q^{l(2\alpha)}
from Macdonald
corresponds to
q^{2\absolutevalue{\psi}^{2}l(2\alpha)}
, which we need to
equal
q^{\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})}
.
Consequently, we need to have

l(2\alpha)=\frac{\absolutevalue{\alpha}^{2}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})}{2\absolutevalue{\psi}^{2}},

which implies that

l^{\vee}((2\alpha)^{\vee})=\frac{\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})}{2}.

Remark 5.7

(Koornwinder,
\mathsf{AI}_{1}
)
.

We write
K=K_{h_{1}/2},E:=E_{1},F:=F_{1}
, then
the generators
A,B,C,D
from
[
21
, §3]
correspond to

A=K,\qquad D=K^{-1},\qquad B=-iEK^{-1},\qquad C=iKF.

Then the twisted primitive
X_{\sigma}
from
[
21
, §4]
equals

X_{\sigma}=q^{-1/2}K\quantity(F + EK^{-2} + [\sigma]_q(K^{-2}-1))=q^{-1/2}K(B_{1}-\epsilon(B_{1})),

where
B_{1}
is the generator of
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}_{1,[\sigma]_{q}}
. Consequently, the left ideal
generated by
X_{\sigma}
is equivalently generated by
x-\epsilon(x)
(
x\in\mathbf{B}
).
Furthermore,

X_{\sigma}=q^{-1/2}\ad(K)(B_{1}-\epsilon(B_{1}))K,

where
K=K_{\rho}
. Consequently, the right ideal generated by
X_{\sigma}
is equivalently
generated by
x-\epsilon(x)
(
x\in\ad(K)(\mathbf{B})
).

This shows that every
f\in\mathcal{A}
satisfying
X_{\sigma}\cdot f=f\cdot X_{\tau}=0

(what is called
“
(\sigma,\tau)
-spherical”
in
[
21
]
), is a ZSF for
\ad(K_{\rho})(\mathbf{B}_{1,[\tau]_{q}})
and
\mathbf{B}_{1,[\sigma]_{q}}
.
Consequently,
f\cdot K_{\rho}
is a ZSF
for
\mathbf{B}_{1,[\tau]_{q}},\mathbf{B}_{1,[\sigma]_{q}}
and
\Res(f)=(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f\cdot K_\rho)
.
In other words,
f\mapsto f\cdot K_{\rho}
maps the
(\sigma,\tau)
-spherical functions from
Koornwinder to our ZSFs, and the restrictions that Koornwinder determines in

[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
are precisely the polynomials we are looking for.

Remark 5.8
.

[Noumi, Dijkhuizen, Sugitani,
\mathsf{AIII}
]
As is explained in
[
35
, Section 3]
, the
authors of
[
32
]
use a solution
J
to the
reflection equation to define a two-sided coideal (not an algebra!)

\mathfrak{k}^{\sigma}
(depending on a parameter
\sigma
)
as the span of
L^{+}J-JL^{-}
.
An element
f\in\mathcal{A}
is a ZSF if
\mathfrak{k}^{\sigma}\cdot f=f\cdot\mathfrak{k}^{\tau}=0
.

In
[
35
, Proposition 3.8]
it is then proven
(in a way that straightforwardly generalises to
cases where
2p<n+1
) that
f
is a ZSF from
[
32
]
, if and only if

f\triangleleft K_{\rho}
is a ZSF
in the sense of this paper.
The reason for this is that the left ideal

\mathbf{U}\mathfrak{k}^{\sigma}

contains
a-\epsilon(a)
for generators
a
of

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
, and the
right ideal
\mathfrak{k}^{\tau}\mathbf{U}
contains

K_{\rho}(a-\epsilon(a))
for
a\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{d},\bm{t}}
.
(For the relation between
\bm{d},\bm{c}
—or
\bm{t},\bm{s}
if
2n=p+1
—and
\sigma,\tau
, cf.

[
30
, Lemma A.2]
or
[
35
]
).

Remark 5.9

(Sugitani,
\mathsf{CII}
)
.

The author of
[
42
]
uses a similar approach as in Remark
5.8
,
except that the coideal
\mathfrak{k}_{q}
is
defined as the span of
L^{+}J-JS(L^{-})^{T}
, and
f\in\mathcal{A}
is a ZSF if

(33)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}_{q}\cdot f=f\cdot\ad(K_{\rho})(\mathfrak{k}_{q}^{*})=0.

Suppose
\varphi\in E^{\epsilon}
, we would like to show
that
\varphi\triangleleft K_{-\rho}
is a ZSF in the
sense of Sugitani.

It is shown in
[
22
, Lemma 6.1, p. 762]
that
the left ideal
\mathbf{U}\mathfrak{k}_{q}
is generated by
a-\epsilon(a)
for
a\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
.
Consequently, we have

(a-\epsilon(a))\triangleright\varphi\triangleleft K_{-\rho}=0.

Moreover, we have

\varphi\triangleleft K_{-\rho}\triangleleft\ad(K_{\rho})(\mathfrak{k}_{q}^{*})\mathbf{U}=0\Leftrightarrow\varphi\triangleleft\mathfrak{k}_{q}^{*}\mathbf{U}=0.

Moreover, by the end of
[
42
, §1.1]
, the ideal

\mathbf{U}\mathfrak{k}_{q}
is invariant under
*\circ S
.
Consequently,
(\mathbf{U}\mathfrak{k}_{q})^{*}=\mathfrak{k}_{q}^{*}\mathbf{U}=S(\mathfrak{k})\mathbf{U}
.
The right ideal
S(\mathfrak{k})\mathbf{U}
is generated by

S(a)-\epsilon(a)
, and by Corollary
2.14
,
we indeed have

\varphi\triangleleft(S(a)-\epsilon(a))=0.

By uniqueness of the ZSF, we thus conclude that our ZSF
equals the one from
[
42
]
up to a scalar.

Remark 5.10

(Noumi, Sugitani,
\mathsf{DIII}
)
.

The authors of
[
33
]
use the matrix
J
to
define a coideal subalgebra
U^{\mathrm{tw}}_{q}(\mathfrak{k})
, which by
[
22
, §6]

(more specifically, Lemma 6.3) is a right coideal
subalgebra.
Therefore, there is an automorphism of
\mathbf{U}

mapping
U^{\mathrm{tw}}_{q}(\mathfrak{k})
to

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c}(a)}
for a suitable choice of
a
.
The ZSFs that are then
considered are elements
f\in\mathcal{A}
such that

\displaystyle\forall a\in U^{\mathrm{tw}}_{q}(\mathfrak{k}):\qquad a\cdot f

\displaystyle=\epsilon(a)f,

\displaystyle\forall b\in\ad(K_{\rho})(U^{\mathrm{tw}}_{q}(\mathfrak{k})^{*}):\qquad f\cdot b

\displaystyle=\epsilon(b)f.

An argument analogous to Remark
5.9
shows that

(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Phi^\lambda_\epsilon)=CP_{\lambda}.

Note that from the reflection matrix
J
from
[
33
]
it can be shown that
the twisted Hopf algebra
U^{\mathrm{tw}}_{q}(\mathfrak{k})
, which is just our
coideal subalgebra
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
,
is generated by the elements of the matrix

S(L^{+})JS(L^{-})^{T}
.
In the correct entry, this matrix contains (up to scalars) the elements

B_{n}K_{n-1}K_{n}
and
B_{n-1}K_{n-1}^{-1}K_{n}^{-1}
(and also
K_{n-1}K_{n}
)
for the parameters
c_{n-1}=c_{n}=-\frac{a_{n-1}}{a_{n}}

(to show this, the conventions of
[
14
, §8.5.2]
were used, with the caveat that
the Chevalley map
\omega
from
[
18
, §3.2]
has to be applied afterwards
to match conventions).
Consequently,
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
is conjugate to
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c}(1),0}
.

In Lemma
5.11
we use the identification of zonal spherical functions with orthogonal polynomials to obtain an explicit description of the bilinear form
h\circ\Xi_{\epsilon}:E^{\epsilon}\otimes E^{\epsilon}\to k
from Proposition
2.21
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\varphi_{\lambda}\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}
for all
\lambda\in 2L^{+}
be nonzero zonal
spherical functions.
Assume that
((-\rho)\rhd\Res(\varphi_\lambda))_{\lambda\in 2L^{+}}
is a family of orthogonal polynomials with respect to
??
, a
k
-valued distribution over
L
, i.e.

\forall\mu,\lambda\in 2L^{+}:\quad\ct(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_\lambda)\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_\mu)}\triangledown)=\delta_{\lambda,\nu}h_{\mu}

with
h_{\lambda}\neq 0
(
\lambda\in L
).
Then for all
\psi,\phi\in E^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}
we have

h\circ\Xi_{\epsilon}(\phi,\psi)=\ct(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\psi)\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi)}\triangledown)/\ct(\triangledown).

Proof.

By
[
25
, Theorem 4.2]
,
h\circ\Xi_{\epsilon}

factors through
-\rho\triangleright\Res(\cdot)
.
Consequently, there is a distribution
\triangledown^{\prime}
such that

h(\Xi(\phi,\psi))=\ct(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi)\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\psi)}\triangledown^{\prime}).

The maps
l^{\prime}:f\mapsto\ct(f\triangledown^{\prime})
and

l:f\mapsto\ct(f\triangledown)
are both elements of

k[2L]^{*}
.
Let
\lambda\in(2L^{+})\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, then

0=h(\Xi(\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{0}))=\overline{\varphi_{0}(1)}\ct(\triangledown^{\prime}(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\varphi_\lambda))=\overline{\varphi_{0}(1)}l^{\prime}(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_\lambda))

as
\phi_{0}
is a constant.
Similarly,
l(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_\lambda))=0
.
We thus see that
l,l^{\prime}
have the same kernel of
codimension 1: the span of
\varphi_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\neq 0
.
Consequently,
l,l^{\prime}
span the same 1-dimensional
vector space, so they are linearly dependent.
Since both are non-zero, they are proportional.
Since
l,l^{\prime}
depend injectively on
\triangledown,\triangledown^{\prime}
, we obtain that
\triangledown,\triangledown^{\prime}

differ by a non-zero scalar.
Evaluation of
l,l^{\prime}
at 1 gives that scalar.
∎

6.
Orthogonal Polynomials

In this section, we associate to each quantum commutative triple
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
, a family of vector-valued orthogonal polynomials. We are interested in the general properties of these associated polynomials, which include:

(i)

highest order terms (Proposition
6.1
);

(ii)

orthogonality relations (Proposition
6.6
);

(iii)

matrix valued interpretations (Definition
6.10
);

(iv)

q
-difference equations (Theorem
6.17
);

(v)

q\mapsto q^{-1}
invariance (Theorem
6.19
);

(vi)

recurrence relations (Lemma
6.21
).

Properties (i) and (ii) will be crucial for identifying the associated vector-valued polynomials with known families of vector-valued orthogonal polynomials.

6.1.
Leading Terms

From now on we will assume that
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
and
that
V=W
, i.e. that our MSF consist of square
matrices that are endomorphisms of the same
representation space.
This allows us to define

\Phi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}\in E^{\gamma}(\lambda)
as the unique
MSF satisfying
\Phi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}(1)=1
as in Lemma
3.35
.

Let
\varpi_{1},\dots\varpi_{r}
be a basis of
2L
made up of dominant
elements.
For
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}\varpi_{i}\in X^{+}(\epsilon)
we
define
\varphi_{\lambda}\in E^{\epsilon}(\lambda)
to be the ZSF such
that
-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_\lambda)=e^{\lambda}+\lot
(this is possible by
[
25
, Lemma 4.1]
,
whose proof can be extended to cases where

L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
is not spanned by
\Sigma
).
We also define

\psi_{\lambda}:=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\varphi_{\varpi_{i}}^{n_{i}}.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}(\epsilon)
. We have

\psi_{\lambda}\in\varphi_{\lambda}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in X^{+}(\epsilon)\\
\mu<\lambda\end{subarray}}k\varphi_{\mu},\qquad\varphi_{\lambda}\in\psi_{\lambda}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in X^{+}(\epsilon)\\
\mu<\lambda\end{subarray}}k\psi_{\mu}.

Proof.

Note that the product of two matrix elements is a matrix element for
the tensor product of the representations.
As such,
\psi_{\lambda}
is a matrix element of

L(\varpi_{1})^{\otimes n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes L(\varpi_{n})^{\otimes n_{r}}=L(\lambda)\oplus\bigoplus_{\mu<\lambda}L(\mu)^{m_{\mu}}

for some multiplicities
m_{\mu}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Therefore, by the proof of Lemma
3.35
,
\phi_{\lambda}
can be
expressed as claimed with an arbitrary constant

C
.
But since the leading terms of
-\rho\rhd\Res(\varphi_\lambda)

and
-\rho\triangleright\Res(\psi_\lambda)

both equal
e^{\lambda}
, this constant
C
is one.

The reverse equation follows from the triangularity and the fact that
the dominance order on
X^{+}
has finite down-sets.
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

We have
E^{\epsilon}=k[\varphi_{\varpi_{1}},\dots\varphi_{\varpi_{r}}]
.

Recall the elementary spherical function
\Phi^{b}_{\lambda}
from Lemma
3.35
and the set
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
from Definition
3.44
. By
[
37
, Proposition 4.2]
it follows that each
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
has a unique decomposition
\lambda=b+\mu
with
\mu\in X^{+}(\epsilon)
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Assume that
(\Phi^{b}_{\gamma})_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
is linearly independent over
E^{\epsilon}
.
For

\lambda=b+\mu\in X^{+}(\gamma)

(with
b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
), we define

\Psi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}:=\psi_{\mu}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}.

Then for every
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
, there is
C\neq 0
such that

\Psi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}\in C\Phi^{\gamma}_{\lambda}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\lambda\\
\nu\in X^{+}(\gamma)\end{subarray}}k\Phi^{\nu}_{\gamma},\qquad\Phi^{\gamma}_{\lambda}\in C^{-1}\Psi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\lambda\\
\nu\in X^{+}(\gamma)\end{subarray}}k\Psi^{\nu}_{\gamma}.

Proof.

By a proof very similar to Proposition
6.1
, we see that
for every
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
there is
C\in k
such that

(34)

\Psi^{\lambda}_{\gamma}=C\Phi^{\gamma}_{\lambda}+\sum_{\nu<\lambda}k\Phi^{\nu}_{\gamma}.

It remains to show that
C\neq 0
.
(
34
) shows that
\Psi^{\nu}_{\gamma}
(
\nu\leq\lambda
) are contained in the span of
\Phi^{\nu}_{\gamma}
(
\nu\leq\lambda
). Since they are also linearly independent (since we assumed

(\Phi^{6}.OrthogonalPolynomialsb_{\gamma})_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
to be
E^{\epsilon}
-linearly independent) and are contained in a finite-dimensional vector space, they also form a basis. The change-of-basis matrix between these
two bases is triangular by (
34
). Consequently, the diagonal entries, i.e. the
C
’s, are nonzero, and its inverse is also triangular
with the corresponding
C^{-1}
’s on the diagonal.
∎

Proposition
6.4
is a quantum analog of
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
and will show that the assumptions of Lemma
6.3
are satisfied for the specializable parameters of Definition
3.27
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}(\epsilon)
,
\mu\in X^{+}(\gamma)
, let
\mathrm{span}_{k}\{v_{\mathrm{sph}}\}=V(\epsilon)\subseteq L(\lambda)
.
Then, the
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-equivariant embedding
\eta:V(\gamma)\to L(\lambda)\otimes L(\mu)
defined by

v\mapsto v_{\mathrm{sph}}\otimes v\qquad\text{for}\qquad v\in V(\gamma)

composed with the Cartan projection is nonzero.

Proof.

Write
\pi_{\lambda+\mu}:L(\lambda)\otimes L(\mu)\to L(\lambda+\mu)
for the Cartan projection on the quantum group and at
q=1
.
Recall the subring
\mathbf{A}\subseteq k
and the notion of a specializable basis, as introduced in Section
3.4
. Let
\mathcal{B}(\mu)
and
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
be specializable bases for
L(\mu)
and
L(\lambda)
, respectively. By Theorem
3.28
, we may assume that
v_{\mathrm{sph}}\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
and that there is a specialiazble basis
\mathcal{B}(\gamma)\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mu)
of
V(\gamma)
. Let

\eta_{\mathbf{A}}:V(\gamma)_{\mathbf{A}}\to L(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes L(\mu)_{\mathbf{A}}

be the unique
\mathbf{A}
-linear map with

b\mapsto v_{\mathrm{sph}}\otimes b,\qquad\qquad b\in\mathcal{B}(\gamma).

The map
\cl\circ\eta_{\mathbf{A}}
is then the nonzero map described in the proof of
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
. As the projection
\pi_{\lambda+\mu}
commutes with taking the classical limit, cf.
[
12
, Theorem 5.15]
, it follows from
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
that

\pi_{\lambda+\mu}\circ\cl\circ\eta_{\mathbf{A}}=\cl\circ\pi_{\lambda+\mu}\circ\eta_{\mathbf{A}}\neq 0.

The theorem then follows.
∎

As a straightforward consequence of Proposition
6.4
, we show in Lemma
6.5
, under its assumptions, that the assumptions of Lemma
6.3
are satisfied.

Lemma 6.5
.

E^{\gamma}
is a free left module over
E^{\epsilon}
with basis
(\Phi^{b}_{\gamma})_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
.

Proof.

Let
\phi_{b}\in E^{\epsilon}
for each
b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
and suppose that
\sum_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\phi_{b}\Phi_{b}=0
. Recall that, up to a scalar multiple,
\Res(\phi_b)=v_{\lambda_{b}}+\lot
. Let
b_{0}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
be such that
v_{\lambda_{b_{0}}}
is maximal and
\phi_{b_{0}}\neq 0
. Using Proposition
6.4
, up to a nonzero scalar multiple,
