\phi_{b_{0}}\Phi_{b_{0}}=\Phi_{b_{0}+\lambda_{b_{0}}}+\lot
. By maximality of
b_{0}
this shows that
\sum_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\phi_{b}\Phi_{b}\neq 0
, a contradiction.
∎

6.2.
Intermediate Macdonald Polynomials and Their Vector Versions

In this subsection we use the identification of the zonal spherical functions with orthogonal polynomials to deduce the matrix valued orthogonality weight for the matrix valued spherical functions.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
\triangledown
be a
k
-valued distribution over
L
such that

h(\Xi_{\epsilon}(\Phi,\Psi))=\ct((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Phi))\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Psi)}\triangledown)

for
\Phi,\Psi\in E^{\epsilon}
.
Expand
\Phi\in E^{\gamma}
as

\sum_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\phi_{b}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}

for
\phi_{b}\in E^{\epsilon}
, we define
\underline{\Phi}:=(\phi_{b})_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
. Let

M_{b,b^{\prime}}:=(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Xi_\gamma(\Phi^b_\gamma,\Phi^{b'}_\gamma)),

then

h(\Xi_{\gamma}(\Phi,\Psi))=\ct((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\underline{\Phi}))^{T}M\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\underline{\Psi})}\triangledown)

for
\Phi,\Psi\in E^{\gamma}
.

Proof.

Expand
\Phi,\Psi\in E^{\gamma}
as

\Phi=\sum_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\phi_{b}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma},\qquad\Psi=\sum_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\psi_{b}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}.

Then we have

\displaystyle h(\Xi_{\gamma}(\Phi,\Psi))

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}h(\Xi_{\gamma}(\phi_{b}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma},\psi_{b^{\prime}}\Phi^{b^{\prime}}_{\gamma}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}h\Big(\phi_{b}\Xi_{\gamma}(\Phi^{b}_{\gamma},\Phi^{b^{\prime}}_{\gamma})S(K_{-2\rho}\triangleright\psi_{b^{\prime}})\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}h\Big(\Xi_{\epsilon}\big(\phi_{b}\Xi_{\gamma}(\Phi^{b}_{\gamma},\Phi^{b^{\prime}}_{\gamma}),\psi_{b^{\prime}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\ct((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi_b))M_{b,b^{\prime}}\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\psi_{b'})}\triangledown)

\displaystyle=\ct((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\underline{\Phi}))^{T}M\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\underline{\Psi})}\triangledown)

as claimed.
∎

In the following, we recall some notions from
[
40
]
needed to understand Corollary
6.7
. Let
J\subseteq I
, then we denote

2L_{+,J}=\{\lambda\in 2L\,:\,\forall j\in J:\quad\langle\lambda,\alpha_{j}\rangle\geq 0\}

the set of
J
-dominant elements. The
J
-dominant elements label

W_{J}
-invariant monomials

m_{J,\lambda}=\sum_{\mu\in W_{J}\lambda}e^{\mu}\qquad\lambda\in L_{+,J}

and distinguished
W_{J}
-invariant polynomials

P_{J,\lambda}=m_{J,\lambda}+\lot\qquad\lambda\in 2L_{+,J}

known as the
Intermediate Macdonald polynomials
, cf.
[
40
, Definition 4.7]
.
They generalize the symmetric and non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials, alternatively Intermediate Macdonald polynomials can be interpreted as vector-valued Macdonald polynomials, cf.
[
40
, §6]
. We refer the reader to
[
40
]
for a detailed exposition.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
J
be a subset of the simple roots of
\Sigma
and let

W_{J}\leq W_{\Sigma}
be the corresponding parabolic subgroup of the restricted Weyl group. Define
e_{b}:=(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Phi^b_\gamma)
for

b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
and let

\Gamma:k[2L]^{W_{J}}\to k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}\otimes\operatorname{span}_{k}\left\{e_{b}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)\right\}

be a
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linear isomorphism. Equip the target space
of
\Gamma
with the following monomial basis:
m_{\lambda}\otimes e_{b}
for
\lambda\in 2L_{+},b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
(
m_{\lambda}
as in
[
29
, §5.3]
),
whose order is inherited from the dominance order of
X^{+}(\gamma)
by the isomorphism
(b,\lambda)\mapsto b+\lambda
.

Let
t:2L_{+,J}\to X^{+}(\gamma)
be a bijection
with
t^{-1}
monotonic, such that
\Gamma^{-1}
is triangular (with
nonzero
“diagonal”
terms) with respect to
t^{-1}
.
Assume that there is a triangular
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linear automorphism
C
of
k[2L]^{W_{J}}
such that

\forall b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma):\quad\frac{1}{\#W_{\Sigma}}\sum_{w\in W_{\Sigma}}w\frac{\Gamma^{-1}(e_{b})\overline{C(\Gamma^{-1}(e_{b^{\prime}}))}}{\Delta^{0}}=M_{b,b^{\prime}}

(left-hand side similar to
[
40
, Lemma 6.1]
, right-hand side as in Proposition
6.6
), where
\Delta^{0}
as in
[
29
, §5.1.25]
is chosen in conventions such that
\triangledown

from Proposition
6.6
coincides with
\triangledown

from
[
29
, §5.1.26]
.

Then for every

\lambda\in 2L_{+,J}
, there is a nonzero constant
C_{\lambda}
such
that
\Gamma(P_{J,\lambda})=C_{\lambda}(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Phi^{t(\lambda)}_\gamma)
.

Proof.

As a consequence of Lemma
6.5
and of
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linearity and injectivity we
thus have
(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(E^\gamma)\subseteq\im(\Gamma)
. Let
now
\lambda,\mu\in X^{+}(\gamma)
and write

\Phi:=\Phi^{\gamma}_{\lambda}
and
\Psi:=\Phi^{\mu}_{\gamma}
. We now
have

\displaystyle h(\Xi_{\gamma}(\Phi,\Psi))

\displaystyle=\ct((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\underline{\Phi}))^{T}M\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\underline{\Psi})}\triangledown)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\#W_{\Sigma}}\sum_{w\in W_{\Sigma}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\ct((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi_b))\overline{-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi_{b'})}w\Gamma^{-1}(e_{b})\overline{C(\Gamma^{-1}(e_{b^{\prime}}))}\Delta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\ct(\Gamma^{-1}((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi_b))e_{b})\overline{C(\Gamma^{-1}((-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi_{b'}))e_{b^{\prime}}))}\Delta)

\displaystyle=\ct(\Gamma^{-1}(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Phi))\overline{C(\Gamma^{-1}(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Psi)))}\Delta)

\displaystyle=\quantity(\Gamma^{-1}(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Phi)),
C(\Gamma^{-1}(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Phi)))^\circ),

where
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the non-symmetric Macdonald inner product
with weight
\Delta
(see
[
29
, §§5.1.7,5.1.17]
) and

\circ:k[2L]\to k[2L]
is the ring automorphism mapping

q\mapsto q^{-1}
and
e^{\lambda}\mapsto e^{\lambda}
.

As a consequence,
(\Gamma^{-1}((-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Phi^\gamma_\lambda)))_{\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)}

is a sequence of orthogonal polynomials. By Lemma
6.3
, we can expand

(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma)=am_{\lambda}\otimes e_{b}+\lot

for
a\neq 0
. Since
\Gamma^{-1}
is triangular with respect
to
t^{-1}
, so is

C\circ\Gamma^{-1}
.
Since
t^{-1}
is also monotonic, we then have

C\Gamma^{-1}(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma))=a^{\prime}m_{J,t^{-1}(\lambda+b)}+\lot

where
m_{J,\nu}
is defined as in
[
40
, §4.2 p.23]
. The two facts
of orthogonality and leading terms identify our polynomials as
Intermediate Macdonald polynomials.
∎

As a consequence, to establish a link between elementary MSFs for

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}},\gamma)
it is sufficient to match
2L_{+,J}
with the
spherical weights, and to match a
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
basis of
k[2L]^{W_{J}}
with the
E^{\epsilon}
-basis

\left\{\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}\right\}_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
in such a way
that the matrix weights from Proposition
6.6
and

[
40
, Lemma 6.1]
(using
\overline{\cdot}
instead of
*
) match.

6.2.1.
Matrix-Valued Interpretations

In this section we assosiate to each family of matrix-spherical functions a family of matrix valued orthogonal polynomials. This construction generalizes that of
[
2
]
in the quantum case and that of
[
44
]
in the classical case. In this section this viewpoint is taken and we study the properties of the associated matrix valued orthogonal polynomials. In particular, we show that the polynomials are invariant under the involution
q\mapsto q^{-1}
and are eigenfunctions of matrix valued
q
-difference operators.

Notation 6.8
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}=k^{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)\times\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
,
the space of square matrices over
k
whose rows and
columns are indexed by the finite set
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
.

Remark 6.9
.

Recall that we assumed that for each
b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
the elementary spherical functions
\Phi_{\gamma}^{b}
from Lemma
3.35
are normalized with
\Phi_{\gamma}^{b}(1)=1
. Using Lemma
6.3
, we now assume that
\Phi_{\gamma}^{b+\lambda}
is normalized so that
\Phi_{\gamma}^{b+\lambda}=\varphi_{\lambda}\Phi_{\gamma}^{b}+\lot
,
and
\varphi_{\lambda}
such that
-\rho\,\triangleright\,\Res(\varphi_\lambda)=m_{\lambda}+\lot
.

For each spherical function
\Phi\in E^{\gamma}
, recall the notation
\underline{\Phi}\in(k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}})^{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
, as introduced in Proposition
6.6
.

Definition 6.10
.

For
\lambda\in 2L^{+}
define
??\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
to be

Q_{\lambda}:=\quantity((-\rho)\triangleright\Res\qty(\underline{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma}))_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)},

i.e. as the matrix-valued polynomial whose
b
-th
column is
(-\rho)\triangleright\Res\quantity(\underline{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma})
.

Remark 6.11
.

Lemma
6.3
and the
choice of normalisation show that the diagonal entries of
Q_{\lambda}
have leading term
m_{\lambda}
and that the off-diagonal
(b,b^{\prime})
-th entry has
leading exponent
<\lambda+b^{\prime}-b
.

Definition 6.12
.

Define the symmetric matrix-valued bilinear form

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}

by

(35)

\langle A,B\rangle_{b,b^{\prime}}:=\mathrm{ct}\big((A^{T}M\overline{B})_{b,b^{\prime}}\triangledown\big),\quad\text{where}\quad A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}.

Here, the involution
\overline{\,\cdot\,}
is defined component-wise.

The matrix valued polynomials
Q_{\lambda}
are the objects of interest of this subsection.

Proposition 6.13
.

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a
matrix-valued symmetric bilinear form.

Proof.

Let
F,G\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
and

A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
, then

\displaystyle\langle FA,GB\rangle

\displaystyle=\ct((FA)^{T}M\overline{GB}\triangledown)=\ct(A^{T}F^{T}M\overline{G}\overline{B}\triangledown)

\displaystyle=A^{T}\ct(F^{T}M\overline{G}\triangledown)B=A^{T}\langle F,G\rangle B

(recall that
\overline{\cdot}
is
k
-linear and
in particular does nothing to constant matrices).
Combined with the fact that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is
k
-bilinear, it therefore is

\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
-bilinear,

Furthermore, for
F,G\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
we have

\langle F,G\rangle=\ct(F^{T}M\overline{G}\triangledown)=\ct(\overline{G}^{T}M^{T}F\triangledown)^{T}=\ct(G^{T}\overline{M}^{T}\overline{F}\triangledown)^{T}.

From Proposition
3.61
we find that

\overline{M}^{T}=M
, so that the above equals

=\langle G,F\rangle^{T}.\qed

Using Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
2.21
, we conclude the following theorem.

Theorem 6.14
.

The matrix-valued polynomials
\{Q_{\lambda}\,:\,\lambda\in 2L^{+}\}
span

\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}

\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
-linearly and satisfy

\langle Q_{\lambda},Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle=\delta_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}D_{\lambda},\qquad\text{where}\qquad\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in 2L^{+}

and where
D_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}
is diagonal and invertible.

Using the Cartan decomposition, we continue by describing a family of matrix valued
q
-difference operators for which
\{Q_{\lambda}\,:\,\lambda\in 2L^{+}\}
are simultaneous eigenfunctions. Recall that

\mathbf{U}^{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}=\{\Omega\in\mathbf{U}:\Omega b=b\Omega\qquad\text{for all }b\in\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}\}.

By Schur’s Lemma and multiplicity freeness, for each
\Omega\in\mathbf{U}^{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}
and
\lambda\in X^{+}(\gamma)
, there exists a scalar
C^{\gamma}_{\Omega}
such that
\Omega\triangleright\Phi^{\gamma}_{\lambda}=C^{\gamma}_{\Omega}\Phi^{\gamma}_{\lambda}
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{d}\}
and let
V_{\gamma}:\big(E^{\epsilon}\big)^{\oplus d\times d}\to\big(E^{\gamma}\big)^{\oplus d}
denote the unique linear isomorphism with:

(36)

\quantity(\phi_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}\mapsto\quantity(\sum_{j} \phi_{j,i}\Phi_\gamma^{b_j})_{1\leq i\leq d},

such that

Q_{\lambda}=-\rho\triangleright\Res\quantity(V_\gamma^{-1}\qty((\Phi^{b_i+\lambda})_{1\le i\le d}))

Definition 6.15
.

For each
\Omega\in\mathbf{U}^{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}
we let
\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}
denote the unique operator making the following diagram commute:

(37)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(E^{\gamma}\big)^{\oplus d}\quad}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quad\qquad\big(E^{\gamma}\big)^{\oplus d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(E^{k}\big)^{\oplus d\times d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(E^{k}\big)^{\oplus d\times d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 16.38895pt(\Phi_{\gamma}^{b_{1}},\dots,\Phi_{\gamma}^{b_{d}})\mapsto(\Omega\triangleright\Phi_{\gamma}^{b_{1}},\dots,\Omega\triangleright\Phi_{\gamma}^{b_{d}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\gamma}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\rho\triangleright\Res(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\thicksim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\rho\triangleright\Res(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\thicksim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}}

Write
\mathcal{D}_{\mu}:=\mathcal{D}_{c_{\mu}}
for the operator obtained from (
37
)
and the distinguished central element
c_{\mu}\in Z(\mathbf{U})
from
Lemma
3.14
.

By the construction of the matrix-valued orthogonal polynomials and
[
13
, §7.1.19]
, we conclude that:

Corollary 6.16
.

The matrix-valued orthogonal polynomials
\{Q_{\lambda}:\lambda\in 2L^{+}\}
satisfy the relation

\mathcal{D}_{\mu}(Q_{\lambda})=Q_{\lambda}\sum_{\nu\in X}\diag\quantity(q^{(\lambda+b_1+\rho)\cdot\nu},
\dots,
q^{(\lambda+b_d+\rho)\cdot\nu})\dim(L(\mu)_{\nu})\quad\lambda\in 2L^{+},\mu\in X^{+},2h_{\mu}\in Y.

Using Proposition
4.24
, Equation (
36
),
it follows that conjugation with the elementary MSF from
\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)
yield a
q
-difference equation for the matrix-valued orthogonal polynomials
\{Q_{\lambda}:\lambda\in 2L^{+}\}
.

Theorem 6.17
.

For each
\Omega\in\mathbf{U}^{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}
the matrix-valued orthogonal polynomials
\{Q_{\lambda}:\lambda\in 2L^{+}\}
satisfy the matrix-valued
q
-differential equation

(38)

Q_{\lambda}\Lambda_{\Omega,\lambda}=\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(Q_{\lambda})

where
\Lambda_{\lambda,\Omega}
is a diagonal matrix.

Thus, the matrix-valued orthogonal polynomials diagonalize a commutative algebra

k\langle\mathcal{D}_{\mu}:\mu\in X^{+},2h_{\mu}\in Y\rangle

of difference-reflection operators.
From Proposition
4.27
, we know that

c_{\mu}
has order
-2h_{\mu}
,
which shows that
\mathcal{D}_{\mu}
is a difference
operator of order
2h_{\mu}
.
This order needs to be understood modulo the annihilator
of
2L
, as we know that all entries of
Q_{\lambda}
are
contained in
k[2L]
.

Recall the anti-linear involution
{}^{0}:k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}\to k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
introduced in Section
3.6
and the involution
\sigma=\tau\circ\tau_{0}
also as introduced in Section
3.6
. We extend
{}^{0}:k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}\to k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
to
{}^{0}:\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)}\otimes k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
component-wise. In the case
V(\gamma)\cong V(\gamma)^{\sigma}
, we show that
Q_{\lambda}=Q_{\lambda}^{0}
.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
\mu\in X^{+}(\gamma)
, let
p,i
be a
\mathbf{B}
-linear
projection and injection with
p\circ i=\id_{V(\gamma)}
for
V(\gamma)
and
L(\mu)
,
and let
v\in V(\gamma),f\in V(\gamma)^{*}
such that

i(v)
and
f\circ p
are
\imath
bar-invariant.
Then

(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f\Phi^\mu_\gamma v)=w_{0}\quantity((-\rho)\triangleright\Res(fT_{w_\bullet})\Phi^\mu_{\sigma(\gamma)}T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}v))^{\circ},

where we take
\Phi^{\mu}_{\gamma}
and
\Phi^{\mu}_{\sigma(\gamma)}
to be defined by
(p,i)

and
(p\circ\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{-1}\circ i)
.

Proof.

Let
h\in Y
, then

\displaystyle(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f\Phi^\mu_\gamma v)(h)

\displaystyle=c^{\mu}_{f\circ p,i(v)}(K_{h-\rho})

\displaystyle=\overline{c^{\mu}_{\overline{f\circ p},\overline{i(v)}}(K_{-h+\rho})}

\displaystyle=\overline{c^{\mu}_{fT_{w_{\bullet}}\circ p\circ\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{-1}i(T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}v)}(K_{-w_{0}h-\rho})}

\displaystyle=\overline{fT_{w_{\bullet}}\Phi^{\mu}_{\sigma(\gamma)}(K_{-w_{0}h-\rho})T_{w_{\bullet}}v}

\displaystyle=\quantity((-\rho)\triangleright\Res(fT_{w_\bullet}
\Phi^\mu_{\sigma(\gamma)}T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}v))^{\circ}(w_{0}h)

by Lemmas
3.56
and
3.57
.
∎

Theorem 6.19
.

Let
\gamma\cong\sigma(\gamma)
, then for each
\lambda\in 2L^{+}
we have
Q_{\lambda}=Q_{\lambda}^{0}
.

Proof.

As in Proposition
3.53
let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V(\gamma)
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in V(\gamma)^{*}
be
dual bases that are
\imath
bar-invariant under every
injection and projection used to define the functions used here.

For
j,k=1,\dots,n
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)}(Q_{\lambda})_{b^{\prime},b}w_{0}\quantity((-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f_jT_{w_\bullet}\Phi^{b'}_\gamma T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}v_k))^{\circ}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Lem\ref{lem-msf-0}\,\&\,\gamma\cong\sigma(\gamma)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)}(Q_{\lambda})_{b^{\prime},b}(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f_j\Phi^{b'}_\gamma v_k)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Def\ref{def:matrixtovect}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(-\rho)\triangleright\Res\quantity(\sum_{b'\in\mathfrak{B}^+(\gamma)} \underline{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma}_{b'}
f_j\Phi^{b'}_\gamma v_k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f_j\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma v_k)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Lem\ref{lem-msf-0}\,\&\,\gamma\cong\sigma(\gamma)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w_{0}\quantity((-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f_jT_{w_\bullet})\Phi^{b+\lambda}_{\sigma(\gamma)}T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}v_k))^{\circ}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)}w_{0}(Q_{\lambda})^{\circ}_{b^{\prime},b}w_{0}\quantity((-\rho)\triangleright\Res(f_jT_{w_\bullet}\Phi^{b'}_\gamma T_{w_\bullet}^{-1}v_k))^{\circ}.

Since the
\Phi^{b^{\prime}}_{\gamma}
are linearly independent, and
since
\Res
is injective, we conclude

w_{0}Q_{\lambda}^{\circ}=Q_{\lambda},

which implies the claim as
w_{0}Q_{\lambda}=Q_{\lambda}
.

∎

Theorem
6.19
is the marix-valued analogue of the
q\to q^{-1}
invariance of Macdonald polynomials, which is shown in
[
29
, §5.3.2]
. Moreover, in the cases we know the
\{Q_{\lambda}:\lambda\in 2L^{+}\}
to be Macdonald polynomials, cf.
[
26
]

[
31
]
and Section
7.1
(in these cases
V(\gamma)\cong V(\gamma)^{\sigma}
), this gives a new proof and interpretation of the
q\to q^{-1}
invariance.

Lastly, we show the existence of recurrence relations. Recall that
\{\varpi_{1},\dots\varpi_{r}\}
denotes a generating set of the monoid
2L^{+}
.

Notation 6.20
.

For
\lambda\in X^{+}
, let
\Lambda(\lambda)
denote the weights occurring in the module
L(\lambda)
.

Lemma 6.21
.

For each
1\leq i\leq r
and
\lambda\in 2L^{+}
we have

-\rho\triangleright\Res(\phi_{\varpi_i})Q_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu\in\Lambda(\varpi_{i})}a_{i,\mu,\lambda}Q_{\mu+\lambda},\qquad\qquad a_{i,\mu,\lambda}\in k.

Proof.

Lemma
6.3
shows that
-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_{\varpi_i})Q_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu\in X^{+}(\gamma)}a_{i,\lambda,\mu}Q_{\mu}
. The scalar
a_{i,\lambda,\mu}
is zero if
\mu
is not a highest weight contained in the tensor product decomposition
L(\varpi_{i})\otimes L(\lambda+b)
, for some
b\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
. As the highest weights in the tensor product decomposition
L(\varpi_{i})\otimes L(b+\lambda)
are contained in
\lambda+b+\Lambda(\varpi_{i})
, cf.
[
11
, §24.4]
, the result follows.
∎

Remark 6.22
.

From Proposition
6.4
, we furthermore observe that
a_{i,\lambda+\varpi_{i},\lambda}\neq 0
.

Since

k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}=k[-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_{\varpi_1}),\dots-\rho\triangleright\Res(\varphi_{\varpi_r})],

the
E^{\epsilon}
-module structure of
E^{\gamma}
can be understood through the coefficients
a_{i,\lambda,\mu}
.

Part II
Examples

In Part
I
, a general theory of quantum matrix-spherical functions was developed. In particular, Section
6
associated to each quantum commutative triple a family of vector-valued orthogonal polynomials. We now explicit study of these polynomials. Specifically, Section
7.1
treats the cases corresponding to Satake diagrams of type
\mathsf{BII}n
and
\mathsf{CII}{n,1}
; Section
7.2
treats type
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
; and Section
7.3
treats types
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
-group case, and
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
.

7.
Examples

We now provide some further examples of MSF that are not ZSFs that we
can nevertheless equate to polynomials within the Macdonald framework, polynomials with a slightly relaxed symmetry requirement:
the Intermediate Macdonald polynomials from
[
40
]
.
This identification will be achieved using Corollary
2.20
and the fact that the inner product
h\circ\Xi_{\gamma}
can be expressed as an inner product with a matrix
weight that also occurs when describing Intermediate Macdonald polynomials.

7.1.
Integrable Small
\mathbf{B}
-Type:
\mathsf{BII}
and
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}

We start with two examples of what for groups is called a small
K
-type:
two integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-types.
(We show later that these two examples are the only two that are not known already.)
For this case, as in

[
30
]
, we expect to obtain symmetric Macdonald polynomials.

7.1.1.
Notation

Let
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
correspond to the following
Satake diagrams:
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
or
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7}{0.0}{0.0}{0.7}{80.35747pt}{-0.22075pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}.

Let
X
be the weight lattice and let
Y
be the coroot lattice. Let

\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
be the roots,
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
the coroots,
and
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n}
the fundamental weights. Moreover,
we pick
\epsilon_{1}=\dots=\epsilon_{n-1}=2
and
\epsilon_{n}=1
, or vice-versa.

In case of
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
we also assume that

n>2
; otherwise, it is identical to
\mathsf{BII}_{2}
.

The subalgebra
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
is then generated by

E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}
for
i\neq 1
(
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
) or
i\neq 2

(
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
).

The coideal subalgebra is then generated by
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
and

\displaystyle\mathsf{BII}_{n}:\quad B_{1}

\displaystyle=F_{1}+c\ad(E_{2}\cdots E_{n}^{2}\dots E_{2})(E_{1})K_{1}^{-1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}:\quad B_{2}

\displaystyle=F_{2}+c\ad(E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3})(E_{2})K_{2}^{-1}.

Note that these elements lie in the weight spaces

\mathbf{U}_{-\alpha_{1}}\oplus\mathbf{U}_{2\omega_{1}-\alpha_{1}},\qquad\mathbf{U}_{-\alpha_{2}}\oplus\mathbf{U}_{\omega_{2}-\alpha_{2}},

respectively.
The restricted root system is

\displaystyle\mathsf{BII}_{n}:\quad\Sigma

\displaystyle=\left\{\pm\omega_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}:\quad\Sigma

\displaystyle=\left\{\pm\frac{\omega_{2}}{2},\pm\omega_{2}\right\}.

Consequently,
2L
is spanned by
\omega_{1}
and

\omega_{2}
, respectively.

7.1.2.
Classical Branching Rules

For the classical branching rules, we consult

[
37
, Table 2]
.
In lines B.4 and B.7.6, we find that
for
\gamma
corresponding to the simple module of highest
weight (in the notation of loc.cit.)

s\varpi_{n}
or
s\varpi_{n-1}
, or

s\varpi_{1}
, respectively, we have

X^{+}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}s\omega_{n}+\mathbb{N}_{0}\omega_{1}&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
s\omega_{1}+\mathbb{N}_{0}\omega_{2}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}.\end{cases}

Moreover, we have

\begin{cases}L(s\omega_{n})|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\bigoplus_{r=0}^{s}L_{\mathfrak{k}}(r\varpi_{n-1}+(s-r)\varpi_{n})&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
L(s\omega_{1})|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\bigoplus_{r=0}^{s}L_{\mathfrak{k}}(r\varpi_{1}+(s-r)\varpi_{1}^{\prime})&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}.\end{cases}

To further examine how these
\mathfrak{k}
-representations restrict
to
\mathfrak{m}
, we note that for
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
we have

\mathfrak{m}\cong\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(2n-4)
, where
the first summand is the diagonal subspace of two copies of

\mathfrak{su}(2)
.
Therefore, the branch rule
\mathfrak{k}\to\mathfrak{m}
may
be obtained in that case, by branching the second summand

\mathfrak{sp}(2n-2)
of
\mathfrak{k}
into

\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(2n-4)
, and then
taking the diagonal of the two
\mathfrak{su}(2)
summands (i.e.
considering the tensor product decomposition).

As such, from Lines B.5 and B.7.6, we obtain

\begin{cases}L_{\mathfrak{k}}(r\varpi_{n-1}+(s-r)\varpi_{n})|_{\mathfrak{m}}=\bigoplus_{t=0}^{r\wedge(s-r)}L_{\mathfrak{m}}(t\omega_{n-2}+(s-t)\omega_{n-1})&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
L_{\mathfrak{k}}(r\varpi_{1}+(s-r)\varpi_{1}^{\prime})|_{\mathfrak{m}}=\bigoplus_{t=0}^{s-r}\bigoplus_{u=\absolutevalue{r-t}}^{r+t}L_{\mathfrak{m}}((u-s+r+t)\omega_{1}+(s-r-t)\omega_{3})&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}.\end{cases}

We obtain precisely one summand if
r=0
or
r=s
in the first case,
and
r=s
in the second case.
Moreover we see that in the first case, there is exactly one
summand isomorphic to
L_{\mathfrak{m}}(s\omega_{n-1})
(namely
t=0
); and in the
second case, exactly one summand is isomorphic to
L_{\mathfrak{m}}(s\omega_{1})
(namely
t=s-r
and
u=s
).

This shows that
L(s\omega_{n})|_{\mathfrak{m}}
contains

s+1
copies of
L_{\mathfrak{m}}(s\omega_{n})
(
=L_{\mathfrak{m}}(s\omega_{n-1})
) in the first case,
and
L(s\omega_{1})|_{\mathfrak{m}}
contains
s+1
copies of

L_{\mathfrak{m}}(s\omega_{1})
in the second case.

Remark 7.1
.

There is an error in
[
37
, Table 2, Line B.5]
.
One of the generators is given as
(\omega_{n},-\varpi_{n-1})
, which corresponds to a branch rule for
the
\mathfrak{so}(2n+2)
and
\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
-modules
with highest weights

(a_{1},\dots,a_{n+1})=\quantity(\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}),\qquad(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})=(1,\dots,1,0).

This contradicts the well-known branching rules for this
case, which require all differences
a_{i}-b_{j}
to be
integers.

It seems that this generator should be replaced by

(\omega_{n}+\omega_{n+1},-\varpi_{n-1})
, which corresponds
to

(1,\dots,1,0),\qquad(1,\dots,1,0),

which conforms to the well-known branching rules.

7.1.3.
Describing
V(\gamma)

As is suggested by the previous section, we will now describe
V(\gamma)
as it lies in
L(b):=L(s\omega_{n})
or
L(s\omega_{1})
, respectively.
Moreover, since classically for
K=\operatorname{Spin}(2n)

or
K=\operatorname{Sp}(2)\times\operatorname{Sp}(2n-2)

the subgroup
M
(=normaliser in
K
of a maximal commutative
subalgebra in
\mathfrak{g}^{-\Theta}
) is analytic,
we obtain that the specialisation of
V(\gamma)
is
simple over
\mathfrak{m}
, and hence that

V(\gamma)
is simple over
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
.

It is therefore sufficient to find a
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-highest-weight
vector
v
for
V(\gamma)
.

Notation 7.2
.

For a tuple
J=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in I^{r}
write

\overline{J}:=(i_{r},\dots,i_{1})
and

F_{J}:=F_{i_{1}}\cdots F_{i_{r}}

similarly for
E_{J},B_{J}
(for the latter we interpret

B_{i}=F_{i}
if
i\in I_{\bullet}
).
Let moreover

K_{J}:=K_{i_{1}}\cdots K_{i_{r}}=K_{\sum_{j=1}^{r}\epsilon_{i_{j}}h_{i_{j}}}.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let

V^{\prime}:=\bigoplus_{r=0}^{s}V^{\prime}_{r}:=\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{r=0}^{s}L(s\omega_{n})_{s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}}&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
\bigoplus_{r=0}^{s}L(s\omega_{1})_{s\omega_{1}-r\omega_{2}}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1},\end{cases}\qquad K:=\begin{cases}(n,\dots,1)&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
(2,\dots,n,\dots,1)&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}.\end{cases}

Then
V^{\prime}
is an
s+1
-dimensional vector space
and
E_{J},F_{\overline{J}},B_{\overline{J}}
restrict
to endomorphisms of it.

Proof.

Note that
E_{J}
and
F_{\overline{J}}
have weight

\pm\omega_{1}
for
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
and

\pm\omega_{2}
for
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
.
Moreover,

B_{\overline{J}}\in\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}_{\omega_{1}}\oplus\mathbf{U}_{-\omega_{1}}&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
\mathbf{U}_{\omega_{2}}\oplus\mathbf{U}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{U}_{-\omega_{2}}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}.\end{cases}

As a consequence, these three operators map
V^{\prime}
to itself.
Note that every vector in
v\in V^{\prime}
is a
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-highest weight vector of weight

b:=s\omega_{n}

or
s\omega_{1}
(which we understand to be meant modulo
weights annihilated by all
h_{i}
(
i\in I_{\bullet}
)).

This follows from the fact that
s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}+\alpha_{i}
(
i=2,\dots,n
) lies outside the support of

L(s\omega_{n})
and the same for
s\omega_{1}-r\omega_{2}+\alpha_{i}
(
i=1,3,\dots,n
), so hence
E_{i}v
for

i\in I_{\bullet},v\in V^{\prime}
is zero.

Consequently,
L(b)|_{\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}}
contains

\dim(V^{\prime})
-many simple
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-submodules
of highest weight
\tilde{b}
.
From Remark
7.1
we know that there
should be exactly
s+1
-many.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

E_{J},F_{\overline{J}},K_{J}
acts as a
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
-triple on
V^{\prime}
, i.e. as endomorphisms of
V^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle K_{J}E_{J}K_{J}^{-1}

\displaystyle=q^{2}E_{J}

\displaystyle K_{J}F_{\overline{J}}K_{J}^{-1}

\displaystyle=q^{-2}F_{\overline{J}}

\displaystyle\commutator{E_J}{F_{\overline{J}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{J}-K_{J}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}},

see
[
12
, (4.4.5)]
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.1
.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

If
\eta\in L(b)
is the highest-weight vector, then

E_{J}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta=[r]_{q}[s+1-r]_{q}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta.

In particular
F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta
for
r=0,\dots,s
form a basis of

V^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The equation is a standard identity from the representation
theory of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
and follows by induction.
It shows in particular that the elements

F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta
(
r=0,\dots,s
) are all nonzero.
Since they have different weights, they are linearly independent, and since there are
s+1
of them, they therefore
form a basis.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

B_{\overline{J}}
acts as follows on
V^{\prime}
:

B_{\overline{J}}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta=F_{\overline{J}}^{r+1}\eta+b_{r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta+c_{r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta

where

\displaystyle b_{r}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
-c\quantity(q^{r-1-s}[r]_q + q^{r+3-2n}[s-r]_q)&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}\end{cases}

\displaystyle c_{r}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-c\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}q^{3-2n}[2]_{q}\quantity(1-q^{2r})\quantity(1-q^{2(r-s-1)})&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
-\frac{c^{2}}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}q^{2-2n}\quantity(1-q^{2r})\quantity(1-q^{2(r-s-1)})&\mathsf{CII}_{n-1}\end{cases}

Proof.

See Appendix
A.1

∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
v\in V(\gamma)\subseteq L(b)
be a
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-highest-weight vector.
Then
v\in V^{\prime}
and there is
i\in\left\{0,\dots,s\right\}
such that

v=\sum_{r=0}^{s}a_{s-r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

where

a_{r}=a^{-r}(q^{2s};q^{-2})_{r}K_{i}(q^{2r};-Cq^{2s};q^{-2})

(
q
-Kravchuk polynomial, see
[
16
, §14.15]
).
All vectors are given in a similar way but for a different value
of
i
belong to other simple
\mathbf{B}
-modules.
The constants
a,C
are chosen as follows:

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\pm\frac{q-q^{-1}}{\sqrt{(-1)^{n}[2]_{q}c}}q^{s+n-\frac{3}{2}}&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
\mp\frac{q-q^{-1}}{c}q^{n+s-1\pm(2-n)}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}\end{cases}

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-1&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
-q^{\pm(4-2n)}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}\end{cases}

Proof.

Note that
c_{s+1-r}=\frac{Cq^{2s}}{a^{2}}\quantity(1-q^{-2r})\quantity(1-q^{-2r+2s+2})
in
every case.

V(\gamma)
is a simple
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-module, and as such
its intersection with
V^{\prime}
is one-dimensional (as it has
only one ray of highest-weight vectors).
Consequently, any such highest-weight vector
v
has to
be an eigenvector of
B_{\overline{J}}
.

Assume that
B_{\overline{J}}v=\lambda v
.
Expanded in terms of the
(F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta)_{0\leq r\leq s}
, this
yields the following recursion relation:

\lambda a_{r}=a_{r+1}+b_{s-r}a_{r}+c_{s+1-r}a_{r-1}

(where
a_{-1}=a_{s+1}=0
).
This shows that
a_{r}=a_{r}(\lambda)
is a polynomial in
\lambda
of degree

r
.
In particular, all eigenspaces are one-dimensional and

\lambda
is a root of the polynomial

(\lambda-b_{0})a_{s}(\lambda)-c_{1}a_{s-1}(\lambda)
.

The relation between
c_{s+1-r}
and
C,a
suggests that
the polynomials are normalised dual
q
-Kravchuk polynomials (see
[
16
, §14.17]
).
In particular, supposing

a_{r}(\lambda)=a^{-r}(q^{2s};q^{-2})_{r}K_{r}(a\lambda+b;C,s|q^{-2}),

we can match our recursion relation provided that

a,C
are as given in the claim and

b=\begin{cases}0&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
q^{s}\quantity(q^{\mp s}-q^{\pm(s+4-2n)})&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}.\end{cases}

This shows that
a\lambda+b=q^{2i}+Cq^{2s-2i}
for an

i=0,\dots,s
,
and that hence

a_{r}=a^{-r}(q^{2s};q^{-2})_{r}K_{r}(q^{2i}+Cq^{2s-2i};C,s|q^{-2})=a^{-r}(q^{2s};q^{-2})_{r}K_{i}(q^{-2r};-Cq^{2s},s;q^{-2})

(
q
-Kravčuk polynomials, see
[
16
, §14.15]
).

Since we have shown that on
V^{\prime}
, the operator

B_{\overline{J}}
is diagonalisable, any subspace of

V^{\prime}
that is invariant under
B_{\overline{J}}
is a direct
sum of eigenspaces.
In particular, if we can (as
\mathbf{B}
-modules) decompose

L(b)=V(\gamma)\oplus V(\gamma)^{\perp}
, then

V(\gamma)\cap V^{\prime}=kv
and
V(\gamma)^{\perp}\cap V^{\prime}

are both invariant under
B_{\overline{J}}
.
We conclude that
V(\gamma)^{\perp}\cap V^{\prime}
consists of
those eigenspaces for other values of
i
.
∎

Conjecture 7.8
.

The number
i
as in Lemma
7.7
equals

0
or
n
, in such a way that

V(\gamma)
is generated by a vector
v\in V^{\prime}
satisfying

B_{\overline{J}}v=\lambda v

for

\lambda=\begin{cases}\pm\sqrt{(-1)^{n}[2]_{q}}q^{\frac{3}{2}-n}[s]_{q}&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
2cq^{1-n}[s+2-n]_{q}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}.\end{cases}

7.1.4.
MSF For the Bottom Element

Even without Conjecture
7.8
, we can still proceed a bit further in full generality.

Lemma 7.9
.

Write
v_{i}
for the
i
-th eigenvector from
Lemma
7.7
.

Define
p:L(b)\to V(\gamma)
as follows:
we decompose
L(b)
into simple
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-modules, map all modules whose highest weight
is not
s\omega_{n}
(or
s\omega_{1}
, respectively) to 0,
and which operates as follows on all other modules, i.e.

v\in\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}V^{\prime}
:
v
can be written as

v=\sum_{r=0}^{s}x_{r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

for unique
x_{r}\in U_{\bullet}^{-}
.
We then define

p(v):=\sum_{r=0}^{s}x_{r}v_{i}\frac{(a^{-1}Cq^{2s})^{r-s}}{(q^{-2};q^{-2})_{s-r}}K_{i}(q^{2s-2r};-Cq^{2s},s;q^{-2}).

Then
p
is a
\mathbf{B}
-intertwiner.

Proof.

Note that

p(v_{j})=v_{i}\sum_{r=0}^{s}(Cq^{2s})^{r-s}\frac{(q^{2s};q^{-2})_{s-r}}{(q^{-2};q^{-2})_{s-r}}K_{i}K_{j}(q^{2s-2r};-Cq^{2s},s;q^{-2}),

which is a non-zero multiple of
v_{i}
if

i=j
, and 0 otherwise, see
[
16
, (14.15.2)]
.
This shows that it is a
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
intertwiner.
Since the splitting of
L(b)
into simple

\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-modules is subordinate to the one into
simple
\mathbf{B}
-modules, we obtain it is also a

\mathbf{B}
-intertwiner.
∎

Notation 7.10
.

Let
v=xv_{i}\in V(\gamma)
for
x\in\mathbf{U}_{\bullet,-\nu}
, then we say that
b-\nu
is its
weight
.
These are representatives for the equivalence classes
modulo elements annihilated by
Y^{\Theta}=\{y\in Y:\Theta(h)=h\}
.

This gives us a definition of weight spaces and of
formal characters:

\ch_{\gamma}:=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}\dim(V(\gamma)_{b-\nu})e^{b-\nu}.

Corollary 7.11
.

Let
v\in V(\gamma)_{\mu}
, then

\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}(K_{h})v=\frac{p(K_{h}v)}{p(v)}=\frac{\sum_{r=0}^{s}q^{\langle h,\mu-r\omega_{k}\rangle}(Cq^{2s})^{r-s}\frac{(q^{2s};q^{-2})_{s-r}}{(q^{-2};q^{-2})_{s-r}}K_{i}(q^{2s-2r};-Cq^{2s},s;q^{-2})^{2}}{\sum_{r=0}^{s}(Cq^{2s})^{r-s}\frac{(q^{2s};q^{-2})_{s-r}}{(q^{-2};q^{-2})_{s-r}}K_{i}(q^{2s-2r};-Cq^{2s},s;q^{-2})^{2}}v

where
k=1,2
depending on whether we are considering
the
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
or
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
case.
The denominator is a scalar given by the orthogonality relation of the
q
-Kravchuk polynomials.
Assuming Conjecture
7.8
, this reduces to

\frac{q^{\langle h,\mu-s\omega_{k}\rangle}\quantity(C^{-1}q^{\langle h,\omega_k\rangle};q^{-2})_{s}}{\quantity(C^{-1};q^{-2})_{s}}v=\frac{q^{\langle h,\mu\rangle}\quantity(Cq^{-\langle h,\omega_k\rangle};q^2)_{s}}{(C;q^{2})_{s}}v

by
[
16
, (1.11.1),(1.8.7)]
.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4})=\begin{cases}\quantity(\frac{2n-1}{2}, \frac{2n-1}{2}+s, 0, 0)&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
\quantity(\frac{2n-1}{2},\frac{3}{2}+s,n-2,0)&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1},\end{cases}

i.e. the labelling from Proposition
5.4
with
l_{2}
increased by
s
.
For every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is a non-zero constant
D

such that

DP_{m}=\begin{cases}-\rho\triangleright\Res\quantity(\frac{\Phi^{s\omega_n + m\omega_1}_\gamma}{\Phi^{s\omega_n}_\gamma})&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
-\rho\triangleright\Res\quantity(\frac{\Phi^{s\omega_1+m\omega_2}_\gamma}{\Phi^{s\omega_1}_\gamma})&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}\end{cases}

where
P_{m}
is the
m
-th symmetric
Macdonald polynomial for the Macdonald data

\displaystyle S=S^{\prime}

\displaystyle=S(2\Sigma^{0})\cup 2S(2\Sigma^{0})^{\vee}

\displaystyle R=R^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2\Sigma^{0}

\displaystyle L=L^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2P(\Sigma)=\mathbb{Z}\omega_{k}

(with
k=1,2
for the
\mathsf{BII}_{n},\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
case) of type
(C_{1}^{\vee},C_{1})
,
the labelling
l
, and base
q^{2}
.

Proof.

Since
\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}
is a basis of the left
E^{\epsilon}
-module
E^{\gamma}
, the fraction

\frac{\Phi^{\mu}_{\gamma}}{\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}}
(for
\mu\in X^{+}(\gamma)
) can be understood to be the unique element
\phi\in E^{\epsilon}
such that
\phi\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}=\Phi^{\mu}_{\gamma}
, which can
then be restricted to
\mathbf{U}^{0}
and
-\rho
-shifted.

Note that
\omega_{1}=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
(
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
) and that

\omega_{2}=\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}
(
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
), so that

\Res(\Phi^b_\gamma)v=\frac{e^{\mu}\quantity(Ce^{-\omega_k};q^2)_{s}}{(C;q^{2})_{s}}v=\quantity(\frac{e^\mu}{(C;q^2)_s} + \lot)v

for
v\in V(\gamma)_{\mu}
, so that the leading exponent of
\Res(\Phi^b_\gamma)
is

e^{b}
.

We conclude that for
\phi\in E^{\epsilon}
, the leading exponent of
\Res(\phi)
is
\lambda
iff
the leading exponent of
\Res(\phi\Phi^b_\gamma)=e^{b+\lambda}
.
This shows that there is
D^{\prime}\in k^{\times}
such that

\Res\quantity(\frac{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma}{\Phi^b_\gamma})=D^{\prime}e^{\lambda}+\lot,

where
\lot
refers to lower terms in
X
with respect to the cone
Q^{+}
.
Since the right-hand side is a restriction of a zonal spherical function, we know that
all terms of its restriction are contained in
2L
(whose generator
\omega_{k}
lies in

Q^{+}
, so the order it inherits from
X
is the usual order on
\mathbb{Z}
).
Moreover, there is
D=q^{-\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle}D^{\prime}
such that

-\rho\triangleright\Res\quantity(\frac{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma}{\Phi^b_\gamma})=Dm_{\lambda}+\lot

(where
\lot
refers to lower terms in
2L_{+}
).

Moreover, if the
-\rho
-shifts of restrictions of zonal spherical functions are
orthogonal with respect to the weight
\triangledown
, then the family

\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix-\rho\triangleright\Res\qty(\frac{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma}{\Phi^b_\gamma})\endlx@physics@matrix)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\omega_{k}}

is orthogonal with respect to the weight
\triangledown^{\prime}=\triangledown\quantity(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Xi_\gamma(\Phi^b_\gamma,\Phi^b_\gamma)))
.
This follows from Lemma
5.11
.

We have

\displaystyle\quantity(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Xi_\gamma(\Phi^b_\gamma,\Phi^b_\gamma)))(h)

\displaystyle=\tr_{V(\gamma)}(\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}(K_{h-\rho})\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}(K_{-h-\rho}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu}\frac{\dim(V(\gamma)_{\mu})}{(C;q^{2})_{s}^{2}}q^{\langle-2\rho,\mu\rangle}\quantity(Cq^{-\langle h-\rho,\omega_k\rangle},Cq^{-\langle-h-\rho,\omega_k\rangle};q^2)_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{ch}_{\gamma}(-2\rho)}{(C;q^{2})_{s}^{2}}\quantity(Cq^{-\langle h-\rho,\omega_k\rangle},Cq^{-\langle-h-\rho,\omega_k\rangle};q^2)_{s}.

For
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
we have
\omega_{1}=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
, so that

\langle\rho,\omega_{1}\rangle=\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n}=2n-1
.
For
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
we have
\omega_{2}=\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}
,
so that

\langle\rho,\omega_{2}\rangle=\epsilon_{1}+2\epsilon_{2}+\cdots+2\epsilon_{n-1}+\epsilon_{n}=2n-1,

hence

-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Xi_\gamma(\Phi^b_\gamma,\Phi^b_\gamma))=\frac{\operatorname{ch}_{\gamma}(-2\rho)}{(C;q^{2})_{s}^{2}}\quantity(Cq^{2n-1}e^{-\omega_k}, Cq^{2n-1}e^{\omega_k};q^2)_{s}.

Recall that

C=\begin{cases}-1&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
-q^{4-2n}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1},\end{cases}

so that our weight function becomes
\triangledown^{\prime}=\frac{\operatorname{ch}_{\gamma}(-2\rho)}{(C;q^{2})_{s}^{2}}\triangle^{\prime+}\overline{\triangle^{\prime+}}
with

\triangle^{\prime+}=\begin{cases}\frac{(e^{2\omega_{1}};q^{2})_{\infty}}{\quantity(q^{2n-1}e^{\omega_1},-q^{2s+2n-1}e^{\omega_1},qe^{\omega_1},-qe^{\omega_1};q^2)_{\infty}}&\mathsf{BII}_{n}\\
\frac{(e^{2\omega_{2}};q^{2})_{\infty}}{\quantity(q^{2n-1}e^{\omega_2},-q^{2s+3}e^{\omega_2},q^{2n-3}e^{\omega_2},-qe^{\omega_2};q^2)_{\infty}}&\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}\end{cases}

(where we used Proposition
5.4
and
[
29
, §5.1.28]
to identify an appropriate
weight for the zonal spherical functions).
We conclude that
\triangledown^{\prime}
is proportional to the weight function for
the Macdonald polynomials of type
(C_{1}^{\vee},C_{1})
with the data and parameters claimed.

From

-\rho\triangleright\Res\quantity(\frac{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma}{\Phi^b_\gamma})=Dm_{\lambda}+\lot

we can thus conclude that

-\rho\triangleright\Res\quantity(\frac{\Phi^{b+\lambda}_\gamma}{\Phi^b_\gamma})=DP_{\lambda}.\qed

7.2.
Single-Variable 2-Vector:
\mathsf{DII}
with Spin Representation

We now consider another example of rank one. But now,
\#\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=2
. In the group case, this corresponds to
(Spin(2n),Spin(2n-1))
for

n\geq 1
. This case is studied by van Horssen and van Pruijssen
[
43
]
, they identified matrix spherical functions on the
associated with the fundamental spin-representation of

Spin(2n-1)
with non-symmetric Jacobi polynomials of type
\mathsf{BC}_{1}
.

Consequently, we would expect to find not symmetric but non-symmetric
Macdonald polynomials, which will indeed be the case.

Note that for
n=1
, this corresponds to an Abelian group with
trivial subgroup, for
n=2
, this corresponds to the group case of type
\mathsf{A}_{1}
(i.e. a diagram of type
\mathsf{A}_{1}\times\mathsf{A}_{1}
with white nodes and

\tau
interchanging the two nodes), for
n=3
, this corresponds to
a Satake diagram of type
\mathsf{AII}_{3}
, and for
n\geq 4
, this corresponds to
a Satake diagram of type
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
.

For the case
n=1
, we take
X,Y
to be lattices of rank 1 that are perfectly
paired and
\mathbf{U}
to be the group algebra of
Y
. The algebra
\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
is
then the trivial algebra 1, which has only one simple module
\gamma
. Similarly, for every element
\mu\in X
, the
corresponding simple representation of
\mathbf{U}
is 1-dimensional and we have

K_{h}\cdot 1=q^{\langle h,\mu\rangle}.

Since there are no roots, we have
\rho=0
, so that

(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Phi^\mu_\gamma)=e^{\mu},

which is the
\mu
-th non-symmetric Askey–Wilson polynomial with
parameters
(1,q^{1/2},-1,-q^{1/2})
(alternatively: non-symmetric

q^{2}
-ultraspherical polynomial with
k=0
). This covers the case of
n=1
. The
cases
n=3
and
n\geq 4
can be covered together, which will be done in the
following. The case
n=2
will be covered later.

7.2.1.
Notation

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a root system of type
\mathsf{D}_{n}
, let
X
be the weight
lattice and
Y
the coroot lattice. Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
be the
simple roots,
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
the coroots, and
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n}
the
fundamental weights. We pick
\epsilon_{1}=\cdots=\epsilon_{n}=1
, which implies
that
K_{i}=K_{h_{i}}
.

Let
I_{\bullet}=\left\{2,\dots,n\right\}
and
\tau=\id_{I}
. The coideal subalgebra

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
is then generated by

E_{2},\dots,E_{n},F_{2},\dots,F_{n},K_{2},\dots,K_{n}

and

B_{1}:=F_{1}+c\ad(E_{2}\cdots E_{n}E_{n-2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})K_{1}^{-1}.

The restricted root system is again
\Sigma=\left\{\pm\omega_{1}\right\}
so that

2L=\mathbb{Z}\omega_{1}
. The corresponding Satake diagram is

7.2.2.
Classical Branching Rules

We describe the branching rules of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
,

\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{so}(2n-1)
, and
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{so}(2n-2)
.
As explained in Section
7.1.2
, irreducible
representations of
\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{m}
are
described by half-integers
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1},c_{1},\dots,c_{n-1}
such that

a_{1}\geq\cdots\geq a_{n-1}\geq\absolutevalue{a_n},\qquad b_{1}\geq\cdots\geq b_{n-1}\geq 0,\qquad c_{1}\geq\cdots\geq c_{n-2}\geq\absolutevalue{c_{n-1}}

and such that all the
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i}
each only differ by integers. One
irreducible representation is contained in the other precisely once when
the numbers interlace and differ by integers.

We are
interested in the representation described by

b_{1}=\cdots=b_{n-1}=\frac{1}{2}
, i.e. the spin representation. It contains the two simple
\mathfrak{m}
-modules described by
c_{1}=\cdots=c_{n_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}
and
c_{n-1}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
, and it is contained in the simple
\mathfrak{g}
-modules
described by
a_{1}\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
,
a_{2}=\cdots=a_{n-1}=\frac{1}{2}
, and
a_{n}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
. In other words, if
\gamma
is the corresponding

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
-type, then
X^{+}(\gamma)=\left\{\omega_{n-1},\omega_{n}\right\}+\mathbb{N}_{0}\omega_{1}
.
This implies that
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=\left\{\omega_{n-1},\omega_{n}\right\}
.

Lastly, we note that both bottom elements restrict to the simple

\mathfrak{k}
-module we are considering. Consequently, we pick

V(\gamma):=L(\omega_{n})|_{\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}}
and equip it (and analogously
L(\omega_{n-1})
) with the basis
v_{\mu}

(
\mu\in W\omega_{n}
) described in
[
12
, §5A.1]
.

We also remark that
\Theta
acts on
X
as the reflection that negates

a_{1}
(in the notation given here) and leaves all other numbers intact.

7.2.3.
MSF for Bottom Elements

Using the notation of Lemma
3.35
we write
\Psi_{1}:=\Phi^{\omega_{n-1}}_{\gamma}
and
\Psi_{2}:=\Phi^{\omega_{n}}_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 7.13
.

We have

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(K_{h})v_{\mu}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h,\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{\mu}

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(K_{h})v_{\mu}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h,\mu\rangle}v_{\mu}

for
\mu\in W\omega_{n},h\in Y
.

Proof.

For
\Psi_{2}
we take
i=p=\id_{L(\omega_{n})}
and obtain the claimed
equation from
\Psi_{2}(K_{h})v_{\mu}=K_{h}v_{\mu}
.

For
\Psi_{1}
we define
j:V(\gamma)\to L(\omega_{n-1})
and

p:L(\omega_{n-1})\to V(\gamma)
as follows:

\displaystyle j:v_{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}v_{\mu-\omega_{1}}&\mu-\omega_{1}\in W\omega_{n}\\
(-q)^{2-n}cv_{\mu+\omega_{1}}&\mu+\omega_{1}\in W\omega_{n}\end{cases}

\displaystyle p:v_{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}(-q)^{n-2}c^{-1}v_{\mu-\omega_{1}}&\mu-\omega_{1}\in W\omega_{n-1}\\
v_{\mu+\omega_{1}}&\mu+\omega_{1}\in W\omega_{n-1}\end{cases}.

Note that these maps are well-defined since in the standard notation the
Weyl orbits of
\omega_{n},\omega_{n-1}
can be described as
n
-tuples
of
\left\{\pm\frac{1}{2}\right\}
with an even/odd number of
-\frac{1}{2}
’s.
Consequently, by flipping the first entry (i.e. adding or
subtracting
\omega_{1}
) we can always make one weight from the other.
Note that the maps
j,p
are
U_{\bullet}
-intertwiners as
\omega_{1}
’s
inner product with
h_{2},\dots,h_{n}
is zero, and as the weights are
unchanged apart from adding
\pm\omega_{1}
.

It therefore suffices to show that
p,j
preserve the action of
B_{1}
. For this we note that
B_{1}v_{\mu}
lies in the span of
v_{\mu-\alpha_{1}}
and
v_{\mu+\omega_{2}}
. In particular, since both cannot
be in the same Weyl orbit as
v_{\mu}
, it is only one term. Note that if

\langle h_{1},\mu\rangle=-1
, we just have

B_{1}v_{\mu}=F_{1}v_{\mu}=v_{\mu-\alpha_{1}}
. On the other hand, if

\langle h_{1},\mu\rangle=0
and
\langle h_{2},\mu\rangle=-1
, we know
that
v_{\mu}
can be reached from
v_{\omega_{n-1}-\omega_{2}}
or
v_{\omega_{n}-\omega_{2}}
by applying a polynomial in
F_{3},\dots,F_{n}
. Each such polynomial commutes with
B_{1}
, so that it suffices to
compute
B_{1}v_{\omega_{n}-\omega_{2}}
and
B_{1}v_{\omega_{n-1}-\omega_{2}}
,
which are multiples of
v_{\omega_{n}},v_{\omega_{n-1}}
.
We have

\displaystyle B_{1}v_{\mu-\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle=c\ad(E_{2}\cdots E_{n}E_{n-2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})K_{1}^{-1}v_{\mu-\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle=c\ad(E_{2}\cdots E_{n}E_{n-2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})v_{\mu-\omega_{2}}.

for
\mu=\omega_{n-1}
or
\omega_{n}
. Note that

\im(E_{i})\cap kv_{\omega_{n-1}}\neq 0
only for
i=n-1
, similarly for

\omega_{n}
(then
i=n
). Consequently, in
c\ad(E_{2}\cdots E_{n}E_{n-2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})v_{\mu-\omega_{2}}
all terms vanish that don’t
have
E_{n-1}
(resp.
E_{n}
) as their left-most term. Thus,

\displaystyle B_{1}v_{\omega_{n-1}-\omega_{2}}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{n-2}cK_{2}\cdots K_{n-2}K_{n}\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})K_{n}^{-1}E_{n}K_{n-2}^{-1}E_{n-2}\cdots K_{2}^{-1}E_{2}\eta_{\omega_{n-1}-\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-2}q^{2-n}c\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})v_{\omega_{n}-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle=(-q)^{2-n}cv_{\omega_{n-1}}.

We conclude

B_{1}v_{\mu}=\begin{cases}v_{\mu-\alpha_{1}}&\mu-\alpha_{1}\in W\mu\\
(-q)^{2-n}cv_{\mu+\omega_{2}}&\mu+\omega_{2}\in W\mu\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Then we have

j(B_{1}v_{\mu})=B_{1}j(v_{\mu})=\begin{cases}(-q)^{2-n}cv_{\mu+\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}&\mu-\alpha_{1}\in W\mu\\
(-q)^{2-n}cv_{\mu+\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}&\mu+\omega_{2}\in W\mu\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

We obtain similar relations for
p
. Furthermore,
p\circ j=\id_{V(\gamma)}
. Note that the definition of
j
can be
summarised as
j(L(\omega_{n-1})_{\mu})\subseteq L(\omega_{n})_{\Theta(\mu)}
. We conclude

\Psi_{1}(K_{h})v_{\mu}=q^{\langle h,\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{\mu}.\qed

Corollary 7.14
.

There is a nonzero constant
C\in k
such that

M=C\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix q^{n-1}+q^{1-n} & m_{\omega_1}\\m_{\omega_1} &
q^{n-1}+q^{1-n}\endlx@physics@matrix).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(K_{h-\rho})\Psi_{1}(K_{-h-\rho})v_{\mu}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h-\rho-h-\rho,\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{\mu}=q^{\langle-2\rho,\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{\mu}

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(K_{h-\rho})\Psi_{2}(K_{-h-\rho})v_{\mu}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h-\rho,\Theta(\mu)\rangle+\langle-h-\rho,\mu\rangle}v_{\mu}=q^{-\langle\rho,\mu+\Theta(\mu)\rangle-\langle h,\mu-\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{\mu}

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(K_{h-\rho})\Psi_{1}(K_{-h-\rho})v_{\mu}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h-\rho,\mu\rangle+\langle-h-\rho,\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{\mu}=q^{-\langle\rho,\mu+\Theta(\mu)\rangle+\langle h,\mu-\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{\mu}

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(K_{h-\rho})\Psi_{2}(K_{-h-\rho})v_{\mu}

\displaystyle=q^{\langle h-\rho-h-\rho,\mu\rangle}v_{\mu}=q^{\langle-2\rho,\mu\rangle}v_{\mu}.

Note that
D_{n}
is simply laced, so that
2\rho=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{R}^{\vee+}}h
. In particular,
\langle 2\rho,\omega_{1}\rangle=2(n-1)
,
so that

\langle 2\rho,\mu\rangle=\pm(n-1)+\langle\rho,\mu+\Theta(\mu)\rangle=\pm 2(n-1)+\langle 2\rho,\Theta(\mu)\rangle

based on whether
\mu-\Theta(\mu)=\pm\omega_{1}
. We conclude that

M_{1,1}=\ch_{\gamma}(-2\Theta(\rho))=M_{2,2}=\ch_{\gamma}(-2\rho)=\quantity(q^{n-1}+q^{1-n})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in W\omega_{n}\\
\mu-\Theta(\mu)=\omega_{1}\end{subarray}}q^{-\langle\rho,\mu+\Theta(\mu)\rangle},

and

M_{1,2}=M_{2,1}=\quantity(e^{-\omega_1}+e^{\omega_1})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in W\omega_{n}\\
\mu-\Theta(\mu)=\omega_{1}\end{subarray}}q^{-\langle\rho,\mu+\Theta(\mu)\rangle}.

∎

Note that the weight
\triangledown
considered here is the symmetric Macdonald weight
for the root system
S=\left\{\pm 2\omega_{1}+2r\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}r\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
with

\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})=2n-2
, i.e.

\triangledown=\prod_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\frac{(e^{2\epsilon\omega_{1}};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q^{2n-2}e^{2\epsilon\omega_{1}};q^{2})_{\infty}},

which is obtained from
[
29
, §6.3.1]
by doubling powers of
q
and setting
k=n-1
(and by replacing
x
by
e^{\omega_{1}}
). Consequently,
we have

\Delta^{0}=q^{1-n}\frac{e^{\omega_{1}}-e^{-\omega_{1}}}{q^{1-n}e^{\omega_{1}}-q^{n-1}e^{-\omega_{1}}}.

For the basis
v_{1}=1,v_{2}=q^{n-1}e^{\omega_{1}}
we then obtain the following
weight matrix

(39)

m_{i,j}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{w\in W_{\Sigma}}w\frac{v_{1}\overline{v_{2}}}{\Delta^{0}}\qquad m=\frac{q^{1-n}}{2}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix q^{n-1}+q^{1-n} & m_{\omega_1}\\m_{\omega_1} & q^{n-1}+q^{1-n}\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & 0\\0 & q^{2n-2}\endlx@physics@matrix).

Lemma 7.15
.

Define
C:k[2L]\to k[2L]
as a
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linear
map mapping

\displaystyle v_{1}\mapsto 2q^{n-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in W\omega_{n}\\
\mu-\Theta(\mu)=\omega_{1}\end{subarray}}q^{-\langle\rho,\mu+\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{1}

\displaystyle v_{2}\mapsto 2q^{1-n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in W\omega_{n}\\
\mu-\Theta(\mu)=\omega_{1}\end{subarray}}q^{-\langle\rho,\mu+\Theta(\mu)\rangle}v_{2}.

Then
C
is triangular with respect to the Macdonald
ordering on
(e^{\mu})_{\mu\in 2L}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.3
.
∎

Lemma 7.16
.

Define
t:2L\to X^{+}(\gamma)
by

-m\omega_{1}\mapsto m\omega_{1}+\omega_{n-1},\qquad(m+1)\omega_{1}\mapsto m\omega_{1}+\omega_{n}.

Then
t
is a bijection and
t^{-1}
is monotonic
with respect to the Macdonald ordering on
2L
and
the dominance ordering on
X^{+}(\gamma)
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.3
.
∎

Theorem 7.17
.

Let
\Gamma:k[2L]\to k[X]\otimes\operatorname{span}\left\{e_{\omega_{n-1}},e_{\omega_{n}}\right\}

be given by
\Gamma(v_{1}):=e_{\omega_{n-1}}
and
\Gamma(v_{2}):=e_{\omega_{n}}
.

Then, for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}
there is a
nonzero constant
C_{m}
such that

\Gamma(E_{m})=C_{m}\quantity(-\rho\triangleright\Res(\Phi^{t(m)}_\gamma)),

where
E_{m}
is the
m
-th non-symmetric Macdonald polynomial (see
[
29
, §6.2]
for the
root system
\left\{\pm 2\omega_{1}\right\}
of type
A_{1}
with parameter
k=n-1
and base
q^{2}
.

Proof.

Note that
C
is triangular by Lemma
7.15
,

\Gamma^{-1}
is monotonic with respect to
t^{-1}
by
construction,
and

t^{-1}
is monotonic by Lemma
7.16
.

Furthermore, we see from (
39
) and
Corollary
7.14
that

\forall b,b^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(\gamma):\quad\frac{1}{\#W_{\Sigma}}\sum_{w\in W_{\Sigma}}w\frac{\Gamma^{-1}(e_{b})\overline{C(\Gamma^{-1}(e_{b^{\prime}}))}}{\Delta^{0}}=M_{b,b^{\prime}}.

With Corollary
6.7
and noting that
the Intermediate Macdonald polynomials for
J=\emptyset
are just the
non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials, we conclude the claim.
∎

7.2.4.
Case
n=2

The
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case is considered in generality in
[
1
]
.
This particular case can be found in loc.cit, §4.1.1.

We consider the quantum group

\mathbf{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))^{\otimes 2}
with

\mathbf{B}_{\bm{c},\bm{s}}
generated by

B_{1}=F_{1}-cE_{2}K_{1}^{-1},\qquad B_{2}=F_{2}-cE_{1}K_{2}^{-1},\qquad K_{h_{1}-h_{2}}^{\pm 1}.

Classically, this corresponds to the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(2)
with
\mathfrak{k}
the diagonal
algebra. The branching rules correspond to the Clebsch–Gordan rules
for
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
: the simple
\mathfrak{g}
-module
L(m\omega_{1}+n\omega_{2})
contains the simple modules
L(\ell\omega)
of

\mathfrak{k}
for

\absolutevalue{m-n}\leq\ell\leq m+n,

where
m+n+\ell
is an even integer. Consequently, both
L(\omega_{1})
and

L(\omega_{2})
are irreducible when restricted to
\mathfrak{k}
and give
us the (same) spin module described by
\ell=1
. This
\mathfrak{k}
-module
is then contained in every simple
\mathfrak{g}
-module
L(m\omega_{1}+n\omega_{2})
satisfying
\absolutevalue{m-n}=1
. Consequently, we have

2L=\mathbb{Z}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})
and
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=\left\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\right\}
. Moreover, we have
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2}=1
, so that

2\rho=h_{1}+h_{2}.

We take
V(\gamma)=L(\omega_{1})
. In the basis
v_{\omega_{1}},v_{-\omega_{1}}
,
we have

\Phi^{\omega_{1}}_{\gamma}(K_{h})=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix q^{\langle h,\omega_1\rangle} & 0\\0 & q^{-\langle h,\omega_1\rangle}\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad\Phi^{\omega_{2}}_{\gamma}(K_{h})=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix q^{-\langle h,\omega_2\rangle\rangle} & 0\\0 & q^{\langle h,\omega_2\rangle}\endlx@physics@matrix).

We conclude that

M=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix q+q^{-1} & m_{\omega_1}\\m_{\omega_1} & q+q^{-1}\endlx@physics@matrix)

where we ordered the bottom elements as
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
.
This matches the findings of

[
1
]
.
The rest of the arguments
from Section
7.2.3
also work here, so that we also obtain non-symmetric
Macdonald polynomials for
k=n-1=1
.

7.3.
Multi-Variable 3-Vector:
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
,
\mathsf{A}_{2}
-group case,
\mathsf{AII}_{5}

We now consider three examples of rank 2: the three examples from

[
45
]
which are the three examples from
[
41
]
.
As is pointed out in loc.cit., Remark 2.3, there is one more Satake diagram whose restricted root system is of type
\mathsf{A}_{2}
:
\mathsf{EIV}
, but there doesn’t seem to be
a suitable
\mathbf{B}
-type that produces a similar result as we obtain here.

Moreover, the matrix spherical functions in the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
-group case has been studied in
[
1
]
from the viewpoint of matrix valued orthogonal polynomials, as introduced in Definition
6.10
. There the assosiated matrix valued orthogonal polynomials are identified with matrix-valued analogs of Askey-Wilson polynomials.

Rank 2 (and in particular
\Sigma
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2}
)
is the simplest case for which
there exist non-trivial examples of Intermediate Macdonald polynomials:
the ones from
[
40
, Section 6.2]
.
These Intermediate Macdonald polynomials turn out to describe
exactly the MSF we consider in this section.
However, as per
usual with the examples from
[
40
]
, the author chose exactly the
wrong conventions when initially working them out.

Notation

Let
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
correspond to the following Satake diagrams:

\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, the group case for
\mathsf{A}_{2}
, and
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
. Their respective Satake diagrams are

Let
X
be the weight lattice and
Y
the coroot lattice.
Let
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
be the simple roots,
(h_{i})_{i\in I}
the
corresponding coroots, and
(\omega_{i})_{i\in I}
the fundamental weights.
We pick
\epsilon_{i}=1
, so that
K_{i}=K_{h_{i}}
(
i\in I
).

The coideal subalgebra
\mathbf{B}
is generated by the following elements:

(i)

For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
:

B_{i}:=F_{i}-c_{i}E_{i}K_{i}^{-1}\qquad(i\in I).

(ii)

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case:

B_{i}:=F_{i}-c_{i}E_{\tau(i)}K_{i}^{-1}\qquad(i\in I)

(iii)

For
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
: by
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i},K_{i}^{-1}
(
i=1,3,5
) and

B_{2}:=F_{2}-c_{2}\ad(E_{1}E_{3})(E_{2})K_{2}^{-1},\qquad B_{4}:=F_{4}-c_{4}\ad(E_{3}E_{5})(E_{4})K_{4}^{-1}.

Moreover, the involution
\Theta
gives us the following root systems
\Sigma
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2}
and weight lattices
L
:

(i)

For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, we have
\Theta=-1
, so that
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha

for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{R}
. Consequently, we have

\Sigma=\mathcal{R}
and
L=X
, generated by
\tilde{\omega}_{1},\tilde{\omega}_{2}
.

(ii)

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, we have
\Theta=-\tau
, so that

\tilde{\alpha}_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+2}}{2}
for
i=1,2
.
\Sigma
has

\tilde{\alpha}_{1},\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
as simple roots, and
L
is generated by

\tilde{\omega}_{1}=\frac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{3}}{2}
and

\tilde{\omega}_{2}=\frac{\omega_{2}+\omega_{4}}{2}
.

(iii)

In the common notation of
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
,
\Theta
maps
exchanges
e_{1},e_{3},e_{5}
with
-e_{2},-e_{4},-e_{6}
, respectively. Consequently,

\Sigma
has

\tilde{\alpha}_{2}=\frac{\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}{2}=\frac{e_{1}+e_{2}-e_{3}-e_{4}}{2},\qquad\tilde{\alpha}_{4}=\frac{\alpha_{3}+2\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{4}}{2}=\frac{e_{3}+e_{4}-e_{5}-e_{6}}{2}

as simple roots, and its weight lattice
L
is spanned by

\tilde{\omega}_{2}=\frac{\omega_{2}}{2}
and
\tilde{\omega}_{4}=\frac{\omega_{4}}{2}
.

7.3.2.
Classical Branching Rules

We choose the quantum equivalents of the standard representations of
\mathfrak{k}

in every case.
From
[
45
, §6]
we know that in every case,
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)

has three elements, and for
\lambda
one of two elements we get that the

\mathfrak{g}
-representation of highest weight
\lambda
stays irreducible when
restricted to
\mathfrak{k}
.
By Theorem
3.28
, this stays true on the quantum group level.

In particular, we find that

(i)

for
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=\left\{\omega_{1},\omega_{1}+\omega_{2},\omega_{2}\right\}
, and we pick
V(\gamma)=L(\omega_{1})|_{\mathbf{B}}
;

(ii)

for the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case,
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=\left\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}+\omega_{3},\omega_{4}\right\}
, and we pick
V(\gamma)=L(\omega_{1})|_{\mathbf{B}}
;

(iii)

for
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
,
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=\left\{\omega_{1},\omega_{3},\omega_{5}\right\}
, and we pick
V(\gamma)=L(\omega_{1})|_{\mathbf{B}}
.

7.3.3.
Describing
V(\gamma)

In the previous section we picked concrete vector spaces for
V(\gamma)
.
Note that these are all representations whose highest weight is minuscule.
Consequently, we can make use of the description provided in
[
12
, §5A]
,
which we will do (again using the letter
v
instead of
x
to denote basis elements).

Proposition 7.18
.

(i)

For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, we order the basis as
v_{\omega_{1}},v_{-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}},v_{-\omega_{2}}
.
Then, the generators
B_{1},B_{2}
act as the following matrices:

B_{1}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & -c_1q & 0\\1 & 0 & 0\\0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad B_{2}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & 0 & 0\\0 & 0 & -c_2q\\0 & 1 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix).

(ii)

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, we order the basis the same way.
Then,

B_{1}v_{\omega_{1}}=v_{-\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}},B_{2}v_{-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}=v_{-\omega_{2}},B_{3}v_{-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}=-c_{1}v_{\omega_{1}},B_{4}v_{-\omega_{2}}=-c_{2}v_{-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}.

Any other combination of generator and basis element vanishes.

(iii)

For
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, we order the basis as

v_{\omega_{1}},v_{-\omega_{i}+\omega_{i+1}},v_{-\omega_{5}}
(
i=1,\dots,4
).
The vectors
v_{\omega_{1}},v_{-\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}},v_{-\omega_{4}+\omega_{5}}
are
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-highest weight vectors of
weights
\omega_{1},\omega_{3},\omega_{5}
, respectively, and
B_{2},B_{4}
act
as follows:

\displaystyle B_{2}v_{-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle=v_{-\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}},\qquad

\displaystyle B_{2}v_{-\omega_{3}+\omega_{4}}

\displaystyle=-c_{2}q^{-1}v_{\omega_{1}},

\displaystyle B_{4}v_{-\omega_{3}+\omega_{4}}

\displaystyle=v_{-\omega_{4}+\omega_{5}},

\displaystyle B_{4}v_{-\omega_{5}}

\displaystyle=-c_{4}q^{-1}v_{-\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}}

with all other combinations being 0.

7.3.4.
MSF for Bottom Elements

We enumerate our bottom as follows
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=\left\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\right\}
,
with
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}
the elements given earlier (in the same order).
We already note that this is chosen in such a way that
the permutation
\sigma
from
3.6
exchanges
b_{1}
and
b_{3}
,
and leaves
b_{2}
.

Using the notation of Lemma
3.35
, we write
\Psi_{i}:=\Phi^{b_{i}}_{\gamma}
for
i=1,2,3
.

Proposition 7.19
.

\Res(\Psi_i)
is always a diagonal matrix.
Writing the diagonal entries in columns, we obtain the following:

(i)

for
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
:

\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix e^{\omega_1}\\e^{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\e^{-\omega_2}\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix qe^{\alpha_2}+q^{-1}e^{-\alpha_2}\\
qe^{\alpha_1+\alpha_2} + q^{-1}e^{-\alpha_1-\alpha_2}\\
qe^{\alpha_1} + qe^{-\alpha_1}\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix e^{-\omega_1}\\e^{\omega_1-\omega_2}\\e^{\omega_2}\endlx@physics@matrix);

(ii)

for the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case:

\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix e^{\omega_1}\\
e^{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\
e^{-\omega_2}\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix qe^{\omega_2-\omega_3+\omega_4} + q^{-1}e^{\omega_1-\omega_2-\omega_4}\\
qe^{\omega_2+\omega_3}
+ q^{-1}e^{-\omega_1-\omega_4}\\
qe^{\omega_1-\omega_2+\omega_3} + q^{-1}e^{-\omega_1-\omega_3+\omega_4}\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix e^{-\omega_3}\\e^{\omega_3-\omega_4}\\
e^{\omega_4}\endlx@physics@matrix);

(iii)

for
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
:

\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix e^{\omega_1}\\
e^{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\
e^{-\omega_2+\omega_3}\\
e^{-\omega_3+\omega_4}\\
e^{-\omega_4+\omega_5}\\
e^{-\omega_5}\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad\frac{1}{q^{2}+q^{-2}}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix q^2e^{\omega_1-\omega_2+\omega_4}
+ q^{-2}e^{\omega_1-\omega_4}\\
q^2e^{-\omega_1+\omega_4} + q^{-2}e^{-\omega_1+\omega_2-\omega_4}\\
q^2e^{\omega_3}+q^{-2}e^{-\omega_2+\omega_3-\omega_4}\\
q^2e^{\omega_2-\omega_3+\omega_4} + q^{-2}e^{-\omega_3}\\
q^2e^{\omega_2-\omega_4+\omega_5} + q^{-2}e^{-\omega_2+\omega_5}\\
q^2e^{\omega_2-\omega_5} + q^{-2}e^{-\omega_2+\omega_4-\omega_5}\endlx@physics@matrix),\qquad\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix e^{\omega_1-\omega_2}\\
e^{-\omega_1}\\
e^{\omega_3-\omega_4}\\
e^{\omega_2-\omega_3}\\
e^{\omega_5}\\
e^{\omega_4-\omega_5}\endlx@physics@matrix).

Proof.

For
b_{1}
, take
p=j=\id
.
For
b_{3}
, we can take
j,p
to both map

v_{\mu}\mapsto v_{-\tau(\mu)}
(note that

-\tau(\mu)=-\tau_{0}\sigma(\mu)=w_{0}\sigma(\mu)
, so that
the weights are indeed valid weights of the corresponding other module).
That they indeed preserve the action of
\mathbf{B}
can be checked using
the explicit expressions from Proposition
7.18
.

For
b_{2}
, we note that for the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case and

\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, the weight
b_{2}
is minuscule, so we can use

(v_{\mu})_{\mu\in Wb_{2}}
as basis.
For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, it is only pseudominuscule.
In that case, we use the notation from

[
12
, §5A.2]
(i.e.
x_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\mathcal{R}
and

y_{1},y_{2}
, corresponding to Jantzen’s
h_{\alpha_{1}},h_{\alpha_{2}}
).

For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, we pick the following maps

\displaystyle j\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{\omega_1}\\
v_{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\
v_{-\omega_2}\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle:=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix
c_2q^2x_{\alpha_2} + x_{-\alpha_2}\\
-c_1c_2q^3 x_{\alpha_1+\alpha_2}
+ x_{-\alpha_1-\alpha_2}\\
-c_2q(c_1q^2x_{\alpha_1} + x_{-\alpha_1})\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle p\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix x_{\alpha_1}\\
x_{\alpha_1+\alpha_2}\\
x_{\alpha_2}\\
x_{-\alpha_1}\\
x_{-\alpha_1-\alpha_2}\\
x_{-\alpha_2}\\
y_1\\
y_2\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{c_{1}c_{2}q^{2}(q+q^{-1})}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{-\omega_2}\\
v_{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\
-c_1qv_{\omega_1}\\
c_1v_{-\omega_2}\\
-c_1c_2qv_{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\
-c_1c_2qv_{\omega_1}\\
0\\0\endlx@physics@matrix).

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, we pick

\displaystyle j\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{\omega_1}\\v_{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\v_{-\omega_2}\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle:=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{\omega_1-\omega_2-\omega_4} + c_2qv_{\omega_2-\omega_3+\omega_4}\\
v_{-\omega_1-\omega_4} - c_1c_2qv_{\omega_2+\omega_3}\\
-c_2v_{-\omega_1-\omega_3+\omega_4}-c_1c_2qv_{\omega_1-\omega_2+\omega_3}\endlx@physics@matrix),

\displaystyle p\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{\omega_2+\omega_2}\\
v_{\omega_2-\omega_3+\omega_4}\\
v_{\omega_1-\omega_2+\omega_3}\\
v_{\omega_1-\omega_2-\omega_4}\\
v_{-\omega_1-\omega_3+\omega_4}\\
v_{-\omega_1-\omega_4}\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{c_{1}c_{2}(q+q^{-1})}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix-v_{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\\
c_1v_{\omega_1}\\
-v_{-\omega_2}\\
c_1c_2qv_{\omega_1}\\
-c_1qv_{-\omega_2}\\
c_1c_2qv_{-\omega_1+\omega_2}\endlx@physics@matrix).

And for
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, we pick

\displaystyle j\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{\omega_1}\\v_{-\omega_2+\omega_3}\\v_{-\omega_4+\omega_5}\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{\omega_1-\omega_4} - c_4qv_{\omega_1-\omega_2+\omega_4}\\
v_{-\omega_2+\omega_3-\omega_4} + c_2c_4v_{\omega_3}\\
-c_4q^{-1}v_{-\omega_2+\omega_5} + c_2c_4v_{\omega_2-\omega_4+\omega_5}\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle p\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix v_{\omega_1-\omega_4}\\
v_{\omega_1-\omega_2+\omega_4}\\
v_{-\omega_2+\omega_3-\omega_4}\\
v_{\omega_3}\\
v_{-\omega_2+\omega_5}\\
v_{\omega_1-\omega_4}\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}c_{4}(q^{2}+q^{-2})}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix c_2c_4q^{-2}v_{\omega_1}\\
-qc_2v_{\omega_1}\\
c_2c_4q^{-2}v_{-\omega_2+\omega_3}\\
q^2v_{-\omega_2+\omega_3}\\
-c_2q^{-1}v_{-\omega_4+\omega_5}\\
q^2v_{-\omega_4+\omega_5}\endlx@physics@matrix),

extend this
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-linearly, and let the maps be 0 on all other basis
vectors.

These maps all intertwine
\mathbf{B}
and satisfy
p\circ j=\id_{V(\gamma)}
.
This can be seen especially easily for
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, where

V(\gamma)
splits as a direct sum of three two-dimensional

\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-modules of highest weights
\omega_{1},\omega_{3},\omega_{5}
,
respectively.
In order to map
V(\gamma)

\mathbf{B}
-linearly to any other
\mathbf{U}
-module (and
vice-versa), we must therefore identify the
\mathbf{U}_{\bullet}
-highest weight
vectors of these weights.
For
L(\omega_{5})
, the desired weight spaces are spanned by
v_{\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}},v_{\omega_{3}-\omega_{4}},v_{\omega_{5}}
, respectively,
so any intertwiner must map
v_{\omega_{1}}
to the span of
v_{\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}}

or vice-versa (and similarly for the others).
By considering the actions of
B_{2},B_{4}
on
V(\gamma)
, the constants can be
fixed.

For
L(\omega_{3})
, the desired weight spaces are 2-dimensional, but the fact
that
B_{4}v_{\omega_{1}}=0
fixes the candidate.
From these maps
p,j
we see that
\Res(\Psi_i)
(
i=1,2,3
) have indeed the
claimed shapes.
∎

Corollary 7.20
.

We obtain the following matrix weights:

(i)

For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, we obtain

M=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix[3]_q & m_{2\omega_2} & m_{2\omega_1}\\
m_{2\omega_1} & \frac{m_{2\alpha_1+2\alpha_2} + 2}{(q+q^{-1})^2} + 1
& m_{2\omega_2}\\
m_{2\omega_2} & m_{2\omega_1} & [3]_q\endlx@physics@matrix).

(ii)

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, we obtain

M=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix[3]_q & m_{2\tilde{\omega}_2} & m_{2\tilde{\omega}_1}\\
m_{2\tilde{\omega}_1} & \frac{m_{2\tilde{\alpha}_1+2\tilde{\alpha}_2} + 2}{(q+q^{-1})^2} + 1
& m_{2\tilde{\omega}_2}\\
m_{2\tilde{\omega}_2} & m_{2\tilde{\omega}_1} & [3]_q\endlx@physics@matrix).

(iii)

For
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, we obtain

M=(q+q^{-1})\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix
[3]_{q^2} & m_{2\tilde{\omega}_4} & m_{2\tilde{\omega}_2}\\
m_{2\tilde{\omega}_2} & \frac{m_{2\tilde{\alpha}_2+2\tilde{\alpha}_4}+2}{(q^2+q^{-2})^2} + 1 & m_{2\tilde{\omega}_4}\\
m_{2\tilde{\omega}_2} & m_{2\tilde{\omega}_4} & [3]_{q^2}\endlx@physics@matrix).

Proof.

This can be computed from Proposition
7.19
,
using that
M_{b_{i},b_{j}}
can be computed from

(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Psi_i),(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Psi_j)
.
We use that
\rho=h_{1}+h_{2}
for
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
,
\rho=h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}+h_{4}
for
the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, and

\rho=\frac{5}{2}(h_{1}+h_{5})+4(h_{2}+h_{4})+\frac{9}{2}h_{3}

for
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
.

We can furthermore exploit Proposition
3.61
(ii,iii), so that we only
need to compute the entries
M_{b_{1},b_{1}},M_{b_{1},b_{2}},M_{b_{1},b_{3}},M_{b_{2},b_{2}}
.
∎

We now write
\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2}
for
2\tilde{\omega}_{1},2\tilde{\omega}_{2}
(
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
and
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case) or
2\tilde{\omega}_{2},2\tilde{\omega}_{4}
, respectively for the generators of
2L
.

Observe that
M
is proportional to

(40)

M=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix[3]_\tau& m_{\varpi_2} & m_{\varpi_1}\\
m_{\varpi_1} & \frac{m_{\varpi_1+\varpi_2} + 2}{(\tau+\tau^{-1})^2} + 1
& m_{\varpi_2}\\
m_{\varpi_2} & m_{\varpi_1} & [3]_\tau\endlx@physics@matrix)

where
\tau=q
in the first two cases and
q^{2}
in the latter.

We now begin using notation from
[
40
]
, in particular
we set
J:=\left\{2\right\}
and take
W_{J}
to be the parabolic subgroup
W_{J}\leq W_{\Sigma}
generated by
s_{2}
,

L_{+,J}
for the
J
-dominant elements (
[
40
, Definition 3.12]
) of
L
,
and
m_{J,\lambda}
for the
W_{J}
-symmetric
“monomial”
containing
e^{\lambda}
(proof of
[
40
, Lemma 4.6]
).
We consider the
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-basis of
k[2L]^{W_{J}}
(
J=\left\{2\right\}
)
given by

(41)

e_{1}=1,\qquad e_{s_{1}}=\frac{e^{\varpi_{2}}+e^{\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}}}{1+\tau^{-2}},\qquad e_{s_{2}s_{1}}=\tau^{2}e^{\varpi_{1}},

which produces the following matrix weight (using the
\overline{\cdot}

involution instead of
*
):

(42)

\frac{\tau^{4}+\tau^{2}}{6}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix[3]_\tau& m_{\varpi_1} & m_{\varpi_2}\\
m_{\varpi_2} & \frac{m_{\varpi_1+\varpi_2}+2}{(\tau+\tau^{-1})^2} + 1 & m_{\varpi_1}\\
m_{\varpi_1} & m_{\varpi_2} & [3]_\tau\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & 0 & 0\\0 & \tau^2 & 0\\0 & 0 & \tau^4\endlx@physics@matrix).

This suggests that we need to map
e_{1},e_{s_{1}},e_{s_{2}s_{1}}
to

\Psi_{3},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{1}
(or
e_{b_{3}},e_{b_{2}},e_{b_{1}}
).

Lemma 7.21
.

The map
t:2L_{+,J}\to X^{+}(\gamma)
given by

\varpi_{1}+\lambda\mapsto b_{1}+\lambda,\qquad s_{1}(\varpi_{2}+\lambda)\mapsto b_{2}+\lambda,\qquad s_{1}s_{2}\lambda\mapsto b_{3}+\lambda

for
\lambda\in L_{+}
is a well-defined bijection.
Moreover,
t^{-1}
is monotonic (considering the
dominance ordering on
X^{+}(\gamma)
and the Macdonald ordering on
2L_{+,J}
, cf.
[
29
, §2.7]
).

Proof.

See Appendix
A.4
.
∎

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
C
be a
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linear endomorphism of
k[2L]^{W_{J}}
given by

Ce_{v}=a_{v}e_{v}\qquad v\in W^{J}.

Then
C
is triangular with respect to
(m_{J,\mu})_{\mu\in L_{+,J}}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.4
.
∎

Lemma 7.23
.

Define the
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linear map

\Gamma:k[2L]^{W_{J}}\to k[X]\otimes\operatorname{span}\left\{e_{b_{i}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}i=1,2,3\right\}
by
mapping

e_{1}\mapsto e_{b_{3}},\qquad e_{s_{1}}\mapsto e_{b_{2}},\qquad e_{s_{1}s_{2}}\mapsto e_{b_{1}}

(recall (
41
) for
e_{1},e_{s_{1}},e_{s_{1}s_{2}}
).
Then
\Gamma^{-1}
is
t^{-1}
-triangular.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.4
.
∎

Theorem 7.24
.

For every
\mu\in 2L_{+,J}
there is a nonzero constant
C_{\mu}
such that

\Gamma(P_{J,\mu})=C_{\mu}(-\rho)\triangleright\Res(\Phi^{t(\mu)}_\gamma).

Proof.

We define the
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linear automorphism
C\in\End(k[2L]^{W_{J}})
given by the matrix

\frac{6}{\tau^{4}+\tau^{2}}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & 0 & 0\\0 & \tau^{-2} & 0\\0 & 0 & \tau^{-4}\endlx@physics@matrix)

for
\tau\in k^{\times}
. By Lemma
7.22
,

C
is triangular.
Moreover, by Lemma
7.21
,
t^{-1}
is monotonic.
We now show that
t^{-1}
is monotonic.
Moreover, by Lemma
7.23
,
\Gamma^{-1}
is

t^{-1}
-triangular.

Comparing Equations
40
and Equation
42
, we see that the matrix weights match up.
Note that by Proposition
5.4
, the
-\rho
-shifted
zonal spherical functions for
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
are the Macdonald polynomials
for the root system
S(2\Sigma)
(which equals
S((2\Sigma)^{\vee})^{\vee}
as
\mathsf{A}_{2}
is simply-laced) with parameter
k=\frac{1}{2}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})
,
with all
q
-powers multiplied by
\epsilon
.

We can now conclude the claim
from Corollary
6.7
.
∎

7.4.
Conclusion to the Examples

A conclusion that can be drawn from the examples is that in all cases considered,
the
-\rho
-shifted restrictions of MSF can be mapped to

k[2L]^{W_{J}}
for a parabolic subgroup
W_{J}\leq W_{\Sigma}
in such a way that
the elementary MSF are mapped to Intermediate Macdonald polynomials.

We shall now make more precise how the parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
can be obtained in
the examples and make two general conjectures about when MSF are Intermediate Macdonald
polynomials.

By
[
37
, Lemma 8.8]
and Theorem
3.28
,
the elements of
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
correspond to the irreducible
representations of
M
(
M
-types) contained in the
K
-representation
V(\gamma)^{1}
, where
K
is a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}

such that
V(\gamma)^{1}
is integrable and
M=Z_{K}(\mathfrak{a})
for
any choice of maximal commutative
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{g}^{-\theta}
.

Since
M
is a normal subgroup of
N_{K}(\mathfrak{a})
, the restricted
Weyl group
W_{\Sigma}=N_{K}(\mathfrak{a})/M
acts on the

M
-types of
V(\gamma)^{1}
, and hence also on
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
.

Conjecture 7.25
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
be a commutative triple.
The aforementioned action of
W_{\Sigma}
on
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)

can be described as follows: the map

p:\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)\to\mathfrak{P}(X),\qquad b\mapsto W_{\bullet}b+2L

is an injection and has a
W_{\Theta}
-invariant domain (
W_{\Theta}=Z_{W}(\Theta)
).
Pulling back the
W_{\Theta}
-action along this injection gives
a
W_{\Theta}
action on
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
that factors through

W_{\bullet}
, and hence yields an action of
W_{\Sigma}

[
5
, (2.2)]
.

Conjecture 7.26
.

Assume now that the action of
W_{\Sigma}
on
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)

is transitive and one
of the stabilisers
W_{J}
is a parabolic subgroup of
W_{\Sigma}
.
Then the
-\rho
-shifted restrictions of MSF for
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
are Intermediate Macdonald polynomials invariant under

W_{J}
.

We now show that these two conjectures hold for all integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-types
and for the four examples
\mathsf{DII}_{n},\mathsf{AI}_{2}
, the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case,
and
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
.

Lemma 7.27
.

Conjecture
7.25
holds for the six examples from this chapter.

Proof.

Recall that in the examples we had

\mathcal{B}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\left\{s\omega_{n}\right\}&\mathsf{BII}\\
\left\{s\omega_{1}\right\}&\mathsf{CII}_{n}\\
\left\{\omega_{n-1},\omega_{n}\right\}&\mathsf{DII}\\
\left\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\right\}&\mathsf{AI}_{3}\\
\left\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}+\omega_{3},\omega_{4}\right\}&\mathsf{A}_{2}\text{-group}\\
\left\{\omega_{1},\omega_{3},\omega_{5}\right\}&\mathsf{AII}_{5}\end{cases}.

For
\mathsf{BII}
, we have

W_{\bullet}s\omega_{n}+2L=\left\{\quantity(\frac{s}{2}+a,\frac{b_2s}{2},\dots,\frac{b_ns}{2})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in\mathbb{Z},b_{2},\dots,b_{n}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}\right\}.

For
\mathsf{CII}
, we have

W_{\bullet}s\omega_{1}+2L=\left\{\quantity(a,b,0,\dots,0)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\absolutevalue{a-b}=s\right\}.

For
\mathsf{DII}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{n-1}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{\quantity(\frac{1}{2}+a,\frac{b_2}{2},\dots,
\frac{b_n}{2})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in\mathbb{Z},b_{2},\dots,b_{n}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\},b_{2}\cdots b_{n}=-1\right\}

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{n}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{\quantity(\frac{1}{2}+a,\frac{b_2}{2},\dots,\frac{b_n}{2})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in\mathbb{Z},b_{2},\dots,b_{n}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\},b_{2}\cdots b_{n}=1\right\}.

For
\mathsf{AI}_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{1}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}(a-1),b\in 2\mathbb{Z}\right\}

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{2}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,(b-1)\in 2\mathbb{Z}\right\}

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b\in 1+\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case we have

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{1}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{(a+1)\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+a\omega_{3}+b\omega_{4}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
