\displaystyle W_{\bullet}(\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{a\omega_{1}+(b+1)\omega_{2}+(a+1)\omega_{3}+b\omega_{4}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{4}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+a\omega_{3}+(b+1)\omega_{4}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

For
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{1}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{\pm\omega_{1}+a\omega_{2}+b\omega_{4}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{3}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{\pm\omega_{3}+a\omega_{2}+b\omega_{4}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

\displaystyle W_{\bullet}\omega_{5}+2L

\displaystyle=\left\{\pm\omega_{5}+a\omega_{2}+b\omega_{4}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Note that all these sets are disjoint, so
p
is injective, and
every set
p(b)
is permuted by every element of
W_{\bullet}
. Next,
we describe the groups
W_{\Theta}
:

W_{\Sigma}=\begin{cases}\left\{W_{\bullet},s_{1}\cdots s_{n}\cdots s_{1}W_{\bullet}\right\}&\mathsf{BII}\\
\left\{W_{\bullet},w_{0}W_{\bullet}\right\}&\mathsf{CII}\\
\left\{W_{\bullet},s_{1}\cdots s_{n-2}s_{n}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}W_{\bullet}\right\}&\mathsf{DII}\\
W&\mathsf{AI}_{3}\\
\langle s_{1}s_{3},s_{2}s_{4}\rangle&\mathsf{A}_{2}\text{-group}\\
\langle s_{2}s_{1}s_{3}s_{2}W_{\bullet},s_{4}s_{3}s_{5}s_{4}W_{\bullet}\rangle&\mathsf{AII}_{5}\end{cases}.

Write
r_{1}:=s_{1},s_{1}s_{3},s_{2}s_{1}s_{3}s_{2}
for the first generator
of the last three cases, and
r_{2}:=s_{2},s_{2}s_{4},s_{4}s_{3}s_{5}s_{4}
for
the second.
These generators act as follows on the bottom elements:

For
\mathsf{BII}
:

s_{1}\cdots s_{n}\cdots s_{1}s\omega_{n}=s\omega_{n}-s\omega_{1}\in p(s\omega_{n})

For
\mathsf{CII}
:

w_{0}s\omega_{1}=-\omega_{1}=s_{1}s\omega_{1}-s\omega_{2}\in p(s\omega_{1}).

For
\mathsf{DII}
:

\displaystyle s_{1}\cdots s_{n-2}s_{n}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}\omega_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n}-\omega_{1}\in p(\omega_{n})

\displaystyle s_{1}\cdots s_{n-2}s_{n}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}\omega_{n}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n-1}-\omega_{1}\in p(\omega_{n-1}).

For
\mathsf{AI}_{3}
, the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, and for
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
:

\displaystyle r_{1}b_{1}

\displaystyle\in p(b_{2})

\displaystyle r_{1}b_{2}

\displaystyle\in p(b_{1})

\displaystyle r_{1}b_{3}

\displaystyle\in p(b_{3})

\displaystyle r_{2}b_{1}

\displaystyle\in p(b_{1})

\displaystyle r_{2}b_{2}

\displaystyle\in p(b_{3})

\displaystyle r_{2}b_{3}

\displaystyle\in p(b_{2})

Together with the fact that
W_{\Theta}L=L
we obtain that

W_{\Theta}
restricts to a map on
\im(p)
.
This therefore descends to the following actions on
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
:
the trivial action for
\mathsf{BII}
and the standard permutation actions of

S_{2}
on
\left\{\omega_{n-1},\omega_{n}\right\}
(
\mathsf{DII}
) and

S_{3}
on
\left\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\right\}
(
\mathsf{AI}_{3}
,
\mathsf{A}_{2}
-group, and
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
).

We now show that these actions coincide with the group theoretical actions
derived earlier.

For
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
and
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
:

Nothing further needs to be shown.

For
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
:

We take
K=\operatorname{Spin}(2n-1)
. Since the
adjoint action on
\mathfrak{p}
factors via
SO(2n-1)
, our group

M
is the preimage of
SO(2n-2)
under
K\to SO(2n-1)
, i.e.

M=\operatorname{Spin}(2n-2)
. In particular,
M
is a simple
Lie group, so that its representation theory is determined by the
highest weights. As was described earlier,
V(\gamma)^{1}
splits
as
L_{M}(\omega_{n-1}|_{\mathfrak{t}})\oplus L_{M}(\omega_{n}|_{\mathfrak{t}})
, where
\mathfrak{t}\leq\mathfrak{m}\leq\mathfrak{g}
is the Cartan subalgebra that was chosen to be contained in the Cartan subalgebra

\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. In particular,

s_{1}\cdots s_{n-2}s_{n}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}\omega_{n}|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\omega_{n-1}|_{\mathfrak{t}}
and vice-versa, so that

s_{1}\cdots s_{n-2}s_{n}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}
, the non-trivial representative of
W_{\Sigma}
indeed permutes the
M
-types as
claimed.

For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
:

We take
K=SO(3)
and have

M=\left\{\diag(a,b,c)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a,b,c\in\left\{\pm 1\right\},abc=1\right\}.

the
K
-type of
\gamma
corresponds to the standard representation of
K
on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Note that
M
can be seen as a subset of the Cartan
subgroup of
SL(3)
. This gives us a
W
-action on
M
-representations and a direct way of associating weights of
\mathfrak{g}
with one-dimensional representations of
M
.

In particular, our bottom weights induce the
following one-dimensional representations of
M
:

e^{\omega_{1}}_{M}:\diag(a,b,c)\mapsto a,\qquad e^{\omega_{2}}_{M}:\diag(a,b,c)\mapsto ab,\qquad e^{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}_{M}:\diag(a,b,c)\mapsto b.

In particular, since
M
consists of elements that square to 1,
we can ignore any minus signs.
This means that

s_{1}e^{\omega_{1}}_{M}=e^{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}_{M}=e^{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}_{M}

and similarly for all other combinations of elements of
W
and

\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
. This is exactly the same action as
was obtained purely from the weights.

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case:

We take
K=SU(3)
(here properly: the
antidiagonal subgroup of
G=SU(3)\times SU(3)
).
Then
M\leq K
is the Cartan subgroup, i.e. the group of diagonal
matrices.
The bottom weights induce the following one-dimensional representations of
M
:

e^{\omega_{1}}_{M}:\diag(a,b,c)\mapsto a,\qquad e^{\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}}_{M}:\diag(a,b,c)\mapsto b,\qquad e^{\omega_{4}}_{M}:\diag(a,b,c)\mapsto c.

The action of
W_{\Theta}
on
M
is just the action of permutation
matrices,
which is the same as is describe earlier.

For
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
:

We take
K=Sp(3)
and
M=SU(2)\times SU(2)\times SU(2)
.
If
V
is the 2-dimensional representation of
SU(2)
, then

\omega_{1},\omega_{3},\omega_{5}
correspond to the following

M
-types of
V(\gamma)
:

V\otimes\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C},\quad\mathbb{C}\otimes V\otimes\mathbb{C},\quad\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\otimes V.

Since the Weyl group of
G
acts by conjugation with permutation
matrices,
s_{2}s_{1}s_{3}s_{2}=(13)(24)
exchanges the first two
SU(2)

blocks and fixes the third and
s_{4}s_{3}s_{5}s_{4}=(35)(46)
exchanges
the last two blocks and fixes the first.
We conclude that
W_{\Sigma}
acts on the representations and hence
also on the bottom elements exactly like
S_{3}
acts on

\left\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\right\}
.
This is also the action we described earlier.

∎

Lemma 7.28
.

Let
\gamma
be an integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-type.
Then
V(\gamma)
is one-dimensional (i.e. trivial, or
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is of Hermitian type
and
\gamma
is a character), or

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
is any of the following:

(i)

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is of type
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
(
n>1
) and

\gamma
corresponds to the representation of highest weight

s\varpi_{n-1}
(in the notation of
[
37
]
) for

s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(ii)

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is of type
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
(
n>1
)and

\gamma
corresponds to the representation of highest weight
s\varpi_{n}

for
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(iii)

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is of type
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}

(n>2)
and

\gamma
corresponds to the representation of highest weight
s\varpi_{1}

for
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B

∎

Lemma 7.29
.

Assume
\gamma
is an integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-type.
Then Conjecture
7.25
holds for

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.28
,
\gamma
is either one-dimensional,
or
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
is one of the three cases considered as the first two examples
in Lemma
7.27
.

So it remains to check the case where
\gamma
is one-dimensional.
By
[
30
, §2.4]
, nontrivial characters exist for
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
of
type
\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p},\mathsf{BI}_{n,2},\mathsf{CI},\mathsf{DI}_{n,2},\mathsf{DIII},\mathsf{EIII},\mathsf{EVII}
.
In addition, we have to consider
\mathsf{AI}_{1}
and
\mathsf{AIV}_{n}

(which are considered to be
\mathsf{AIII}_{1,1}
and
\mathsf{AIII}_{n,1}
in loc.cit.).

In each case, the characters that exist form a 1-dimensional lattice
generated by
\chi
.
In case
\Sigma
is reduced and
\gamma=\ell\chi
, we know from

[
30
, Lemma 2.7]
that
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)=\left\{b\right\}
for

b=\frac{\absolutevalue{\ell}}{2}\sum_{\Sigma^{+}_{\mathrm{l}}}\alpha

where
\Sigma^{+}_{\mathrm{l}}
consists of the positive longest
roots of
\Sigma
.

We now show that
W_{\bullet}b+2L
is
W_{\Theta}
-invariant.
Note that since
b
lies in the
\mathbb{Q}
-span of
\Sigma
,
W_{\Theta}
’s
action on it factors through the quotient group
W_{\Sigma}
, so
that’s what we look at.

Recall that
W_{\Sigma}
is the Weyl group of
\Sigma
,
so it suffices to show
wb-b\in 2L
for the generators
w=r_{i}
associated with simple
roots
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
.
In case
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
not a long root (recall
that we assumed
\Sigma
to be reduced), we have

r_{i}\Sigma_{\mathrm{l}}^{+}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{l}}^{+}
and hence

r_{i}b=b
.
Otherwise, we have
wb-b=\alpha_{i}
.
Since
\Sigma
is of type
\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C}
, or

\mathsf{A}_{1}
, we then have
\alpha_{i}\in 2P
, hence

wb-b\in 2L
.
The claim follows.

The cases not covered by the assumption of reducedness are

\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}
(
2p<n+1
),
\mathsf{AIV}_{n}
,
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1}
,
and
\mathsf{EIII}
.
In these cases, we find in
[
37
, Table 2]
that the
bottom elements are given by
\absolutevalue{l}\omega_{i}
or

\absolutevalue{l}\omega_{\tau(i)}
where
I_{\bullet}\cup\left\{i,\tau(i)\right\}
is a subdiagram of type
\mathsf{AIV}
, depending
on the sign of
l
(and the choice of fundamental character
\chi
).
Concretely:

\displaystyle\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}

\displaystyle:\qquad\absolutevalue{\ell}\omega_{p},\absolutevalue{\ell}\omega_{n+1-p}

\displaystyle\mathsf{AIV}_{n}

\displaystyle:\qquad\absolutevalue{l}\omega_{1},\absolutevalue{\ell}\omega_{n}

\displaystyle\mathsf{DIII}_{n=2p+1}

\displaystyle:\qquad\absolutevalue{l}\omega_{2p},\absolutevalue{l}\omega_{2p+1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{EIII}

\displaystyle:\qquad\absolutevalue{l}\omega_{1},\absolutevalue{l}\omega_{6}

For
\mathsf{AIII},\mathsf{AIV}
, note that
W_{\Theta}
is generated
by
W_{\bullet},s_{1}s_{n},\dots,s_{p-1}s_{n+2-p},s_{p}\cdots s_{n+1-p}\cdots s_{p}
.
We have
s_{i}s_{n+1-i}b=b
for
i=1,\dots,p-1
as well
as
wb=b
for
w\in W_{\bullet}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle s_{p}\cdots s_{n+1-p}\cdots s_{p}\omega_{p}

\displaystyle=\omega_{p-1}-\omega_{n+1-p}+\omega_{n+2-p}=\omega_{p}-2\tilde{\alpha}_{p}

\displaystyle s_{p}\cdots s_{n+1-p}\cdots s_{p}\omega_{n+1-p}

\displaystyle=\omega_{p-1}-\omega_{p}+\omega_{n+2-p}=\omega_{n+1-p}-2\tilde{\alpha}_{p}.

In any case,
b
and
wb
differ by elements contained in
2L
.

For
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1}
, the group
W_{\Theta}
is generated by

W_{\bullet},s_{2i}s_{2i-1}s_{2i+1}s_{2i},s_{2p}s_{2p-1}s_{2p+1}s_{2p-1}s_{2p}

(
i=1,\dots,p-1
).

W_{\bullet}
fixes
\omega_{2p},\omega_{2p+1}
as do

s_{2i}s_{2i-1}s_{2i+1}s_{2i}
for
i\leq p-1
.
Lastly,

\displaystyle s_{2p}s_{2p-1}s_{2p+1}s_{2p-1}s_{2p}\omega_{2p}

\displaystyle=\omega_{2p-2}-\omega_{2p+1}=\omega_{2p}-2(-\omega_{2p-2}+\omega_{2p-1})^{\sim}\in\omega_{2p}+2L

\displaystyle s_{2p}s_{2p-1}s_{2p+1}s_{2p-1}s_{2p}\omega_{2p+1}

\displaystyle=\omega_{2p-2}-\omega_{2p}=\omega_{2p+1}-2(-\omega_{2p-2}+\omega_{2p-1})^{\sim}\in\omega_{2p+1}+2L.

For
\mathsf{EIII}
, the elements
r_{1}=s_{1}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}s_{6}s_{1}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}s_{1}s_{3}s_{4}s_{1}s_{3}s_{1}

(i.e. the longest element for the
\mathsf{A}_{5}
subdiagram generated by

\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}
) and

r_{2}=s_{2}s_{4}s_{3}s_{5}s_{4}s_{2}
both commute with
w_{\bullet}
and with
\tau
.
Consequently, they commute with
\Theta
.
Furthermore they implement the simple reflections of
\Sigma
, so
that
W_{\Theta}
is generated by
W_{\bullet}
and these elements.
Note that
W_{\bullet}
fixes
\omega_{1},\omega_{6}
, similarly for
r_{2}
.
It remains to see how
r_{1}
acts.
Note that we can rewrite
r_{1}=s_{1}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}s_{6}\langle s_{i}\mid i<6\rangle
,
so that

r_{1}\omega_{6}=s_{1}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}s_{6}\omega_{6}=\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}.

Applying
\tau
we obtain
r_{1}\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}-\omega_{6}
.
In both cases, we have

r_{1}b-b=\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}-\omega_{6}=-2\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\in 2L.

We thus obtain the conclusion that
W_{\bullet}b+2L
is
W_{\Theta}
-invariant for
all cases with nontrivial characters.

In case the character is trivial, we have
b=0
, so the statement follows by
the fact that
W_{\Theta}
acts by linear maps.
∎

Lemma 7.30
.

Conjecture
7.26
holds for all six examples considered here
as well as for the case where
\gamma
is an integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-type (and
where the ZSF case has been discussed in Section
5
).

Proof.

Note that for our six examples and the case where
\gamma
is
one-dimensional (even if it is a small
\mathbf{B}
-type), the premise of

W_{\Sigma}
acting transitively on
\mathfrak{B}(\gamma)
holds.

If
\gamma
is 1-dimensional, the claim follows from
[
30
, Theorem 6.1]
and the fact that the stabiliser of the
(single) bottom element is all of
W_{\Sigma}
.

For the remaining integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-types, the stabiliser of
the single bottom element is
W_{\Sigma}
, and by Theorem
7.12

the
-\rho
-shifted restrictions of matrix-spherical functions
are
W_{\Sigma}
-Intermediate Macdonald polynomials (=symmetric Macdonald polynomials).

For
\mathsf{DII}
, the stabiliser of
\omega_{n}
is trivial,
and we obtained
\{1\}
-intemediate Macdonald polynomials
(=non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials) as
-\rho
-shifted restrictions of MSF in Theorem
7.17
.

For
\mathsf{AI}_{3}
, the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, and

\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, the stabiliser of
\omega_{1}
is the
W_{J}

generated by
s_{2}
, and we obtained
W_{J}
-Intermediate Macdonald
polynomials as
-\rho
-shifted restrictions of MSF in Theorem
7.24
.
∎

Lemma 7.31
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
be a commutative triple of rank 1 such that
the Weyl action from Conjecture
7.25
is transitive,
then one of the following cases holds:

(i)

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is of type
\mathsf{A}_{1}
-group and

\gamma
corresponds to the highest weight
\varpi

(i.e. the standard representation)

(ii)

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is of type
\mathsf{AII}_{3}
and
\gamma

corresponds to the highest weight
\varpi_{1}

(i.e. the standard representation).

(iii)

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B})
is of type
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
and

\gamma
corresponds to the highest weight
\varpi_{n-1}

(i.e. the spin representation).

(iv)

(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
is a small
\mathbf{B}
-type.

Proof.

For the eight possible rank-1 (families of) Satake diagrams

\mathsf{A}_{1}
-group,
\mathsf{AI_{1}}
,
\mathsf{AII}_{3}
,
\mathsf{AIV}_{n}
,
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
,
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
,
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
,
\mathsf{FII}
, we note
that modulo
W_{\bullet}
, the group
W_{\Theta}
is generated by

s_{1}s_{2},s_{1},s_{2}s_{1}s_{3}s_{2},s_{1}\cdots s_{n}\cdots s_{1},s_{1}\cdots s_{n}\cdots s_{1},-1,(-1,-1,1,\dots,1),-1,

respectively.
For the Satake diagrams
\mathsf{AI}_{1},\mathsf{AIV}_{n},\mathsf{BII}_{n},\mathsf{CII}_{n,1},\mathsf{FII}
, we then see that modulo
W_{\bullet}
and

2L
, all weights are stabilised by
W_{\Theta}
,
so in particular only the small
\mathbf{B}
-types
\mathsf{AI}_{1},\mathsf{AIV}_{n}

(characters) and
\mathsf{BII}_{n},\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
have a transitive

W_{\Theta}
-action.

For the three remaining Satake diagrams, the nontrivial element of

W_{\Theta}/W_{\bullet}
acts non-trivially on some weights.
In particular, for the
\mathsf{A}_{1}
group case, the weights
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
are exchanged, for
\mathsf{AII}_{3}
, the weights
\omega_{1},\omega_{3}
are exchanged, and for
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
, the weights

\omega_{n-1},\omega_{n}
are exchanged.
These orbits are exactly the bottoms of the wells corresponding to the
first three claimed cases.
∎

Proposition 7.32
.

Conjectures
7.25
and
7.26
hold in rank 1.

Note that the examples here have focused exclusively on the case where

\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
and
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
.
As a straightforward generalisation of the integrable small
\mathbf{B}
-types
we consider earlier, i.e. cases for which
\#\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)=1
,
the authors of
[
35
]
put forward an example
of two different
\mathbf{B}
-representations
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}

satisfying
\#\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=1
.
Just like for our small
\mathbf{B}
-type examples, the resulting
matrix-spherical functions are symmetric Macdonald polynomials for an
affine root system of type
(\mathsf{C}_{p}^{\vee},\mathsf{C}_{p})
.
From anecdotal evidence, examples of such asymmetric integrable
small
\mathbf{B}
-types are abundant.

8.
Outlook

We conclude by discussing further directions of research.

Firstly, as we require a pairing of Hopf algebras, the
current formalism only works for finite-type
quantum-symmetric pairs.
Nevertheless, the formalism from
[
18
]
describes
quantum-symmetric pairs also if the root system is of infinite type.
The work
[
19
]
suggests that in order to study MSF
in such a setting, completions of various algebras have to be considered.

As a further possible generalisation, we point out the work of
Andrea Appel, Vidas Regelskis, and Bart Vlaar
(
[
38
]
,
[
39
]
,
[
3
, §6]
)
that generalises the notion of a (quantum) symmetric pair.
It should be noted, however, that their theory describes pairs of Lie algebras (and quantisations thereof)
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
for which
\mathfrak{k}
is not reductive.
Therefore, in particular the representation theory and
the branching rules of such pairs have not been much studied.

But even in the world of finite-type quantum-symmetric pairs
there is still ample work to be done.
Most notable, as a foundation of all techniques presented in this paper,
the zonal spherical functions for quantum-symmetric pairs of
Satake-type
\mathsf{BI}_{n,2},\mathsf{CI}_{n},\mathsf{DI}_{n,2},\mathsf{DIII}_{2p},\mathsf{EVII}
with non-zero non-standard parameter,
and of
\mathsf{DIII}_{2p+1},\mathsf{EIII},\mathsf{FII}
are
still not known in full generality.

Next, while we have exhausted all the
“homogeneous”

integral small
\mathbf{B}
-types (i.e.
\gamma
such that

\#\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma,\gamma)=1
),
as is demonstrated in
[
35
]
, there are also a lot of

“heterogeneous”
integral small
\mathbf{B}
-types (i.e.

\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
with
\#\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=1
).
This means that there is a wealth even of symmetric Macdonald
polynomials still to find.

And lastly, the Conjectures
7.25
and

7.26
remain to be proven.
This includes or would ideally be complemented by a classification
of quantum commutative triples
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)

(or
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
and
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
)
with a transitive action of
W_{\Sigma}
on
\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma)
(or
\mathfrak{B}^{+}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
).

Appendix A
Proofs for Section
7

A.1.
Lemma
7.4

We begin with a few computation rules for
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
v\in L(s\omega_{n})_{s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}}
.

(i)

For
1<i\leq n
we have

E_{n}\cdots E_{i}F_{i}\cdots F_{n}v=[s]_{q}v.

(ii)

For
1\leq i<n
we have

F_{1}\cdots F_{i}E_{i}\cdots E_{1}v=[r]_{q^{2}}v.

(iii)

For
1<i\leq n
we have

E_{n}\cdots E_{i}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{i-1}\cdots E_{1}v=[s-2]_{q}[r]_{q^{2}}v+F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{n}\cdots E_{1}v.

(iv)

We have

\commutator{E_n\cdots E_1}{F_1\cdots F_n}v=[s-2r]_{q}v.

Proof.

(i)

We proceed by reverse induction on
i
.
For
n=i
, we obtain

E_{n}F_{n}v=[K_{h_{n}}]_{q}v+F_{n}E_{n}v=[s]_{q}v

since
s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}+\alpha_{n}
is not contained in the
support of
L(s\omega_{n})
.

Assume the statement holds true for
i+1
(for
i>1
), then we have

\displaystyle E_{n}\cdots E_{i+1}E_{i}F_{i}F_{i+1}\cdots F_{n}v

\displaystyle=E_{n}\cdots E_{i+1}[K_{i}]_{q^{2}}F_{i+1}\cdots F_{n}v

\displaystyle\quad+E_{n}\cdots E_{i+1}F_{i}\cdots F_{n}E_{i}v.

Note that
s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}+\alpha_{i}
does not lie in the
support of
L(s\omega_{n})
, so that the last term is 0. Moreover,

\langle h_{i},s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}-\alpha_{n}-\cdots-\alpha_{i+1}\rangle=1,

consequently, we have

=E_{n}\cdots E_{i+1}F_{i+1}\cdots F_{n}v=[s]_{q}v,

as was claimed.

(ii)

We proceed by induction on
i
.
For
i=1
, we have

F_{1}E_{1}v=-[K_{1}]_{q^{2}}v+E_{1}F_{1}v=[r]_{q^{2}}v.

Assume the claim holds for
i-1
for
i<r
, then

\displaystyle F_{1}\cdots F_{i}E_{i}\cdots E_{1}v

\displaystyle=-F_{1}\cdots F_{i-1}[K_{i}]_{q^{2}}E_{i-1}\cdots E_{1}v

\displaystyle\quad+F_{1}\cdots F_{i-1}E_{i-1}\cdots E_{1}E_{i}v.

Since
\alpha_{i}+s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}
does not lie in the support
of
L(s\omega_{n})
, the last term is 0. Furthermore, we have

\langle h_{i},s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}+\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i-1}\rangle=-1,

so that we have

F_{1}\cdots F_{i}E_{i}\cdots E_{1}v=F_{1}\cdots F_{i-1}E_{i-1}\cdots E_{1}v=[r]_{q^{2}}v,

as claimed.

(iii)

We proceed by reverse induction on
i
.
For
i=n
, we have

\displaystyle E_{n}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{n-1}\cdots E_{1}v

\displaystyle=F_{1}\cdots F_{n-1}[K_{n}]_{q}E_{n-1}\cdots E_{1}v+F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{n}\cdots E_{1}v

\displaystyle=[s-2]_{q}F_{1}\cdots F_{n-1}E_{n-1}\cdots E_{1}v+F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{n}\cdots E_{1}v

\displaystyle=[s-2]_{q}[r]_{q^{2}}v+F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{n}\cdots E_{1}v

by (ii).

Assume the claim is true for
i+1
(
i>1
), then we have

\displaystyle E_{n}\cdots E_{i}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{i-1}\cdots E_{1}v=

\displaystyle E_{n}\cdots E_{i+1}F_{1}\cdots F_{i-1}[K_{i}]_{q^{2}}F_{i+1}\cdots F_{n}E_{i-1}\cdots E_{1}v

\displaystyle+E_{n}\cdots E_{i+1}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{i}\cdots E_{1}v.

We have

\langle h_{i},s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}+\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i-1}-\alpha_{n}-\cdots-\alpha_{i+1}\rangle=0,

so the first term vanishes. We then apply the induction hypothesis and obtain

E_{n}\cdots E_{i}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{i-1}\cdots E_{1}v=[s-2]_{q}[r]_{q^{2}}v+F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{n}\cdots E_{1}v.

(iv)

We have

\displaystyle E_{n}\cdots E_{1}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}v

\displaystyle=E_{n}\cdots E_{2}[K_{1}]_{q^{2}}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}v+E_{n}\cdots E_{2}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{1}v

\displaystyle=-[r-1]_{q^{2}}E_{n}\cdots E_{2}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}v+E_{n}\cdots E_{2}F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{1}v.

By (i) and (iii) this equals

-[r-1]_{q^{2}}[s]_{q}v+[s-2]_{q}[r]_{q^{2}}v+F_{1}\cdots F_{n}E_{n}\cdots E_{1}v.

We therefore conclude that

\commutator{E_n\cdots E_1}{F_1\cdots F_n}v=\quantity([r]_{q^2}[s-2]_q - [r-1]_{q^2}[s]_q)v=[s-2r]_{q}v.\qed

Proof of
7.4
for
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
.

Proposition
A.1
(iv) can be rephrased
as
\commutator{E_J}{F_{\overline{J}}}
acting on
V^{\prime}_{r}
as
[s-2r]_{q}
.
Note that
K_{J}
acts on
V^{\prime}_{r}
as

q^{\langle 2h_{1}+\cdots+2h_{n-1}+h_{n},s\omega_{n}-r\omega_{1}\rangle}=q^{s-2r},

so that
\commutator{E_J}{F_{\overline{J}}}=[K_{J}]_{q}
on
V^{\prime}
.

Moreover,
E_{J},F_{\overline{J}}
have weight
\pm(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})=\pm\omega_{1}
, so
\ad(K_{J})(E_{J})=q^{2}E_{J}
and
\ad(K_{J})(F_{\overline{J}})=q^{-2}F_{\overline{J}}
.
∎

We continue with similar computation rules for the case of
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
.

Proposition A.2
.

Write
K=(i_{1},\dots,i_{2n-2})=(1,\dots,n,\dots,2)
.
Let
v\in V^{\prime}_{r}=L(s\omega_{1})_{s\omega_{1}-r\omega_{2}}
.

(i)

For
2\leq k<2n-2
we have

E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k}}F_{i_{k}}\cdots F_{i_{1}}v=[s-r]_{q}.

In particular,

E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{j}F_{j}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v=[s-r]_{q}v

for
j>2
.

(ii)

For
2<k\leq 2n-2
we have

F_{i_{2n-2}}\cdots F_{i_{k}}E_{i_{k}}\cdots E_{i_{2n-2}}v=[r]_{q}v.

In particular,

F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{j}E_{j}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v=[r]_{q}v

for
j>2
.

(iii)

For
2\leq k<2n-2
we have

E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k}}F_{\overline{J}}E_{i_{k+1}}\cdots E_{i_{2n-2}}v=[r]_{q}[s-1-r]_{q}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v.

(iv)

We have
\commutator{E_J}{F_{\overline{J}}}v=[s-2r]_{q}v
.

Proof.

(i)

We proceed by induction in
k
. Write

A
for the term on the left-hand side.
For
k=2
, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{1}E_{2}F_{2}F_{1}v=E_{1}[K_{2}]_{q}F_{1}v+F_{2}E_{1}F_{1}E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-[r-1]_{q}E_{1}F_{1}v+F_{2}[K_{1}]_{q}E_{2}v+F_{2}F_{1}E_{1}E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-[r-1]_{q}[s]_{q}v+[s-1]_{q}[r]_{q}v+F_{2}F_{1}E_{1}E_{2}v.

Note that
E_{1}E_{2}v=0
as
s\omega_{1}-r\omega_{2}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
is not contained in the support of
L(s\omega_{1})
.
Moreover,
[s-1]_{q}[r]_{q}-[s]_{q}[r-1]_{q}=[s-r]_{q}
.

Assume that
k>2
and that the claim holds for
k-1
.

•

If
k\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{k}F_{k}\cdots F_{1}v=E_{1}\cdots E_{k-1}[K_{k}]_{q_{k}}F_{k-1}\cdots F_{1}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{k-1}F_{k-1}\cdots F_{1}v=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k-1}}F_{i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{i_{1}}v

\displaystyle=[s-r]_{q}v

as
E_{k}v=0
and by the induction hypothesis.

•

If
k>n
, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2n-k}F_{2n-k}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2n+1-k}\Big([K_{2n-k}]_{q}F_{2n+1-k}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2n-k}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{2n+1-k}[K_{2n-k}]_{q}F_{2n-1-k}\cdots F_{1}v\Big).

Note that
F_{2n+1-k}
commutes with
F_{1},\dots,F_{2n-1-k}
, so that
in the second expression we can permute
F_{2n+1-k}
to the right.
Since
F_{2n+1-k}v=0
(as
2n+1-k\neq 1
), the second term vanishes:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2n+1-k}F_{2n+1-k}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v

\displaystyle=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k-1}}F_{i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{i_{1}}v=[s-r]_{q}v

by the induction hypothesis.

(ii)

We proceed by reverse induction in
k
. Again, use

A
to denote the term on the left-hand side of the claim.
For
k=2n-2
,
we obtain

A=F_{2}E_{2}v=-[K_{2}]_{q}v=[r]_{q}v.

Now assume that
k<2n-2
and that the claim holds for
k+1
.

•

If
k\geq n
, we obtain

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=F_{2}\cdots F_{2n-k}E_{2n-k}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-F_{2}\cdots F_{2n-1-k}[K_{2n-k}]_{q_{2n-k}}E_{2n-1-k}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=F_{2}\cdots F_{2n-1-k}E_{2n-1-k}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=F_{i_{2n-2}}\cdots F_{i_{k+1}}E_{i_{k+1}}\cdots E_{i_{2n-2}}v

\displaystyle=[r]_{q}v

since
F_{2n-k}v=0
and by the induction hypothesis.

•

If
k<n
, we obtain

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{k}E_{k}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{k+1}\Big([K_{k}]_{q}E_{k+1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\qquad+E_{k}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{k+1}[K_{k}]_{q}E_{k-1}\cdots E_{2}v).

Note that
E_{k+1}
commutes with
E_{k-1},\dots,E_{2}
, so that
we may permute it to the right in the second expression.
Since furthermore
E_{k+1}v=0
(
k>1
), we conclude that the second
expression vanishes.
Consequently, the above equals

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{k+1}E_{k+1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=F_{i_{2n-2}}\cdots F_{i_{k+1}}E_{i_{k+1}}\cdots E_{i_{2n-2}}v=[r]_{q}v

by the induction hypothesis.

(iii)

We proceed by induction on
k
.
Write
A
for the term on the left-hand side of the claim.
For
k=2
, we obtain

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{1}E_{2}F_{\overline{J}}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\Big([K_{2}]_{q}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}[K_{2}]_{q}F_{1}\Big)E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+E_{1}F_{\overline{J}}E_{2}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-[r-1]_{q}E_{1}\Big(F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}\Big)F_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{2}[K_{1}]_{q}E_{2}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v

\displaystyle=-[r-1]_{q}E_{1}\Big(F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3})F_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+[s-2]_{q}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{2}E_{2}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v.

Note that
F_{2}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v=E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v=0
,
so that

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=-[r-1]_{q}\Big(F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3})[K_{1}]_{q}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad-[s-2]_{q}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}[K_{2}]_{q}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v

\displaystyle=\quantity([r]_q[s-2]_q-[r-1]_q[s-1]_q)F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v

\displaystyle=[r]_{q}[s-1-r]_{q}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v.

Next, assume that
k>2
and that the claim holds for
k-1
.

•

If
k<n
, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k}}F_{\overline{J}}E_{i_{k+1}}\cdots E_{i_{2n-2}}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{J}F_{\overline{J}}E_{k+1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{k-1}\Big(F_{2}\cdots F_{k-1}[K_{k}]_{q}F_{k+1}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{k+1}[K_{k}]_{q}F_{k-1}\cdots F_{1}\Big)E_{k+1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+E_{1}\cdots E_{k-1}F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{k-1}F_{\overline{J}}E_{k}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v,

which equals
[r]_{q}[s-1-r]_{q}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v
by the induction
hypothesis.

•

If
k=n
, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}F_{\overline{J}}E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n-1}F_{2}\cdots F_{n-1}[K_{n}]_{q^{2}}F_{n-1}\cdots F_{1}E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+E_{1}\cdots E_{n-1}F_{\overline{J}}E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n-1}F_{\overline{J}}E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v,

which equals
[r]_{q}[s-1-r]_{q}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v
by the induction
hypothesis.

•

If
k>n
, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2n-k}F_{\overline{J}}E_{2n-1-k}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2n+1-k}\Big(F_{2}\cdots F_{2n-1-k}[K_{2n-k}]_{q}F_{2n+1-k}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{2n+1-k}[K_{2n-k}]_{q}F_{2n-1-k}\cdots F_{1}\Big)E_{2n-1-k}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad+E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2n+1-k}F_{\overline{J}}E_{2n-k}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2n+1-k}F_{\overline{J}}E_{2n-k}\cdots E_{2}v,

which equals
[r]_{q}[s-1-r]_{q}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v
by the induction
hypothesis.

(iv)

We have

\displaystyle E_{J}F_{\overline{J}}v

\displaystyle=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3}\Big([K_{2}]_{q}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}[K_{2}]_{q}F_{1}v\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+E_{1}\dots E_{n}\cdots E_{3}F_{\overline{J}}E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-[r-1]_{q}E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v+E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3}F_{\overline{J}}E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-[r-1][s-r]_{q}v+[r]_{q}[s-r-1]_{q}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v

\displaystyle=[s-2r]_{q}v+F_{\overline{J}}E_{J}v.

by (i) and (iii).

∎

Proof of Lemma
7.4
for
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
.

Similar to the
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
case, using Proposition
A.2
.
∎

A.2.
Lemma
7.6

We begin with
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
.

Lemma
7.6
for
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
.

We have

B_{\overline{J}}=F_{\overline{J}}+c\ad(E_{2}\cdots E_{n}^{2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})K_{1}^{-1}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}.

This and the weights of the two terms shows that

B_{\overline{J}}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta
can be expanded
as claimed, with
b_{r}=0
and some yet-undetermined constant

c_{r}
.

Let now
v\in V^{\prime}_{r}
,
then
F_{2}\cdots F_{n}v
has weight
s\omega_{n}-(r-1)\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}=\quantity(\frac{s}{2}-r,\frac{s}{2}-1,\frac{s}{2},\dots)
.
This shows that applying
K_{1}^{-1}
to it yields
q^{2r-2}
.
Furthermore,
E_{i}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}v\neq 0
implies
i=1
or 2.
Moreover,

V^{\prime}_{r-1}\cap\im(E_{i})
is empty unless
i=n
.
Therefore, we can neglect any term that doesn’t have
E_{n}

on the left:
This shows that

\displaystyle c_{r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta

\displaystyle=cq^{2r-2}\ad(E_{2}\cdots E_{n}^{2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})F_{2}\cdots F_{n}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r-2}K_{2}\cdots K_{n-1}\ad(E_{n}^{2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})K_{n-1}E_{n-1}\cdots K_{2}E_{2}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r-2}\ad(K_{2})(\cdots\ad(K_{n-1})(\ad(E_{n}^{2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1}))E_{n-1}\cdots)E_{2}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta.

Note that every
\ad(K_{i})
has inside it an expression
of weight
\omega_{1}+\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
.
Consequently, the
\ad(K_{i})
adds a factor of
q^{-2}
,
hence

\displaystyle c_{r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n}\ad(E_{n}^{2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2}F_{2}\cdots F_{n}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n}\ad(E_{n}^{2}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})F_{n}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

(as in the proof of Proposition
A.1
(i)).
We continue:

\displaystyle c_{r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n}\Big(E_{n}^{2}\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad-(K_{n}E_{n}+E_{n}K_{n})\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})K_{n}^{-1}E_{n}\Big)F_{n}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n}\Big(E_{n}^{2}\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad-(1+q^{-2})E_{n}\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})E_{n}\Big)F_{n}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta.

Since
\im(F_{n})\cap V^{\prime}=0
, we can ignore any terms with
F_{n}

on the left.
Moreover,
E_{i}V^{\prime}=0
unless
i=1
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle c_{r}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n}\Big((E_{n}[K_{n}]_{q}+[K_{n}]_{q}E_{n})\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad-(1+q^{-2})E_{n}\ad(E_{n-1}\cdots E_{2})(E_{1})[K_{n}]_{q}\Big)F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n}\Big(([s-2]_{q}+[s]_{q})E_{J}

\displaystyle\qquad-(1+q^{-2})[s]_{q}E_{J}\Big)F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n-s}[2]_{q}E_{J}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n-s}[2]_{q}[r]_{q}[s+1-r]_{q}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta.

We now conclude that

c_{r}=(-1)^{n}cq^{2r+2-2n-s}[2]_{q}[r]_{q}[s+1-r]_{q}=-\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{1-2n}}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}[2]_{q}\quantity(1-q^{2r})\quantity(1-q^{2r-2-2s})

as claimed.
∎

Now for
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
.

Lemma
7.6
for
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
.

Let
v\in V^{\prime}_{r}
, we compute
B_{\overline{J}}v
.
We begin with
B_{2}F_{1}v
.
We have

\displaystyle B_{2}F_{1}v

\displaystyle=F_{2}F_{1}v+c\ad(E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3})(E_{2})K_{2}^{-1}F_{1}v

\displaystyle=F_{2}F_{1}v+cq^{r-1}\ad(E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3})(E_{2})F_{1}v.

Next, note that
E_{i}F_{1}v=F_{1}E_{i}v
if
i\neq 1
, which is zero unless
i=2
.
Consequently,
E_{i}F_{1}v\neq 0
implies that
i=1
or
i=2
.
Therefore, all terms of
\ad(\cdots)(E_{2})
that don’t have an
E_{1}
or
E_{2}
at the end vanish.
This shows that

\displaystyle B_{2}F_{1}v

\displaystyle=F_{2}F_{1}v+cq^{r-1}E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}F_{1}v-cq^{r-2}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{1}F_{1}v

\displaystyle=F_{2}F_{1}v+cq^{r-1}E_{1}F_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v-cq^{r-2}[s]_{q}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v.

Applying
F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}
, we obtain

\displaystyle F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}B_{2}F_{1}v

\displaystyle=F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v

\displaystyle\quad+cq^{r-1}E_{1}F_{1}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle\quad-cq^{r-2}[s]_{q}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v.

By the proof of Proposition
A.2
(ii), this equals

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle=F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v+cq^{r-1}E_{1}F_{1}E_{2}v-cq^{r-2}[s]_{q}E_{2}v

\displaystyle=F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v-cq^{r-s-1}E_{2}v

Applying
F_{2}
to this, we obtain

F_{2}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}B_{2}F_{1}v=F_{\overline{J}}v-cq^{r-s-1}[r]_{q}v.

We conclude that

B_{\overline{J}}v=F_{\overline{J}}v-cq^{r-s-1}[r]_{q}v+cA

where
A=\ad(E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3})(E_{2})K_{2}^{-1}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}B_{2}F_{1}v
.
Note that

K_{2}^{-1}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}B_{2}F_{1}v=q^{r-1}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v-cq^{2r-s-3}E_{2}v.

Next, we show that
\ad(E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3})(E_{2})
contains the term
-q^{4-2n}E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3}
.
To achieve this, we take the first term of
\ad(E_{1})
and the last of every subsequent
\ad
, yielding

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=-E_{1}K_{3}\cdots K_{n}\cdots K_{3}E_{2}K_{3}^{-1}E_{3}\cdots K_{n}^{-1}E_{n}\cdots K_{3}^{-1}E_{3}

\displaystyle=-E_{1}\ad(K_{3})(\cdots\ad(K_{n})(\cdots\ad(K_{3})(E_{2})E_{3}\cdots)E_{n}\cdots)E_{3}.

Considering the innermost brackets, the term inside
\ad(K_{i})
for
i<n
has weight
\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{i-1}
, so its pairing with

\epsilon_{i}h_{i}
is
-1
.
This shows that

B=-q^{3-n}E_{1}\ad(K_{3})(\cdots\ad(K_{n})(E_{2}\cdots E_{n-1})E_{n}\cdots)E_{3}.

Next,
E_{2}\cdots E_{n-1}
has weight
\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{n-1}
, whose pairing
with
\epsilon_{n}h_{n}=2h_{n}
is
-2
, so that

B=-q^{1-n}E_{1}\ad(K_{3})(\cdots\ad(K_{n-1})(E_{2}\cdots E_{n})E_{n-1}\cdots)E_{3}.

Now, the term inside every
\ad(K_{i})
has weight

\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{i}+2\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}

whose pairing with
\epsilon_{i}h_{i}=h_{i}
is
-1
, so that

B=-q^{4-2n}E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3}.

Having established this, we note that the two terms of
F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{3}B_{2}F_{1}v

(of weights
s\omega_{1}-(r+1)\omega_{2}+\alpha_{2}
and
s\omega_{1}-r\omega_{2}+\alpha_{2}
, respectively)
give rise to two terms in
A
of weights
s\omega_{1}-r\omega_{2}
and
s\omega_{1}-(r-1)\omega_{2}
.
For both weights, the only
E_{i}
whose image has a non-zero intersection with that weight
space is
E_{1}
.
Similarly, the image of
E_{1}E_{i}
intersects non-trivially with these two weight spaces iff
i=2

(or
i=1
).
Otherwise,
E_{1}E_{i}=E_{i}E_{1}
, but
E_{i}
’s image’s intersection with both weight spaces is trivial.
This shows that of the terms of
\ad(E_{1}E_{3}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3})(E_{2})
, only
B
contributes non-trivially
to
A
.
In particular,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=q^{r-1}BF_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v-cq^{2r-s-3}BE_{2}v

\displaystyle=-q^{r+3-2n}E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{3}F_{3}\cdots F_{n}\cdots F_{1}v+cq^{2r-s+1-2n}E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\cdots E_{2}v

\displaystyle=-q^{r+3-2n}[s-r]_{q}v+cq^{2r-s+1-2n}E_{J}v.

In conclusion,

B_{\overline{J}}v=F_{\overline{J}}v-cq^{r-s-1}[r]_{q}v-cq^{r+3-2n}[s-r]_{q}v+c^{2}q^{2r-s+1-2n}E_{J}v,

which implies

B_{\overline{J}}F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta=F_{\overline{J}}^{r+1}\eta-c\quantity(q^{r-s-1}[r]_q+q^{r+3-2n} [s-r]_q)F_{\overline{J}}^{r}\eta+c^{2}q^{2r-s+1-2n}[r]_{q}[s+1-r]_{q}F_{\overline{J}}^{r-1}\eta,

which yields the claimed expressions for
b_{r},c_{r}
.
∎

A.3.
\mathsf{DII}
Example

We start by showing that a
k[2L]^{W_{\Sigma}}
-linear map
that is diagonal with respect to our basis, is in fact also
triangular with respect to the Macdonald ordering.

Proof of Lemma
7.15
.

We begin by expanding monomials in terms of basis elements:

e^{(m+1)\omega_{1}}=fv_{1}+gv_{2},\qquad e^{-m\omega_{1}}=hv_{1}+jv_{2}

where
f
contains
m_{\mu}
for
\mu<(m+1)\omega_{1}
in the
dominance ordering, and

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=q^{1-n}m_{m\omega_{1}}+\lot,

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=m_{m\omega_{1}}+\lot

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle=-q^{1-n}m_{(m-1)\omega_{1}}+\lot.

Then

\displaystyle C(e^{(m+1)\omega_{1}})

\displaystyle\propto q^{n-1}fv_{1}+q^{1-n}gv_{2}=q^{n-1}e^{(m+1)\omega_{1}}+\lot

\displaystyle C(e^{-m\omega_{1}})

\displaystyle\propto q^{n-1}hv_{1}+q^{1-n}jv_{2}=q^{n-1}e^{-m\omega_{1}}+(q^{n-1}-q^{1-n})e^{m\omega_{1}}+\lot

(
\propto
denotes proportionality).
Since
-m\omega_{1}>m\omega_{1}
, we conclude that
C
is triangular.
∎

Now we show that the
t^{-1}
is monotonic.

Proof of Lemma
7.16
.

Let
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}(\gamma)
with
\mu>\mu^{\prime}
.
We write
\mu=m\omega_{1}+b
and
\mu^{\prime}=m^{\prime}\omega_{1}+b^{\prime}
.

In case
b=b^{\prime}
, we have
m-m^{\prime}\in 2\mathbb{N}
and

t^{-1}(\mu)_{+}-t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})_{+}=(m-m^{\prime})\omega_{1}
,
which is a positive linear combination of roots.

In case
b=\omega_{n-1},b^{\prime}=\omega_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle Q^{+}\ni\mu-\mu^{\prime}=(m-m^{\prime})\omega_{1}+\omega_{n-1}-\omega_{n}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n-1}+\frac{m-m^{\prime}-1}{2}\quantity(
2\alpha_1+\cdots+ 2\alpha_{n-2}+\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_n).

This shows that
m-m^{\prime}\in 1+2\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
We then have

t^{-1}(\mu)=-m\omega_{1},\qquad t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})=(m^{\prime}+1)\omega_{1}.

This shows that
t^{-1}(\mu)_{+}\geq t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})_{+}
.
Together with the fact that
t^{-1}(\mu)
is antidominant
(hence the larger element in its Weyl orbit),
this implies that
t^{-1}(\mu)>t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})
.

In case
b=\omega_{n},b^{\prime}=\omega_{n-1}
, we again conclude that
m-m^{\prime}\in 1+2\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
This time, however, we have

t^{-1}(\mu)=(m+1)\omega_{1},\qquad t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})=-m^{\prime}\omega_{1},

so in particular
t^{-1}(\mu)_{+}>t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})_{+}
and hence
t^{-1}(\mu)>t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})
.
∎

A.4.
\mathsf{A}_{2}
Examples

We start by showing that
t^{-1}
is monotonic.

Proof of Lemma
7.21
.

Let
\mu\in 2L_{+,J}
, we first show that there exists
\lambda\in 2L_{+}

such that either
\mu=\varpi_{1}+\lambda
, or
\mu=s_{1}(\varpi_{2}+\lambda)
,
or
\mu=s_{1}s_{2}\lambda
.
Let
\mu=a\varpi_{1}+b\varpi_{2}
.
That
\mu\in 2L_{+,J}
means that
b\geq 0
.
In case
a>0
, we have
a\geq 1
, so that
\mu\in\varpi_{1}+2L_{+}
.
In case
a\leq 0
and
a+b>0
, we have

s_{1}\mu=-a\varpi_{1}+(a+b)\varpi_{2}.

Since
(a+b)\geq 1
, we have
s_{1}\mu\in\varpi_{2}+2L_{+}
,
so that
\mu\in s_{1}(\varpi_{2}+2L_{+})
.

In case
a,a+b\leq 0
, we have

s_{2}s_{1}\mu=b\varpi_{1}-(a+b)\varpi_{2}\in 2L_{+},

so that
\mu\in s_{1}s_{2}(2L_{+})
.

Since only one of these three cases can occur, we conclude that

2L_{+,J}=\quantity(\varpi_1 + 2L_+)\sqcup s_{1}(\varpi_{2}+2L_{+})\sqcup s_{2}s_{1}(2L_{+}).

This shows that
t
is well-defined.
Since moreover it is a bijection on each subset and the images are
disjoint, it is a bijection.

Let
\mu^{\prime}=b^{\prime}+\lambda^{\prime}<\mu=b+\lambda
in
X^{+}
.
Write
\mu-\mu^{\prime}=\alpha\in Q^{+}
.

First note that
Q^{+}\cap 2L=2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}
(and in
particular
Q\cap 2L=2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma
) in all cases.
Depending on
b,b^{\prime}
we can therefore see in which equivalence class modulo

Q\cap 2L
the element
\alpha
is contained.
We obtain the following:

(i)

For
\mathsf{AI}_{2}
: If
b=b^{\prime}
, we have
\alpha\in 2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma
.
If
\left\{b,b^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{b_{1},b_{2}\right\}
, we have
\alpha\in 2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma
.
If
\left\{b,b^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{b_{1},b_{3}\right\}
, we have
\alpha\in\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma
.
If
\left\{b,b^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{b_{2},b_{3}\right\}
, we have
\alpha\in-\varpi_{1}+2\varpi_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma
.

(ii)

For the
\mathsf{A}_{2}
group case, we obtain the following table:

\downarrow b^{\prime},b\rightarrow

b_{1}

b_{2}

b_{3}

b_{1}

2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{3}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

b_{2}

\alpha_{1}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{4}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

b_{3}

\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma
.

(iii)

For
\mathsf{AII}_{5}
, we obtain the following:

\downarrow b^{\prime},b\rightarrow

b_{1}

b_{2}

b_{3}

b_{1}

2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{5}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

b_{2}

\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

b_{3}

\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{4}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}+2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

2\mathbb{Z}\Sigma
.

In particular, since
\alpha\in Q^{+}
, we can add
+
to all
2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)
’s appearing in these formulae.
Moreover, from
\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}=b^{\prime}-b+\alpha
and the aforementioned expressions for
\alpha
, we can now conclude
the following information on
\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}
:

\downarrow b^{\prime},b\rightarrow

b_{1}

b_{2}

b_{3}

b_{1}

2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

\varpi_{1}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

b_{2}

\varpi_{1}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

\varpi_{2}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

b_{3}

\varpi_{2}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

-\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}

2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}
.

Moreover, we obtain the following expressions for
t^{-1}(\mu)_{+}-t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})_{+}
:

\downarrow b^{\prime},b\rightarrow

b_{1}

b_{2}

b_{3}

b_{1}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}_{\in 2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}-\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}}_{\in 2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}-\varpi_{1}}_{\in 2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

b_{2}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}}_{\in 2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}_{\in 2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}-\varpi_{2}}_{\in 2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

b_{3}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}+\varpi_{1}}_{\in\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}+2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}+\varpi_{2}}_{\in-\varpi_{1}+2\varpi_{2}+(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}

\underbrace{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}_{\in 2(\mathbb{Z}\Sigma)^{+}}
.

For the three strictly lower-diagonal cases, we conclude that
t^{-1}(\mu)_{+}>t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})_{+}
, and that therefore
t^{-1}(\mu)>t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})
.
For the other cases, we have the non-strict inequality,
but
t^{-1}(\mu)
lies in a less dominant Weyl chamber than
t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})
, whence

t^{-1}(\mu)>t^{-1}(\mu^{\prime})
.
∎

Then we show that a diagonal map (on the basis elements) is
also triangular with respect to the Macdonald order.

Proof of Lemma
7.22
.

We define

\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}:=\sum_{w\in W_{J}}e^{w\lambda},\qquad\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}:=\sum_{w\in W}e^{w\lambda}

Then
\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}=nm_{J,\lambda}
with
n=\#W_{J,\lambda}
and

\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}=nm_{\lambda}
for
n=\#W_{\lambda}
.
Consequently,
C
is triangular with respect to
(m_{J,\lambda})_{\lambda\in L_{+,J}}
if and
only if it is triangular with respect to
(\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda})_{\lambda\in L_{+,J}}
.

Let
\lambda\in 2L_{+}
, then

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}=\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda}+\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\tau^{-2}}\big(\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+\varpi_{2}}+\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}}+\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda+\varpi_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}}+\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda+\varpi_{2}}+\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}\quantity(\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+\varpi_1}
+ \widetilde{m}_{J,s_1\lambda+ \varpi_1} + \widetilde{m}_{J,s_1s_2\lambda+ \varpi_1}).

If
\lambda
is generic, then
\lambda-\varpi_{1}
and
\lambda-\varpi_{2}
are dominant, so we can rewrite the
J
-dominant weights
occurring in
\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{1}}
as

\lambda+\varpi_{2},\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2},s_{1}(\lambda+\varpi_{2}),s_{1}(\lambda-\varpi_{1}),s_{1}s_{2}(\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}),s_{1}s_{2}(\lambda-\varpi_{1})

and those in
\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}
as
\lambda+\varpi_{1},s_{1}(\lambda-\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}),s_{1}s_{2}(\lambda-\varpi_{2})
. Consequently,
we can write

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\tau^{-2}}\quantity(
\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+ \varpi_2} + \widetilde{m}_{J,s_1(\lambda+\varpi_2)})+\sum_{\mu\in L_{+,J},\mu_{+}<\lambda+\varpi_{2}}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\mu}

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+\varpi_{1}}+\sum_{\mu\in L_{+,J},\mu_{+}<\lambda+\varpi_{1}}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\mu}.

In case
W_{\lambda}=\langle s_{1}\rangle
, the
J
-dominant weights contained in

\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{1}}
are

\lambda+\varpi_{2},\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2},s_{1}s_{2}(\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}).

and those in
\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}
are
\lambda+\varpi_{1},s_{1}s_{2}(\lambda-\varpi_{2})
.
Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\tau^{-2}}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+\varpi_{2}}+\sum_{\mu\in L_{+,J},\mu_{+}<\lambda+\varpi_{2}}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\mu}

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}

\displaystyle=2\tau^{2}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+\varpi_{1}}+\sum_{\mu\in L_{+,J},\mu_{+}<\lambda+\varpi_{1}}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\mu}.

If
W_{\lambda}=\langle s_{2}\rangle
, the
J
-dominant weights contained in

\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{1}}
are

\lambda+\varpi_{2},s_{1}(\lambda+\varpi_{2}),s_{1}(\lambda-\varpi_{1})

and those in
\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}
are

\lambda+\varpi_{1},s_{1}(\lambda-\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2})
.
Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\tau^{-2}}\quantity(\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+ \varpi_2}
+ \widetilde{m}_{J,s_1(\lambda+\varpi_2)})+\sum_{\mu\in L_{+,J},\mu_{+}<\lambda+\varpi_{2}}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\mu}

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda+\varpi_{1}}+\sum_{\mu\in L_{+,J},\mu_{+}<\lambda+\varpi_{1}}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\mu}.

And lastly,

\widetilde{m}_{0}e_{s_{1}}=\frac{6}{1+\tau^{-2}}\widetilde{m}_{J,\varpi_{2}},\qquad\widetilde{m}_{0}e_{s_{1}s_{2}}=3\tau^{2}\widetilde{m}_{J,\varpi_{1}}.

We now proceed by induction. Assume that
C
is triangular restricted to the
span of

\left\{m_{J,\mu}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mu\in L_{+,J},\mu_{+}<\lambda\right\}

is triangular. We
now show that the same is also true on
W\lambda
. If
\lambda
is regular,
we have

\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}=\begin{cases}\tau^{-2}\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{1}}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}&\langle h_{1},\lambda\rangle>2\\
\frac{1}{2}\tau^{-2}\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{1}}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}&\langle h_{1},\lambda\rangle=2\end{cases}+\sum_{\nu\in L_{+,J},\nu_{+}<\lambda}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\nu},

so that

C\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}=a_{s_{2}s_{1}}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\sum_{\nu\in L_{+,J},\nu_{+}<\lambda}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\nu}=a_{s_{2}s_{1}}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\lot

(here we use
\lot
to explicitly denote elements with weights in lower orbits).
Furthermore,

\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda}=\begin{cases}(1+\tau^{-2})\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{2}}e_{s_{1}}&\langle h_{2},\lambda\rangle>2\\
\frac{1+\tau^{-2}}{2}\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{2}}e_{s_{1}}&\langle h_{2},\lambda\rangle=2\end{cases}-\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\sum_{\nu\in L_{+,J},\nu_{+}<\lambda}k\widetilde{m}_{J,\nu},

so that

\displaystyle C\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda}

\displaystyle=a_{s_{1}}\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda}+(a_{s_{1}}-a_{s_{2}s_{1}})\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\lot

\displaystyle C\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}

\displaystyle=C(\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{1}-m_{J,s_{1}\lambda}-m_{J,\lambda})=a_{1}\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,\quad+(a_{1}-a_{s_{1}})(\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda}+\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda})+\lot

In case
W_{\lambda}=\langle s_{1}\rangle
, we
have

\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}=\begin{cases}\frac{1+\tau^{-2}}{2}\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{2}}e_{s_{1}}&\langle h_{2},\lambda\rangle>2\\
\frac{1+\tau^{-2}}{6}\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{2}}e_{s_{1}}&\langle h_{2},\lambda\rangle=2\end{cases}+\lot

so that

\displaystyle C\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}

\displaystyle=a_{s_{1}}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\lot

\displaystyle C\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}

\displaystyle=C(\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{1}-2\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda})=a_{1}\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}+2(a_{1}-a_{s_{1}})\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\lot

In case
W_{\lambda}=\langle s_{2}\rangle
, we have

\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}=\begin{cases}\tau^{-2}\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{1}}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}&\langle h_{1},\lambda\rangle>2\\
\frac{1}{3\tau^{2}}\widetilde{m}_{\lambda-\varpi_{1}}e_{s_{2}s_{1}}&\langle h_{1},\lambda\rangle=2\end{cases}+\lot

so that

\displaystyle C\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}

\displaystyle=a_{s_{2}s_{1}}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\lot

\displaystyle C\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}C(\widetilde{m}_{\lambda}e_{1}-\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda})=a_{1}\widetilde{m}_{J,s_{1}\lambda}+\frac{a_{1}-a_{s_{2}s_{1}}}{2}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}+\lot

And lastly, if
\lambda=0
we have
C\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}=2Ce_{1}=a_{1}\widetilde{m}_{J,\lambda}
.

We conclude that
C
is also triangular on the span of
\left\{m_{J,\nu}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\nu\in L_{+,J},\nu_{+}\leq\lambda\right\}
.
∎

Now we show that our correspondence of functions is also
monotonic.

Proof of Lemma
7.23
.

We proceed on a case-by-case basis.
We have
\Gamma^{-1}(m_{\lambda}\otimes e_{b_{3}})=m_{\lambda}
.
If
\lambda
is generic, this equals
m_{J,\lambda}+m_{J,s_{1}\lambda}+m_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}
,
whose leading term is
m_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}
.
If
W_{\lambda}
is generated by
s_{1}
we have

m_{\lambda}=m_{J,\lambda}+m_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}
whose leading term is
again
m_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}
.
If
W_{\lambda}
is generated by
s_{2}
, we
have
m_{\lambda}=m_{J,\lambda}+m_{J,s_{1}\lambda}
, whose leading term
is
m_{J,s_{1}\lambda}=m_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}
.
Lastly, if
W_{\lambda}
is generated by
s_{1},s_{2}
, we have
m_{\lambda}=m_{J,\lambda}=m_{J,s_{1}s_{2}\lambda}
.
In all cases, the leading exponent is

s_{1}s_{2}\lambda=t^{-1}(b_{3}+\lambda)
.

Next, we have

\Gamma^{-1}(m_{\lambda}\otimes e_{b_{2}})=m_{\lambda}e_{2}=\frac{m_{\lambda}e^{\varpi_{2}}+m_{\lambda}e^{\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}}}{1+\tau^{-2}}.

If
\lambda
is generic, then both
\lambda-\varpi_{1},\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}
are dominant.
The above expression then
contains monomials contained in
W(\lambda+\varpi_{1}),W(\lambda+\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}),W(\lambda-\varpi_{1})
.
Since
\lambda+\varpi_{2}
dominates the others, only the
terms contained in the
\lambda+\varpi_{2}
orbit are relevant for the
leading term.
In particular, these terms are

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\tau^{-2}}\quantity(e^{\lambda+\varpi_2} + e^{s_1\lambda+ \varpi_2}
+ e^{s_2\lambda+ \varpi_1-\varpi_2} + e^{s_2s_1\lambda+ \varpi_1-\varpi_2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\tau^{-2}}\quantity(m_{J,\lambda+\varpi_2} +
m_{J,s_1(\lambda+ \varpi_2)}).

If
W_{\lambda}
is generated by
s_{1}
, we have

m_{\lambda}e_{2}=\frac{1}{1+\tau^{-2}}\quantity(m_{J,\lambda+\varpi_2} + m_{J,\lambda+\varpi_1-\varpi_2}
+ m_{J,s_1s_2(\lambda+\varpi_1-\varpi_2)}).

If
W_{\lambda}
is
generated by
s_{2}
, we have

m_{\lambda}e_{2}=\frac{1}{1+\tau^{-2}}\quantity(m_{J,\lambda+\varpi_2} +
m_{J,s_1(\lambda+\varpi_2)}
+ m_{J,s_2s_1(\lambda-\varpi_1)}).

Lastly, for
\lambda=0
we have
m_{\lambda}e_{2}=e_{2}=\frac{m_{J,\varpi_{2}}}{1+\tau^{-2}}
.
Note that in every case, the leading exponent is
s_{1}(\lambda+\varpi_{2})=t^{-1}(b_{2}+\lambda)
.

Finally, note that

\Gamma^{-1}(m_{\lambda}\otimes e_{b_{1}})=m_{\lambda}e_{3}=\tau^{2}m_{\lambda}e^{\varpi_{1}}.

For
\lambda
generic, this equals

\tau^{2}\sum_{w\in W_{\Sigma}}e^{w\lambda+\varpi_{1}}=\tau^{2}\quantity(m_{J,\lambda+ \varpi_1} + m_{J,s_1(\lambda- \varpi_1+\varpi_2)}
+ m_{J,s_1s_2(\lambda-\varpi_2)})

(note that
\lambda-\varpi_{2},\lambda-\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}
are dominant as
well).
If
W_{\lambda}
is generated by
s_{1}
we have

m_{\lambda}e_{3}=\tau^{2}\quantity(e^{\lambda+\varpi_1} + e^{s_2\lambda+ \varpi_1}
+ e^{s_1s_2\lambda+ \varpi_1})=\tau^{2}\quantity(m_{J,\lambda+\varpi_1} + m_{J,s_1s_2(\lambda- \varpi_2)}).

If
W_{\lambda}
is generated by
s_{2}
, we have

m_{\lambda}e_{3}=\tau^{2}\quantity(e^{\lambda+\varpi_1} + e^{s_1\lambda+ \varpi_1} + e^{s_2s_1\lambda+ \varpi_1})=\tau^{2}\quantity(m_{J,\lambda+ \varpi_1} + m_{J,s_1(\lambda- \varpi_1+\varpi_2)}),

and if
\lambda=0
, we have
m_{\lambda}e_{3}=\tau^{2}m_{J,\varpi_{1}}
. In
all cases the leading exponent is
\lambda+\varpi_{1}=t^{-1}(b_{1}+\lambda)
.
This shows that
\Gamma^{-1}
is triangular.
∎

Appendix B
Classifying Integrable Small
\mathbf{B}
-Types

The small
K
-types of real Lie groups are classified in
[
36
]
,
and similar to what we do in Section
7.1
, their spherical functions
are identified with appropriate Heckman–Opdam hypergeometric functions in loc.cit.

We shall now translate their findings in the language of Satake diagrams and weights
as in
[
37
]
.
The following
K
-types are small:

For
\mathsf{AI}_{n}
:

If
n=1
:
l\varpi_{1}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
n>1
odd:

\varpi_{\frac{n+1}{2}-1},\varpi_{\frac{n+1}{2}}
.
If
n>1
even:
\varpi_{\frac{n}{2}}
.

For
\mathsf{AII}_{n}
:

None.

For
\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p}
:

l\varpi_{p}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For
\mathsf{AIV}_{n}
:

l\varpi_{1}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For
\mathsf{BI}_{n,p}
:

(i.e.
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{so}(p)\times\mathfrak{so}(2n+1-p)
).
If
p=2
:
l\varpi_{0}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For
p>2
even:
\varpi_{\frac{p}{2}-1},\varpi_{\frac{p}{2}}
.
For
p>2
odd:
\varpi_{\frac{p-1}{2}},\varpi_{\frac{2n-1-p}{2}}^{\prime},\varpi_{\frac{2n+1-p}{2}}^{\prime}
.

For
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
:

s\varpi_{n-1},s\varpi_{n}
for
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

For
\mathsf{CI}_{n}
:

l\varpi_{n+1}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For
\mathsf{CII}_{n,p}
:

If
p=1
and
n>2
:
s\varpi_{1}
for
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
If
p=1
and
n=2
:
s\varpi_{1},s\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
for
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

For
\mathsf{DI}_{n,p}
:

If
p=2
:
l\varpi_{0}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.
If
p<n
and
p
even:
\varpi_{\frac{p}{2}-1},\varpi_{\frac{p}{2}}
.
If
p<n
and
p
odd:
\varpi_{\frac{p-1}{2}}

If
p=n
and
n
even:
\varpi_{\frac{n}{2}-1},\varpi_{\frac{n}{2}},\varpi_{\frac{n}{2}-1}^{\prime},\varpi_{\frac{n}{2}}^{\prime}
.
If
p=n
and
n
odd:
\varpi_{\frac{n-1}{2}},\varpi_{\frac{n-1}{2}}^{\prime}
.

For
\mathsf{DII}_{n}
:

None.

For
\mathsf{DIII}_{n}
:

l\varpi_{n+1}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For
\mathsf{EI}
:

\varpi_{1}
.

For
\mathsf{EII}
:

(i.e.
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{su}(6)\times\mathfrak{su}(2)
)

\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
.

For
\mathsf{EIII}
:

(i.e.
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{so}(10)\times\mathbb{C}
)

l\varpi_{0}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For
\mathsf{EIV}
:

None.

For
\mathsf{EV}
:

\varpi_{1},\varpi_{7}
.

For
\mathsf{EVI}
:

(i.e.
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{so}(12)\times\mathfrak{su}(2)
)

\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
.

For
\mathsf{EVII}
:

(i.e.
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{e}_{6}\times\mathbb{C}
)

l\varpi_{0}
.

For
\mathsf{EVIII}
:

\varpi_{1}
.

For
\mathsf{EIX}
:

(i.e.
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{e}_{7}\times\mathfrak{su}(2)
)

\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
.

For
\mathsf{FI}
:

(i.e.
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{sp}(6)\times\mathfrak{su}(2)
)

\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
.

For
\mathsf{FII}
:

None.

For
\mathsf{G}
:

\varpi_{1},\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
.

Any occurrence of
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
occurs for a character of
\mathfrak{k}
for

\mathsf{AI}_{1},\mathsf{AIII}_{n,p},\mathsf{AIV}_{n},\mathsf{BI}_{n,2},\mathsf{CI}_{n},\mathsf{DI}_{n,2},\mathsf{DIII}_{n},\mathsf{EIII},\mathsf{EVII},

the Hermitian cases.
In these cases, the only small
K
-types are the characters.

There are four other families, namely
\mathsf{BII}_{n}
with
s\varpi_{n-1}
(
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
),

\mathsf{BII}_{n}
with
s\varpi_{n}
(
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
),

\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
with
s\varpi_{1}
(
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
),
and
\mathsf{CII}_{2,1}
with
s\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
(
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
).
Note that the Satake diagrams
\mathsf{CII}_{2,1}
and
\mathsf{BII}_{2}
are identical
(exchanging 1 and 2),
and under this isomorphism,
\varpi_{1},\varpi_{1}^{\prime}
correspond to
\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2}
, so we can
assume that
n>2
when dealing with
\mathsf{CII}_{n,1}
.

The remaining cases are the following
“sporadic/exceptional cases”
:
spin representations
\varpi_{n-1},\varpi_{n}
or
\varpi_{n}
when part of
\mathfrak{k}
is of type
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
or
\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
and

(43)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{EI},\varpi_{1}),(\mathsf{EII},\varpi_{1}^{\prime}),(\mathsf{EV},\varpi_{1}),(\mathsf{EV},\varpi_{7}),(\mathsf{EVI},\varpi_{1}^{\prime}),(\mathsf{EVIII},\varpi_{1}),(\mathsf{EIX},\varpi_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle(\mathsf{FI},\varpi_{1}^{\prime}),(\mathsf{G},\varpi_{1}),(\mathsf{G},\varpi_{1}^{\prime}).

We shall show that none of these exceptional cases is integrable.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k},\lambda)
correspond to any of the cases from (
43
).

Let
\mathfrak{t}\leq\mathfrak{k}
and

\mathfrak{h}\leq\mathfrak{g}
be Cartan subalgebras satisfying
\mathfrak{t}\leq\mathfrak{h}
.
I.e. we pick
\mathfrak{h}
to be maximally compact.
Then there exists no integral weight
\mu
such that
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\lambda
.

Proof.

Consulting
[
15
, Appendix C]
, we obtain a convention to realise the simple roots
of
\mathfrak{k}
inside
\mathfrak{h}
.

We now consider the cases.
For every case, we define
simple roots
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
that
generate the root system
\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{h})

and simple roots
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r}
that generate the
root system
\Sigma(\mathfrak{k}:\mathfrak{t})
.
Let
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n}
and
\tilde{\omega}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\omega}_{r}

be the corresponding fundamental weights.

Next, we assume that
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\omega_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}

restrict to a certain weight and show that this means that
\mu
cannot be
integral.

Case
\mathsf{AI}_{n}
for
n=2r-1>1
odd:

We define
\alpha_{i}:=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
i=1,\dots,n

and

\beta_{i}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{n+1-i})&i<r\\
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{r-1}+2\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{r+1})&i=r\end{cases}\qquad\beta_{i}^{\vee}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}+\alpha_{n+1-i}^{\vee}&i<r\\
\alpha_{r-1}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{r}^{\vee}+\alpha_{r+1}^{\vee}&i=r.\end{cases}

•:

In case
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{r-1}
, we have

a_{r-1}+a_{r+1}=1
and
a_{r-1}+a_{r+1}+2a_{r}=0
, which implies

a_{r}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

In case
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{r}
, we have

a_{r-1}+a_{r+1}=0
and
a_{r-1}+a_{r+1}+2a_{r}=1
, which implies

a_{r}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{AI}_{n}
for
n=2r>1
even:

With the same
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
as before and

\beta_{i}:=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{n+1-i})\quad(i\leq r),\qquad\beta_{i}^{\vee}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}+\alpha_{n+1-i}^{\vee}&i<r\\
2\alpha_{r}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{r+1}^{\vee}&i=r\end{cases}

Assume that
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{r}
, then

2a_{r}+2a_{r+1}=1
, which means that at least one of
a_{r},a_{r+1}
is not an integer.

Case
(\mathfrak{so}(2n+1),\mathfrak{so}(2p)\times\mathfrak{so}(2q+1))
for
1<p,q
:

Let
\alpha_{i}:=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
(
i<n
) and
\alpha_{n}:=e_{n}
.
Let

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i\neq p\\
\alpha_{p-1}+2\alpha_{p}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n}&i=p\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{i}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}&i\neq p\\
\alpha_{p-1}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{p}^{\vee}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n-1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{n}^{\vee}&i=p.\end{cases}

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{p-1}
, we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p-1,p
as well
as
a_{p-1}=1
and
a_{p-1}+2a_{p}=0
, hence
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{p}
, we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p
and

2a_{p}=1
, which again implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{n}
, we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p,n
as well as

a_{n}=1
and
2a_{p}+a_{n}=0
, which implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

In no case is it possible to choose
\mu
integral.
This statement covers all cases of type
\mathsf{BI}_{n,p}
for
p>2
.

Case
\mathsf{DI}_{n,2p}
with
2<2p
:

Let
\alpha_{i}:=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
(
i<n
) and
\alpha_{n}:=e_{n-1}+e_{n}
.
Let

\beta_{i}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i\neq p\\
\alpha_{p-1}+2\alpha_{p}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n-2}+\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}&i=p\end{cases}

Note that all roots are of the same length, so we can add
\vee
’s on all
\alpha
’s and

\beta
’s in the above statement and obtain a true statement.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{p-1}
then
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p-1,p
as well
as
a_{p-1}=1
and
a_{p-1}+2a_{p}=0
, which implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{p}
, then
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p
and

2a_{p}=1
, which implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{n-1}
, then
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p,n-1
as well as

a_{n-1}=1
and
2a_{p}+a_{n-1}=0
, which implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{n}
, then
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p,n
as well as

a_{n}=1
and
2a_{p}+a_{n}=0
, which implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{DI}_{n,2p+1}
with
1\leq p
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
as before and let

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i<p\text{ or }p<i\leq n-2\\
e_{p}=\alpha_{p}+\cdots+\alpha_{n-2}+\frac{\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}}{2}&i=p\\
e_{n-1}=\frac{\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}}{2}&i=n-1.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{i}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}&i<p\text{ or }p<i\leq n-2\\
e_{p}^{\vee}=2\alpha_{p}^{\vee}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n-2}^{\vee}+\alpha_{n-1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{n}^{\vee}&i=p\\
e_{n-1}^{\vee}=\alpha_{n-1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{n}^{\vee}&i=n-1.\end{cases}

Let
\mu\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be expanded as
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\omega_{i}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{p}
, we have

a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p,n-1,n
.
Moreover,

2a_{p}+a_{n}+a_{n-1}=1,\qquad a_{n}+a_{n-1}=0,

which implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{n-1}
, we have

a_{i}=0
for
i\neq p,n-1,n
.
Moreover,

2a_{p}+a_{n}+a_{n-1}=0,\qquad a_{n}+a_{n-1}=1,

which implies
a_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{EI}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{6}
as in
[
8
, Planche V]
.
Let

\beta_{i}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{4}+\frac{\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5}}{2}&i=1\\
\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{6}}{2}&i=2\\
\frac{\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5}}{2}&i=3\\
\alpha_{4}&i=4\end{cases}\qquad\beta_{i}^{\vee}=\begin{cases}2\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{4}^{\vee}+\alpha_{5}^{\vee}&i=1\\
\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{6}^{\vee}&i=2\\
\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{5}^{\vee}&i=3\\
\alpha_{4}^{\vee}&i=4.\end{cases}

In case
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}
, we obtain

a_{4}=a_{3}+a_{5}=a_{1}+a_{6}=0
and hence
2a_{2}=1
, which implies
a_{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{EII}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{6}
be as previously and let

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i\neq 2\\
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{6}+2(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5})+3\alpha_{4}=\omega_{2}&i=2\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{i}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}&i\neq 2\\
\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{6}^{\vee}+2(\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{5}^{\vee})+3\alpha_{4}^{\vee}&i=2.\end{cases}

In case
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{2}
, we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq 2
and

2a_{2}=1
, which implies
a_{2}\neq\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{EV}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{7}
be as in
[
8
, Planche VI]
and let

\beta_{i}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i\neq 2\\
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{6}+2(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5})+3\alpha_{4}&i=2.\end{cases}

Analogously to the last case, we obtain the the expressions for
\beta_{1}^{\vee},\dots,\beta_{7}^{\vee}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}
,
we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq 1,2
and
a_{1}+2a_{2}=0
,
which shows that
a_{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{2}
,
we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\neq 2
and
2a_{2}=1
,
which shows that
a_{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Assume that
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}
we have

a_{i}=0
for
i\neq 1,2
and
a_{1}=1
and
a_{1}+2a_{2}=0
, which shows that

a_{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{EVI}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{7}
be as before and let

\beta_{i}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i\neq 1\\
\omega_{1}\end{cases}\qquad\beta_{i}^{\vee}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}&i\neq 1\\
\alpha_{7}^{\vee}+2(\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+\alpha_{7}^{\vee})+3(\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{5}^{\vee})+4\alpha_{4}^{\vee}&i=1.\end{cases}

Assume that
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}
, then
2a_{1}=1
, which
implies
a_{1}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{EVIII}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{8}
be as in
[
8
, Planche VII]
and let

\beta_{i}
and
\beta_{i}^{\vee}
be defined as in the last item (except
i
can be 8).
As before, if
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}
, we have
2a_{1}=1
, which implies
that
a_{1}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{EIX}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{8}
be as before and let

\beta_{i}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i\neq 8\\
\omega_{8}&i=8,\end{cases}

with

\beta_{8}^{\vee}=2(\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{8}^{\vee})+3(\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+\alpha_{7}^{\vee})+4(\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{6}^{\vee})+5\alpha_{5}^{\vee}+6\alpha_{4}^{\vee}.

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{8}
, we have

2a_{8}=1
, which implies
a_{8}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{FI}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{4}
be as in
[
8
, Plance VIII]
and let

\beta_{i}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&i\neq 1\\
\omega_{1}&i=1\end{cases}\qquad\beta_{i}^{\vee}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}&i\neq 1\\
2\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+3\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{4}^{\vee}&i=1.\end{cases}

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}
, then
2a_{1}=1
, which implies

a_{1}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case
\mathsf{G}
:

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
be as in
[
8
, Planche IX]
and let

\beta_{i}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}&i=1\\
\omega_{2}&i=2\end{cases}\qquad\beta_{i}^{\vee}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}^{\vee}&i=1\\
3\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\frac{2}{3}\alpha_{2}^{\vee}&i=2.\end{cases}

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}
, then

a_{1}=1
and
3a_{1}+\frac{2}{3}a_{2}=0
, i.e.

a_{2}=\frac{9}{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•:

If
\mu|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\tilde{\omega}_{2}
, then

a_{1}=0
and
\frac{2}{3}a_{2}=1
, i.e.
a_{2}=\frac{3}{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
7.28
.

Let
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
be a commuative triple with
\gamma
specialisable, then

\cl(\gamma)
is a small
\cl(\mathbf{B})
-type in the sense that for the
classical branching rules of
(\cl(\mathbf{U}),\cl(\mathbf{B}))
we have

\#\mathfrak{B}(\cl(\gamma))=1
.
But then
\cl(\gamma)
is one of the small
K
-types listed earlier.
Since
\gamma
is integrable, so is
\cl(\gamma)
, so that by
Proposition
B.1
, the only possibilities we are left with
are
\gamma
one dimensional or
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{B},\gamma)
one of the three families from the claim.
∎
